# Optimizing LLM Inference with Key-Value Caching

*A survey of how the key-value (KV) cache shapes LLM serving latency, throughput and memory, from on-GPU paging to offload onto CPU memory and storage.*

**Author:** Dave Graham · **License:** MIT · **Last revised:** September 2026

> Citations are numbered in order of first appearance and link to the reference list at the end. The keyed source lives in [`src/whitepaper.md`](src/whitepaper.md); see the [README](README.md) for how to edit and re-render.

## Executive Summary

The key-value (KV) cache is the per-token attention state an LLM keeps so that it never recomputes what it has already read. It turns decoding into a memory-bandwidth problem and makes GPU memory, not compute, the binding constraint on how many long-context requests a GPU can serve. By September 2026 the cache has changed from a private, per-request GPU buffer into a shared, tiered and increasingly persistent data structure. It spans GPU memory (HBM), host DRAM, local NVMe and networked storage, and it is priced, benchmarked and secured as a first-class system resource. The main findings of this paper:

1. **Architecture and precision set the size.** Per-token KV bytes are $2\cdot L\cdot n_{kv}\cdot d_{head}\cdot s$: 128 KiB for Llama 3 8B and 320 KiB for Llama-3-70B in BF16 [[1](#ref-1)], so a 100,000-token prefix of the larger model occupies about 32.8 GB. Multi-head latent attention cut DeepSeek-V2's cache by 93.3% relative to its dense predecessor [[2](#ref-2)]. DeepSeek-V4's sequence-compressed hybrid attention needs about 10% of DeepSeek-V3.2's cache at 1M tokens [[3](#ref-3)]. Both are vendor-reported figures. FP8 KV caches are now routine in production engines; sub-byte schemes such as NVFP4 and 2-bit KIVI trade accuracy that must be validated per workload [[4](#ref-4), [5](#ref-5)].
2. **Decode is memory-bound on current GPUs.** On an H100 the compute/bandwidth balance point is about 295 FLOPs per byte, and KV-cache reads run one to two orders of magnitude below it at any batch size [[6](#ref-6), [7](#ref-7)]. Memory management therefore decides throughput: paging cut reserved-but-wasted KV memory from 60–80% to under 4% [[8](#ref-8)].
3. **Reuse across requests is now the largest lever.** Prefix caching removes prefill work for any prompt span a previous request has already computed [[9](#ref-9), [10](#ref-10)], and agentic workloads re-send most of each prompt. In a vendor-run eight-hour replay of captured agentic coding sessions, a shared NVMe tier raised the cache hit rate from 40% to 93% and served 2.4× the requests on the same B300 node [[11](#ref-11)]. Hosted APIs bill cache reads at roughly a tenth of the base input price [[12](#ref-12), [13](#ref-13)].
4. **Lower tiers pay off only with a direct data path.** For a 70B-class model, fetching a cached prefix over an RDMA or GPUDirect path beats recomputing it at almost any prefix length. Plain HTTP object storage is a knife-edge that depends on how efficiently the GPU would have recomputed. Energy points the same way: moving a KV byte over PCIe costs at least 400× less energy than recomputing it [[14](#ref-14)].
5. **Disaggregation and shared tiers are production architecture.** Prefill and decode run on separate GPU pools that exchange KV caches [[15](#ref-15), [16](#ref-16), [17](#ref-17)]. KV block managers tier caches from GPU to host, SSD and remote storage [[18](#ref-18), [19](#ref-19)], and NVIDIA has created a storage-processor category for the cache [[20](#ref-20)]. Most major storage vendors now publish KV-offload results, all vendor-reported under setups they chose.
6. **Measurement is catching up.** MLPerf Storage v3.0 added a dedicated KV cache benchmark [[21](#ref-21)]. The commonest measurement errors are cache contamination (a fixed-seed synthetic benchmark rerun against a server that already holds its prompts in cache, which can inflate TTFT gains by more than an order of magnitude), single-arrival-pattern tests that do not generalize, and closed-loop throughput figures that hide where latency breaks down [[22](#ref-22), [23](#ref-23)].
7. **Sharing creates risk.** Prefix-cache hits leak prompt content through timing [[24](#ref-24), [25](#ref-25)], and prompts can be partly reconstructed from KV tensors [[26](#ref-26)]. Per-tenant cache isolation, encryption and retention controls are required as soon as a cache is shared or persisted.
8. **The cache is also an editable state.** Research tools can edit cached keys and values for a single read with an exact finite-change response [[27](#ref-27)]. An independent replication confirms the mathematics and the recorded repair, but the repair reproduced on only two of ten checkpoints [[28](#ref-28)]. The systems implication is general: edited caches need copy-on-write branches and provenance.

## Introduction

Large Language Model (LLM) inference proceeds in two phases: a **prefill** phase, in which every prompt token is processed in a single parallel forward pass to populate the model's internal state, and a **decode** phase, in which output tokens are generated one at a time, each conditioned autoregressively on every token generated or supplied so far [[29](#ref-29), [30](#ref-30)]. This two-phase structure – more than model size alone – is what makes latency and throughput engineering for LLM serving qualitatively different from serving a single dense forward pass, and it is why two distinct latency metrics are used to describe it [[29](#ref-29), [31](#ref-31)]:

* **Time to First Token (TTFT):** the latency from request submission to the emission of the first output token, including any queuing delay [[31](#ref-31)]. TTFT is driven primarily by the cost of the prefill pass over the prompt (below), plus tokenization and network overhead [[31](#ref-31)].

* **Time Per Output Token (TPOT):** the average latency between successive output tokens during decode. NVIDIA's NIM benchmarking documentation treats TPOT as synonymous with **Inter-Token Latency (ITL)**, defined as end-to-end request latency minus TTFT, divided by (total output tokens − 1) [[31](#ref-31)]. MLPerf's inference rules use the same two-metric split – TTFT for the first token, TPOT for "the average interval between all the tokens generated" thereafter – with numeric targets per model and scenario: Llama-3.1-8B's "server" ("Conversational") scenario requires TTFT ≤ 2 s and TPOT ≤ 100 ms, tightened to TTFT ≤ 0.5 s and TPOT ≤ 30 ms for "interactive" ("Interactive") [[32](#ref-32), [33](#ref-33)]. Not every benchmarking tool agrees on whether the first token is included in the TPOT/ITL average, so reported numbers are not always bit-for-bit comparable across tools even when both are computed correctly [[31](#ref-31)].

End-to-end request latency is then approximately $\text{TTFT} + \text{TPOT}\times(N_{out}-1)$ for an $N_{out}$-token completion, following NIM's ITL-based accounting [[31](#ref-31)] – Databricks' own formulation instead uses the simpler $\text{TTFT}+\text{TPOT}\times N_{out}$, without subtracting the first token [[29](#ref-29)], though the difference is one TPOT term and is usually negligible for long completions – and system throughput is the aggregate output-token rate across all concurrent requests [[29](#ref-29)]. TTFT and TPOT trade against each other under a fixed compute budget – batching more requests raises aggregate throughput but lengthens the queuing and cross-request interference each individual request experiences – so a serving system cannot be judged on throughput alone. DistServe introduces **per-GPU goodput** to capture this: the maximum request rate a system can sustain *per GPU provisioned* while a target fraction of requests (e.g., 90%) meet *both* a TTFT and a TPOT service-level objective (SLO) at once – the per-GPU normalization is deliberate, since it is what lets goodput compare configurations that use different numbers of GPUs on a cost-per-query basis [[16](#ref-16)]. This SLO-attainment framing has moved from research into production tooling: NVIDIA's Dynamo serving stack, for instance, ships an autoscaler ("Planner") that sizes prefill and decode worker pools directly against configured TTFT/ITL targets rather than against raw queries-per-second [[34](#ref-34)].

A central enabler of low TPOT is the **Key-Value (KV) cache**: storing the attention key and value tensors computed for each past token so they can be reused rather than recomputed at every later generation step [[30](#ref-30)]. The KV cache is the reason decode-phase per-token cost does not grow with the *square* of context length the way naive re-computation would (below), and it is also the reason GPU memory, not GPU compute, becomes the binding constraint on how many requests can be served concurrently at long context (later sections). This whitepaper follows the cache down the memory hierarchy and out across the data center. It covers the mechanics of KV caching and its precise effect on TTFT and TPOT; on-GPU memory management (paging, kernels, quantization); three ways to make the cache smaller or cheaper – attention architectures that shrink it at the source, compression and eviction, and reuse across requests; offload to CPU memory and SSDs; disaggregated serving that moves the cache between machines; shared and persistent storage tiers for the cache; how these strategies evolved at scale and which workload trends now drive KV demand; how to measure and price cache effects; the security and integrity risks of shared caches; and, finally, the cache as an editable model state rather than only a performance structure. Appendix A describes a sizing calculator that reproduces the paper's KV-size arithmetic. Where a figure comes from a single vendor or paper, it is reported as such, together with the model, hardware, and baseline used to produce it – KV-cache-driven speedups are highly sensitive to sequence length, batch size, and hardware generation, and do not transfer uncritically across settings.

## KV Cache Mechanics and Impact on Latency & Throughput

Self-attention, as originally specified, has per-layer computational complexity $O(n^2\cdot d)$ for a sequence of length $n$ and model width $d$, because every one of the $n$ query vectors must be compared against every one of the $n$ key vectors [[35](#ref-35)]. In autoregressive decoding this becomes wasteful if handled naively: a plain, cache-free re-invocation of the standard forward pass at each step recomputes attention over the *entire* growing prefix rather than reusing anything already computed for earlier tokens [[35](#ref-35)]. The **KV cache** removes this redundancy – "significantly reduc[ing] computational complexity by allowing the model to reference past information efficiently without time-consuming recomputations" [[30](#ref-30)] – by storing each layer's key ($K$) and value ($V$) tensors the first time they are computed and reusing them at every later step; only the new token's query, key and value need to be computed, and only the new query needs to attend against the stored history.

**Precise per-step complexity.** With the cache in place, a transformer layer's cost at decode step $t$ splits into two terms with different scaling behavior, and conflating them is a common source of error:

* The query/key/value/output projections and the feed-forward (MLP) block operate on exactly one new token, so their cost is $\Theta(d_{model}^2)$ per layer – **independent of context length** $t$ [[6](#ref-6), [36](#ref-36)].
* The attention operation itself – the new query's dot product against all $t$ cached keys, then a weighted sum over the $t$ cached values – costs $\Theta(t\cdot d_{model})$ per layer, since $n_{heads}\cdot d_{head}=d_{model}$: this term **grows linearly with the number of tokens already generated or prefilled** [[6](#ref-6)].

By contrast, a naive re-invocation of the full forward pass over the whole prefix at every step (i.e., without any cache, calling the model on the entire sequence so far and reading off the last position) recomputes the complete $t\times t$ self-attention matrix, costing $\Theta(t^2\cdot d_{model})$ per layer, on top of redundantly recomputing the $\Theta(t\cdot d_{model}^2)$ projections for every position in the prefix rather than just the new one [[35](#ref-35), [30](#ref-30)]. So the familiar shorthand "caching drops per-step complexity from $O(n^2)$ to $O(n)$" is accurate as a statement about the *attention* term under this common form of cache-free decoding – it is not a statement about the projection/MLP term, whose $\Theta(d_{model}^2)$-per-token cost is unaffected by caching either way. What caching removes is the *redundant recomputation* of that term across steps (from $\Theta(t)$ back down to $\Theta(1)$ per step); it does not shrink the per-token cost itself.

**Time to First Token.** Prefill computes $K$, $V$ and $Q$ for all $n$ prompt tokens in one parallel pass. Its projection and feed-forward cost is $\Theta(n\cdot d_{model}^2)$ – linear in prompt length, since it is the same fixed per-token cost repeated $n$ times – but the self-attention operation over the full $n\times n$ score matrix costs $\Theta(n^2\cdot d_{model})$, quadratic in prompt length, exactly as in the original complexity analysis of self-attention [[35](#ref-35), [6](#ref-6)]. The two terms are of comparable magnitude when $n\approx d_{model}$: for prompts short relative to model width (roughly $n\lesssim d_{model}$, i.e., a few thousand tokens for a 4k–8k-wide model) the linear projection term dominates and TTFT scales close to linearly; once $n$ grows past that point, the quadratic attention term takes over and TTFT scales more steeply [[6](#ref-6)]. Critically, **FlashAttention does not change this FLOP count for the forward pass relevant to inference.** It is an IO-aware, exact-attention *kernel*: tiling and recomputation cut HBM reads/writes from $\Theta(Nd+N^2)$ to $\Theta(N^2d^2M^{-1})$ ($M$ = on-chip SRAM size), which is what makes it dramatically faster and linear- rather than quadratic-memory in practice; Theorem 1 gives the forward pass's cost as $O(N^2d)$ FLOPs, the same asymptotic class as standard attention [[37](#ref-37)]. (Its *total* FLOP count – forward plus backward – is in fact slightly higher than standard attention's, from backward-pass recomputation; that is a training-time effect, not relevant to the forward-only prefill/decode path here [[37](#ref-37)].) Its successor, FlashAttention-2 [[38](#ref-38)] (an ICLR 2024 poster), further improves GPU occupancy and warp-level work partitioning to close more of the gap to peak matmul throughput, again without changing the asymptotic FLOP count. So "TTFT grows roughly linearly with prompt length" is at best a short-context approximation: it understates cost at long context, and the attention FLOP term stays quadratic with or without FlashAttention-class kernels.

Two mechanisms *do* cut, rather than merely accelerate, redundant prefill work. First, prefix caching lets a new request skip recomputing a prompt segment it shares with an earlier one: PagedAttention was designed from the outset to share KV blocks "within and across requests" via copy-on-write [[39](#ref-39)], and this is now a separately documented production feature (Automatic Prefix Caching) – with the explicit caveat that it only ever shortens the prefill/TTFT portion of a request, never the decode/TPOT portion, and only when a genuine prefix match exists [[9](#ref-9)]. Second, chunked prefill – introduced as "chunked-prefills" by Sarathi [[40](#ref-40)] and refined into the "stall-free scheduling" of Sarathi-Serve [[41](#ref-41)] – splits a long prompt's prefill into near-equal chunks interleaved with other requests' decode steps ("piggybacking"), so decode tokens are never blocked behind a full, uninterrupted prefill. This does not reduce FLOPs, and it is not free: splitting a prefill into $N$ equal chunks means each later chunk's attention must re-load every earlier chunk's KV cache from HBM, raising total KV-cache HBM traffic for that one prefill from $\Theta(N)$ (non-chunked) to $\Theta(N^2)$ in the number of chunks. DistServe's own analysis of the technique – which it attributes to Sarathi/Sarathi-Serve rather than claiming as its own – concludes that it "trades TTFT for TPOT and cannot eliminate the interference" between prefill and decode, only alleviate it [[16](#ref-16)]. Chunked prefill is discussed further alongside prefill/decode disaggregation below [[15](#ref-15), [16](#ref-16)].

**Time Per Output Token and throughput.** Because the decode-step attention term costs $\Theta(t\cdot d_{model})$ while the projection/MLP term costs $\Theta(d_{model}^2)$ regardless of $t$, per-token latency in principle grows only slowly – linearly in context length, not quadratically – as generation proceeds. Whether that growth is visible in wall-clock time depends on which resource is the bottleneck, and for decoding on current data-center GPUs that resource is almost always **memory bandwidth**, not FLOPs. This follows from a simple arithmetic-intensity (FLOPs-per-byte) argument, in the tradition of Shazeer (2019) and Pope et al. (2022) [[42](#ref-42), [6](#ref-6)]. Decode at batch size $B$ has two memory-traffic components per step: model *weights*, loaded once per step regardless of $B$ but used across $B$ matrix–vector products (so this component's arithmetic intensity rises with $B$, and at large enough batch this part of the step can become compute-bound), and the *KV cache*, where each of the $B$ sequences carries its own cache, so bytes read scale with $B$ just as the attention FLOPs performed against them do – leaving the ratio roughly constant, on the order of a few FLOPs per byte transferred, independent of batch size [[6](#ref-6)]. On an NVIDIA H100 SXM (989.5 TFLOPS dense BF16 Tensor Core throughput; 3.35 TB/s HBM3 bandwidth) [[7](#ref-7)], the chip is compute-bound only above roughly $989.5\times10^{12}/3.35\times10^{12}\approx295$ FLOPs per byte. The same arithmetic on a newer NVIDIA B200 SXM (2.25 PFLOPS dense BF16 Tensor Core; 7.7 TB/s HBM3e) gives a nearly identical balance point, $\approx292$ FLOPs per byte [[43](#ref-43)]: despite roughly 2.3$\times$ higher absolute compute and bandwidth on B200, the compute-to-bandwidth *ratio* that determines whether decode is memory-bound is essentially unchanged across this hardware generation, so the argument below is not an artifact of H100-specific numbers. KV-cache reads sit one to two orders of magnitude below that threshold at any batch size, which is why growing the batch size raises aggregate throughput – by amortizing weight loads over more sequences – without proportionally shrinking the marginal cost of reading each sequence's own KV cache, and why, at large batch or long context, KV-cache bandwidth rather than weight bandwidth becomes the binding constraint on decode throughput [[6](#ref-6), [44](#ref-44)]. This is also the underlying motivation for multi-query and grouped-query attention (below): shrinking the KV cache directly raises decode arithmetic intensity, since less data must be re-read per token [[42](#ref-42), [45](#ref-45)].

**Memory footprint of the cache.** The cache must store, for every cached token, a key and a value vector per layer per *KV head* – not per query head, and the distinction matters. Under plain multi-head attention (MHA), the number of KV heads $n_{kv}$ equals the number of query heads, but multi-query attention (MQA) shares a single KV head across all query heads ($n_{kv}=1$) [[42](#ref-42)], and grouped-query attention (GQA) uses an intermediate number of KV heads, $1<n_{kv}<n_{heads}$, trading a small quality cost for most of MQA's bandwidth savings [[45](#ref-45)]. The correct per-token cache size is

$$\text{bytes/token} = 2\cdot L\cdot n_{kv}\cdot d_{head}\cdot s,$$

where $L$ is the number of layers, $d_{head}$ is the per-head dimension, the leading 2 accounts for storing both $K$ and $V$, and $s$ is the number of bytes per stored element (2 for fp16/bf16, 1 for fp8/int8 – see the next section) [[42](#ref-42), [6](#ref-6), [36](#ref-36)]. This reduces to the more familiar $2\cdot L\cdot d_{model}\cdot s$ only in the MHA special case, where $n_{kv}\cdot d_{head}=d_{model}$; substituting total hidden size $d_{model}$ for $n_{kv}\cdot d_{head}$ silently assumes MHA, overstating the cache size of any modern GQA/MQA model, and omitting the bytes-per-element factor $s$ silently assumes a fixed dtype, which breaks under KV quantization.

As a worked example, Llama 3 8B uses $L=32$ layers and $d_{model}=4096$ split across 32 query heads ($d_{head}=128$), but only $n_{kv}=8$ KV heads under GQA, stored in bf16 ($s=2$ bytes) [[1](#ref-1)]. Its per-token KV cache is $2\times32\times8\times128\times2=131{,}072$ bytes $=128$ KiB, so an 8,192-token sequence at batch size 1 occupies $128\text{ KiB}\times8{,}192\approx1\text{ GiB}$. Had the same architecture used full MHA ($n_{kv}=32$) instead of GQA-8, the identical context would cost four times as much – about 4 GiB – since GQA-8's KV-head count is $4\times$ smaller than its query-head count [[1](#ref-1), [45](#ref-45)]:

**Illustrative per-token KV cache size by attention variant (bf16, 8,192-token context, batch size 1)**

| Attention variant | Example | $L$ | $n_{kv}$ | $d_{head}$ | Bytes/token | @ 8K context |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| MHA ($n_{kv}=n_{heads}$) | Llama 3 8B, hypothetically with MHA-32 | 32 | 32 | 128 | 512 KiB | ≈4.0 GiB |
| GQA-8 (actual) | Llama 3 8B [[1](#ref-1)] | 32 | 8 | 128 | 128 KiB | ≈1.0 GiB |
| MQA ($n_{kv}=1$) | Illustrative, same layer/head-dim shape | 32 | 1 | 128 | 16 KiB | ≈128 MiB |

These figures are per sequence at batch size 1; the aggregate cache scales linearly with batch size and with the number of concurrent sequences, which is what actually strains GPU memory in production (discussed later). They also do not capture more aggressive current compression schemes: DeepSeek-V2's Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) projects $K$ and $V$ into a shared low-rank latent vector rather than storing per-head tensors, which DeepSeek reports – vendor-benchmarked, relative to their own dense 67B predecessor within the same model family – cuts KV cache size by 93.3% and raises maximum generation throughput by 5.76$\times$ [[2](#ref-2)]. That is a substantially larger reduction than GQA alone provides, at the cost of a more invasive architecture change that, unlike GQA, cannot be retrofitted onto an existing MHA checkpoint by simple mean-pooling of heads [[45](#ref-45), [2](#ref-2)].

In summary: KV caching removes the *redundant* recomputation that would otherwise make per-token cost grow quadratically with generation length, but it does not remove the inherent quadratic FLOP cost of a single full-context attention pass, does not by itself change whether a workload is compute- or memory-bound, and its memory footprint depends on the number of KV heads and the storage dtype, not on model width alone. Fitting the resulting cache into – and moving it efficiently through – the GPU memory hierarchy is the subject of the next section.

## On-GPU KV Cache: Memory Efficiency and GPU Utilization

Keeping the KV cache in GPU high-bandwidth memory (HBM) is the fastest configuration available, but "fast" needs a concrete number. HBM bandwidth on current NVIDIA data-center GPUs is not "hundreds of GB/s" — it is in the low single-digit terabytes per second, and it varies substantially by generation:

| GPU (SXM) | HBM generation | Capacity | Peak bandwidth |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| H100 | HBM3 | 80 GB | 3.35 TB/s |
| H200 | HBM3e | 141 GB | 4.8 TB/s |
| B200 | HBM3e | 180 GB | up to 8 TB/s |
| B300 / GB300 ("Blackwell Ultra") | HBM3e | up to 288 GB | up to 8 TB/s |

[[7](#ref-7), [46](#ref-46), [47](#ref-47)]. NVIDIA's own collateral is not fully consistent on the B300 figure: the standalone Blackwell Ultra datasheet lists 279 GB of HBM3E per GPU, while NVIDIA's HGX reference-architecture documentation and OEM datasheets list up to 288 GB. The 279 GB figure is the more internally consistent of the two: the same Blackwell Ultra datasheet separately states 20 TB of HBM3E across a 72-GPU GB300 NVL72 rack, and $279 \times 72 \approx 20.1$ TB matches that figure, whereas $288 \times 72 \approx 20.7$ TB would not round to "20 TB" [[48](#ref-48), [47](#ref-47)]. A similar small inconsistency affects B200's per-GPU bandwidth specifically: this table's "up to 8 TB/s" is drawn from NVIDIA's own HGX reference-architecture comparison table [[47](#ref-47)], while this whitepaper's worked decode arithmetic-intensity calculation (Section: KV Cache Mechanics and Impact on Latency & Throughput) instead uses 7.7 TB/s, a figure NVIDIA's own HGX B200 Product Carbon Footprint summary supports at the per-GPU level [[43](#ref-43)]. The two need not be strictly contradictory — "up to 8 TB/s" reads as a rounded ceiling in a comparison table rather than a per-GPU measured figure — but the whitepaper should not be read as having independently verified two different numbers for the same quantity: 7.7 TB/s is the better-grounded of the two, since it is derived from a specific documented per-GPU spec breakdown rather than a rounded comparison-table entry, and it is the figure this whitepaper's own arithmetic-intensity balance point (≈292 FLOPs/byte) relies on. Even the low end of the per-GPU bandwidth range, 3.35 TB/s, is roughly 26× the ~128 GB/s bidirectional peak NVIDIA's own H100 datasheet lists for a PCIe Gen5 x16 link [[7](#ref-7)] (PCI-SIG's own encoding-inclusive figures, used later in this whitepaper's Table 6, put Gen5 x16 lower still — ~63 GB/s per direction, ~126 GB/s bidirectional — and reserve ~128 GB/s for Gen6 x16 per direction) — on any of these readings the gap is what motivates keeping the cache on-GPU rather than reaching across PCIe. Achieved KV-cache offload throughput in practice runs well below that theoretical peak — 16–36 GB/s in the measurements discussed in a later section on CPU/NVMe offloading [[30](#ref-30)] — reflecting protocol overhead and largely unidirectional transfer patterns, but even that reduced figure leaves on-GPU HBM roughly two orders of magnitude faster. When the entire cache fits in GPU memory, each new token's attention reads prior keys/values at the full HBM rate [[30](#ref-30)], avoiding host transfers and their added latency; in a GPU-only setup the decode phase can sustain near-peak throughput until memory capacity, not compute, becomes the limit.

The KV cache's footprint follows directly from model architecture. For $L$ transformer layers, key/value heads of combined width $H_{kv} = n_{kv} \times d_{head}$ (equal to the full hidden size under multi-head attention, but far smaller under grouped-query or multi-query attention, which most current models use), $p$ bytes per stored value, batch size $B$, and sequence length $S$, the cache size in bytes is $2 \cdot L \cdot H_{kv} \cdot p \cdot B \cdot S$, the factor of 2 covering both keys and values. Two examples show the scale this reaches in practice. A 13B-parameter model with Llama-2-13B's published architecture ($L=40$, multi-head attention with 40 heads of dimension 128, so $H_{kv}=5{,}120$) [[49](#ref-49)], at batch size 32 and a 4,096-token context in FP16/BF16 ($p=2$ bytes), gives $2 \times 40 \times 5{,}120 \times 2 \times 32 \times 4{,}096 \approx 107$ GB of KV cache against roughly 26 GB of FP16 weights — a ratio near 4×, consistent with the commonly cited "~100 GB, ~4×" figure for this configuration [[30](#ref-30)]. A Llama-3-8B-class model with grouped-query attention ($L=32$, 8 KV heads of dimension 128, so $H_{kv}=1{,}024$ versus 4,096 for its 32 query heads — a 4× reduction from GQA alone) [[1](#ref-1)], at batch size 1 and a 1-million-token context, gives $2 \times 32 \times 1{,}024 \times 2 \times 1{,}000{,}000 \approx 131$ GB of KV cache by this formula (decimal GB; $\approx 122$ GiB) — close to but not identical to HeadInfer's own stated figure for the same model and context: 128 GB of KV cache, 15 GB of FP16 weights and 64 GB of activation memory, for a 207 GB baseline, before head-wise offloading reduces this to 1 GB of KV cache and 17 GB total [[50](#ref-50)]. Both figures far exceed any single GPU's HBM capacity in the table above, which is why naive GPU-only caching is infeasible at these context lengths or batch sizes without the techniques below.

Even short of hitting capacity limits, allocation inefficiency degrades utilization. vLLM's original analysis found that systems pre-allocating a contiguous block for each sequence's worst-case length wasted 60–80% of reserved KV memory to fragmentation and over-reservation [[8](#ref-8)]. PagedAttention addresses this by partitioning each sequence's KV cache into fixed-size blocks that need not be contiguous in memory, analogous to OS virtual-memory paging; the vLLM team reports this reduces waste to under 4%, and its block-table indirection additionally enables copy-on-write sharing of a common prompt prefix across parallel completions, cutting memory use in such cases by up to 55% and, in the vLLM team's own measurement, improving throughput by up to 2.2× [[8](#ref-8)]. The peer-reviewed SOSP evaluation reports the memory-saving figure independently — 37.6–55.2% on Alpaca and 44.3–66.3% on ShareGPT, depending on beam width — and a corresponding 1.3–2.3× throughput improvement for beam search over a strong Orca (Oracle) baseline; this is consistent with, but not identical to, the blog's 2.2× figure, which the paper does not itself restate [[39](#ref-39)].

KV cache quantization has moved from research technique to a stable, documented feature of production serving engines. FP8 KV cache is supported as a first-class option — not merely a research patch — in vLLM (`kv_cache_dtype="fp8_e4m3"`/`"fp8_e5m2"`, with per-tensor or calibrated per-attention-head scales) [[51](#ref-51)], in SGLang (`--kv-cache-dtype fp8_e4m3`/`fp8_e5m2`) [[52](#ref-52)], and in NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM, which can enable FP8 KV cache even for checkpoints not quantized with it [[53](#ref-53)]. On Blackwell GPUs (compute capability sm100/sm103), TensorRT-LLM additionally supports an NVFP4 KV cache — a 4-bit floating-point format with fine-grained block scaling, requiring offline calibration through NVIDIA's TensorRT Model Optimizer — which NVIDIA reports cuts KV cache memory by roughly 50% versus FP8 (approximately doubling addressable context or batch size at fixed memory) with under 1% accuracy loss on LiveCodeBench, MMLU-Pro, MBPP and Ruler-64K for the models the vendor tested, though values must still be dequantized to FP8 before the attention matmul itself, and Hopper GPUs do not support this path [[4](#ref-4), [53](#ref-53)] (see "Compressing and Evicting the Cache" below for the fuller FP8/NVFP4/KVQuant/QServe survey and its accuracy-vs-compression summary table). These are vendor-reported numbers specific to the cited models, hardware and calibration data, not a general accuracy guarantee, and coverage is uneven: sliding-window and other non-standard attention layers are flagged as more quantization-sensitive and can be selectively excluded in vLLM [[51](#ref-51)]. Independently of vendor formats, academic work has pushed further to 2 bits: KIVI applies per-channel quantization to keys and per-token quantization to values and reports "almost the same quality" (a qualitative claim, not a stated accuracy bound) on Llama, Falcon and Mistral models, with 2.6× lower peak memory (including model weights), up to 4× larger batch size, and 2.35–3.47× higher throughput on the workloads tested [[5](#ref-5)]; note that the frequently repeated "8×" figure for KIVI refers only to the KV tensor's own bit-width reduction (16-bit to 2-bit), not to these more modest end-to-end memory and throughput numbers. This paper does not establish that these results hold for much larger models, mixture-of-experts architectures, or multi-million-token contexts, where quantization error accumulates over far more attended tokens; production deployments should validate accuracy on their own task and context-length distribution before adopting 2-bit KV caches, the same caution NVIDIA gives for its own 4-bit format [[4](#ref-4)].

Neither paging nor quantization delivers its throughput benefit without attention kernels and a scheduler built to exploit them. FlashAttention computes exact attention via tiling and online softmax to minimize HBM reads and writes, making attention IO-bound by design rather than wasteful of bandwidth by default [[37](#ref-37)]. Because a decode step has query length 1, however, plain FlashAttention under-utilizes the GPU's streaming multiprocessors at the small batch sizes long contexts force; Flash-Decoding addresses this by additionally parallelizing over the KV sequence-length dimension, reporting up to 8× faster decoding at long context lengths in the authors' CodeLlama-34B benchmarks [[54](#ref-54)]. FlashInfer generalizes further into a JIT-compiled, block-sparse attention engine built to handle vLLM's and SGLang's paged, non-contiguous KV-cache layout natively; the authors report 29–69% inter-token-latency reduction against a compiler-backend baseline, 28–30% latency reduction for long-context inference, and 13–17% speedup for parallel generation in their own A100/H100 benchmarks, and it is integrated into vLLM, SGLang and MLC-Engine [[55](#ref-55)]. On the scheduling side, continuous (iteration-level) batching — introduced by Orca, which schedules admission and eviction every model iteration rather than every batch, via "selective batching" that lets requests at different sequence positions and phases share a single batched forward pass — is what lets a paged KV-cache manager actually keep GPU occupancy high as requests arrive and finish at different times; Orca reports a 36.9× throughput improvement over NVIDIA FasterTransformer at matched latency on GPT-3 175B [[56](#ref-56)]. That multiplier is measured against a 2022 non-continuous-batching baseline and should be read historically rather than as a live benchmark: vLLM, TGI, SGLang and TensorRT-LLM all now implement continuous batching as standard practice, and it is this combination — IO-aware kernels, paging, quantization and iteration-level scheduling together — rather than any single technique that produces the throughput reported below.

When the cache fits well in GPU memory and is efficiently managed, decode becomes bandwidth-bound against the HBM figures above rather than compute-bound. A Micron engineering analysis of Llama-2-70B inference on an HGX H100 system found throughput scaling near-linearly with batch size until the KV cache saturates memory capacity or bandwidth, after which throughput collapses or the process fails with an out-of-memory error [[44](#ref-44)]; the qualitative pattern generalizes, but the specific batch size at which it occurs is tied to that model, GPU and sequence-length configuration and does not transfer directly to other models or to the higher-bandwidth H200/B200/B300 parts above. The clearest historical demonstration that memory management, not raw compute, was the binding constraint came from vLLM's June 2023 introduction of PagedAttention, which reported up to 24× higher throughput than a then-current, non-batching HuggingFace Transformers baseline and 2.2–3.5× over HuggingFace TGI on LLaMA-7B/13B with ShareGPT-length requests (Table 1) [[8](#ref-8)]. That 24× figure should be read as a 2023-era historical marker rather than a claim about today's alternatives: it compares against a serving stack that itself lacked continuous batching, and vLLM's own peer-reviewed SOSP evaluation reports a more modest — but still substantial — 2–4× improvement over stronger, already-batching baselines (FasterTransformer and Orca) at matched latency [[39](#ref-39)]. The durable claim both papers support is not the specific multiplier but the underlying one: GPU memory management, not FLOPs, was the binding constraint on 2023-era LLM serving throughput, and closing that gap — via paging, quantization, and the kernels and schedulers above — is what let GPU compute utilization approach its ceiling.

**Table 1.** Serving throughput of vLLM (PagedAttention) relative to HuggingFace Transformers (HF) and HuggingFace Text Generation Inference (TGI), as reported by the vLLM team in June 2023 for LLaMA-7B on an NVIDIA A10G and LLaMA-13B on an NVIDIA A100 (40 GB), with request lengths sampled from ShareGPT [[8](#ref-8)]. The HF baseline here is a non-batching 2023 configuration; see the main text for vLLM's own later comparison against stronger, already-batching baselines.

| Workload | vLLM vs. HF | vLLM vs. TGI |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| One output completion per request | 14×–24× | 2.2×–2.5× |
| Three parallel output completions per request | 8.5×–15× | 3.3×–3.5× |

In summary, keeping the KV cache on-GPU and managing it with paging, quantization and IO-aware kernels under a continuous-batching scheduler yields the best achievable TTFT and TPOT on current hardware: the first token emerges as soon as prefill completes, and subsequent tokens stream at a rate bounded by HBM bandwidth — 3.35 to roughly 8 TB/s on current NVIDIA GPUs — rather than by avoidable memory waste. GPU HBM nonetheless remains scarce and expensive relative to host memory or storage, and the worked examples above show it exhausted by context lengths and batch sizes well within current production workloads. Before turning to what happens, and what it costs, when the cache is pushed beyond GPU memory entirely, the next sections first examine how the cache's size can be reduced at the source — through attention architecture, compression, and cross-request reuse.

## Shrinking the Cache at the Source: Attention Architectures

The preceding sections treat the number of key/value heads, head dimension and layer count as fixed properties of "the model" and optimize everything downstream – paging, quantization, offload. Those three numbers are themselves architectural choices, and since 2019 they have been the largest lever on KV cache size: for a dense transformer, KV bytes per token scale as

$$\text{KV bytes/token} = 2 \cdot L \cdot n_{kv} \cdot d_h \cdot s$$

where $L$ is the number of layers, $n_{kv}$ the number of key/value heads, $d_h$ the head dimension, and $s$ the bytes per stored element (2 for bf16/fp16, 1 for int8/fp8). The factor of 2 is for the separate key and value tensors. Every technique in this section attacks one of $n_{kv}$, $L$, or the "2×" itself, either by sharing heads, sharing across layers, compressing K and V into a joint latent, or replacing most layers' attention with a fixed-size recurrent state.

### Fewer heads to cache: MQA and GQA

Shazeer's multi-query attention (MQA) lets all query heads share a single key/value head, cutting $n_{kv}$ to 1 and the KV cache by a factor of $h$ (the original head count), at the cost of a measurable quality drop and a model that generally must be trained (or "uptrained") for it from the start [[42](#ref-42)]. Grouped-query attention (GQA) interpolates: query heads are split into $g$ groups, each sharing one KV head, so $n_{kv} = g$ recovers MHA quality at $g=h$ and MQA speed at $g=1$; Ainslie et al. show that uptraining an existing multi-head checkpoint to GQA-8 with only 5% of original pretraining compute nearly closes the quality gap to full MHA while matching MQA's latency [[45](#ref-45)]. GQA is now the default in essentially every recent open-weight dense model: Llama 3.1 8B/70B/405B all use 8 KV heads regardless of total head count (32/64/128) [[1](#ref-1)], and the same pattern recurs in Mistral, Qwen, Gemma and gpt-oss below. The mechanism is trivial to retrofit into any serving engine's paged-attention and FlashAttention kernels, since a GQA layer is still "one KV cache slab per layer," only narrower.

### Compressing instead of pruning: multi-head latent attention

DeepSeek-V2 took a different route: rather than sharing raw keys/values across heads, multi-head latent attention (MLA) down-projects them into a shared low-rank latent $\mathbf{c}^{KV}_t \in \mathbb{R}^{d_c}$ per token per layer, plus a small decoupled RoPE key of dimension $d_h^R$ that cannot be folded into the latent because rotary position embeddings are not compatible with the up-projection trick; only these two pieces need to be cached, giving $L \cdot (d_c + d_h^R) \cdot s$ bytes/token with no factor of 2 [[2](#ref-2)]. With $d_c = 4d_h$ and $d_h^R = d_h/2$, DeepSeek's own comparison table states this is "equal to GQA with only 2.25 groups" while matching or exceeding vanilla MHA quality, and reports a 93.3% KV cache reduction and 5.76× higher throughput relative to their own prior 67B dense model [[2](#ref-2)] – vendor-reported, against DeepSeek's own baseline, not an independent benchmark. DeepSeek-V3 keeps the same design at larger scale ($d_c=512$, $d_h^R=64$, $L=61$) [[57](#ref-57)]. DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp (September 2025) and DeepSeek-V3.2 (December 2025) add DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) on top of the same MLA cache: a learned top-$k$ selector (a "lightning indexer") restricts attention to the top-2048 cached latents per query, cutting per-layer attention compute from $O(n^2)$ to $O(nk)$ (where $n$ is sequence length and $k=2048$) without shrinking the cache itself — mechanism, training lineage and reported speedups covered under "Compressing and Evicting the Cache" below [[58](#ref-58), [59](#ref-59)]. DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp's own release notes describe it as "an intermediate step toward our next-generation architecture" [[58](#ref-58)]; the subsequent DeepSeek-V3.2 report (December 2025) does not repeat this framing, presenting itself instead as narrowing the gap with GPT-5 and Gemini-3.0-Pro on reasoning and agentic benchmarks, and its own conclusion lists specific remaining limitations relative to Gemini-3.0-Pro (narrower world knowledge from fewer training FLOPs, lower token efficiency, weaker performance on the hardest tasks) rather than framing V3.2 itself as transitional [[59](#ref-59)]. The next generation took the step that V3.2 did not: DeepSeek-V4 (April 2026; open weights, 1M-token context) compresses the cache along the *sequence* dimension. Its interleaved Compressed Sparse Attention (CSA) layers merge the KV entries of every $m$ tokens into one entry and then apply DSA-style top-$k$ selection over the compressed entries, while its Heavily Compressed Attention (HCA) layers merge every $m' \gg m$ tokens into one entry and attend densely; both keep a small uncompressed sliding window for local dependencies [[3](#ref-3)]. The Hugging Face reference implementation defaults to $m=4$ and $m'=128$ [[60](#ref-60)]. DeepSeek reports that at a 1M-token context V4-Pro needs 10% of V3.2's KV cache (V4-Flash, 7%), and roughly 2% of a BF16 GQA-8, head-dimension-128 baseline – vendor-reported estimates, not an independent measurement [[3](#ref-3)]. The price is heterogeneity: layers now hold caches of different sizes with different update and eviction rules, plus a per-request state cache for the sliding window and for tokens not yet compressed, which paged block managers must handle explicitly [[3](#ref-3)]. V3.2's sparse-attention kernels (a separate indexer cache, a paged layout tuned to block size 64) needed day-0 engineering work in vLLM before serving efficiently [[61](#ref-61)].

### Bounding the window: sliding and local/global interleaving

Mistral 7B pairs GQA (8 KV heads) with sliding-window attention (SWA): each layer attends only to the last $W=4096$ tokens in a fixed-size rotating buffer, while stacked layers still propagate information roughly $W \times L$ tokens back [[62](#ref-62)]. Because every layer is capped, the cache stops growing once the sequence exceeds $W$. Gemma 2 interleaves one sliding-window layer ($W=4096$) with one full-attention layer ($1{:}1$) [[63](#ref-63)]; Gemma 3 pushes this to $5{:}1$ with $W=1024$ to control KV memory at its 128K context [[64](#ref-64)]. In a separate 2B-parameter, text-only ablation using a different ($1{:}3$) local:global ratio at the same window size, the authors report that KV-cache memory overhead at a 32K-token context falls from 60% (global-only baseline) to under 15% – evidence for the mechanism, but not a measurement of the shipped $5{:}1$, 27B-scale configuration [[64](#ref-64)]. gpt-oss goes further, alternating a $W=128$ banded window with full attention every other layer, with only 8 KV heads for 64 query heads (group size 8) [[65](#ref-65)]. The trend (4096 → 1024 → 128) shows vendors trusting the global layers, not the local ones, for long-range recall, but none of these model cards publish an ablation isolating the accuracy cost of window size alone – treat the smallest windows as a compute/memory bet, not a proven-lossless one.

### Sharing keys and values across layers: CLA and YOCO

A layer-orthogonal idea is to stop assuming every layer needs its own KV projection. Cross-Layer Attention (CLA) shares KV activations between adjacent layers ("sharing factor" 2, 3, ...); combined with MQA, CLA2 gives another 2× cache reduction with under 1% perplexity change in 1B–3B-parameter experiments, a Pareto improvement over shrinking head dimension instead [[66](#ref-66)]. YOCO goes further: a "self-decoder" (the first $L/2$ layers, efficient attention) produces one global KV cache that every layer in the second-half "cross-decoder" reuses via cross-attention, so memory scales as $O(N + CL)$ instead of $O(NL)$; the authors report roughly 80× less KV memory at 65B scale and 2.87–71.8× faster prefill (from early-exiting before the cross-decoder), scaling to a 1M-token context [[67](#ref-67)]. Neither technique has, as of September 2026, shipped in a frontier open-weight production model at the scale GQA and MLA have – CLA's experiments stop at 3B parameters, YOCO's largest reported model is 13B – so generalization to 100B+ mixture-of-experts (MoE) models remains unverified rather than disproven.

### Replacing most layers' attention with recurrence

The most aggressive reduction discards per-token KV storage in most layers entirely. Jamba interleaves Transformer attention layers with Mamba (state-space) layers at roughly 1 attention layer per 8 total (its released 32-layer config keeps only 4), giving an 8× smaller KV cache than an equivalent all-attention model by the authors' own accounting – reproduced exactly by $32/4 = 8$ [[68](#ref-68)]. Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B mixes Gated DeltaNet (linear attention, a fixed-size recurrent state) with full GQA attention at 3:1 (36 linear layers to 12 attention layers) [[69](#ref-69)]. Kimi Linear hybridizes its own Kimi Delta Attention (KDA, a fine-grained-gated refinement of Gated DeltaNet) with full MLA layers at the same 3:1 ratio, reporting up to 75% less KV cache and 6× faster decoding at 1M-token context versus a matched full-MLA baseline [[70](#ref-70)]. MiniMax-01 places one softmax/GQA layer after every seven lightning-attention (linear) layers, motivated by their own finding that pure lightning attention has "limited retrieval capabilities" alone [[71](#ref-71)]. NVIDIA's Nemotron-H keeps only about 8% of layers as self-attention (10 of 118 in the 56B model, 4 of 52 in the 8B model), the rest split between Mamba-2 and FFN layers; the authors report up to 3× higher inference throughput than similarly-sized all-attention models (Qwen2.5, Llama-3.1) on NVIDIA H100 GPUs at a 65,536-token input / 1,024-token output setting – vendor-reported, at one specific sequence-length/hardware point, not shown to generalize across other context lengths or accelerators [[72](#ref-72)]. In each design the non-attention layers carry a *fixed-size* recurrent state, independent of context length, so total cache memory eventually comes to be dominated by the handful of retained attention layers alone.

### KV bytes per token: a config-based comparison

**Table 2.** Per-token KV cache size for a set of current open-weight models, computed from each model's published `config.json` (or, where noted, its architecture paper) using the formulas above. "Full-attn layers" counts only layers that materialize a growing KV cache; sliding-window and hybrid recurrent layers are noted separately since their steady-state cost saturates rather than growing linearly. All figures are bf16 (2 bytes/element); dividing by 2 or 4 approximates FP8/INT8 or FP4/INT4 caches.

| Model | Mechanism | Layers (total / full-attn) | KV heads (Q heads) | Head dim | KV bytes/token | Source |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| Llama-3.1-8B | GQA | 32 / 32 | 8 (32) | 128 | 131,072 (128 KiB) | [[1](#ref-1)] |
| Llama-3.1-70B | GQA | 80 / 80 | 8 (64) | 128 | 327,680 (320 KiB) | [[1](#ref-1)] |
| Mistral-7B-v0.1 | GQA + SWA ($W$=4096) | 32 / 32, capped at 4096 tok | 8 (32) | 128 | 131,072/token, plateaus at 512 MiB total | [[62](#ref-62)] |
| Qwen2.5-7B | GQA | 28 / 28 | 4 (28) | 128 | 57,344 (56 KiB) | [[73](#ref-73)] |
| Gemma-2-9B | GQA + local:global 1:1 ($W$=4096) | 42 / 21 full + 21 local | 8 (16) | 256 | 344,064 (336 KiB) if all layers uniform; effectively less at long context | [[63](#ref-63)] |
| Gemma-3-27B | GQA + local:global 5:1 ($W$=1024) | 62 / ~10 full + ~52 local | 16 (32) | 128 | 507,904 (496 KiB) if all layers uniform; ~10/62 of that at steady state | [[74](#ref-74)] |
| gpt-oss-120b | GQA + alternating banded(128)/full | 36 / 18 full + 18 banded | 8 (64) | 64 | 73,728 (72 KiB) if all layers uniform; ~half that at steady state | [[65](#ref-65)] |
| DeepSeek-V3 | MLA (compressed latent + decoupled RoPE) | 61 / 61 (compressed) | — | $d_c$=512, $d_h^R$=64 | 70,272 (68.6 KiB); ≈40,016 (39.1 KiB) in the FP8 KV layout vLLM ships | [[57](#ref-57), [61](#ref-61)] |
| Qwen3-Next-80B-A3B | Gated DeltaNet + GQA (3:1) | 48 / 12 full-attn | 2 (16) | 256 | 24,576 (24 KiB) from the 12 attention layers; 36 linear layers add a small fixed state | [[75](#ref-75)] |
| Kimi-Linear-48B | KDA + MLA (3:1) | 27 / 7 full-attn (MLA) | — | $d_c$=512, $d_h^R$=64 | 8,064 (7.9 KiB) from the 7 MLA layers; 20 KDA layers add a small fixed state | [[76](#ref-76), [70](#ref-70)] |
| Nemotron-H-56B | Mamba-2 + GQA (~8% attn) | 118 / 10 full-attn | 8 (64) | 128 | 40,960 (40 KiB) from the 10 attention layers; 54 Mamba-2 layers add a fixed state | [[72](#ref-72)] |
| Jamba-v0.1 | Mamba + GQA (1 attn per 8 layers) | 32 / 4 full-attn | 8 (32) | 128 | 16,384 (16 KiB) from the 4 attention layers | [[68](#ref-68)] |

Four rows cross-check against independently published figures. Llama-3.1-8B/70B: the Llama 3 paper's hyperparameter table states 32/80 layers, 8/8 KV heads and 32/64 attention heads (head dimension $4096/32 = 8192/64 = 128$) [[1](#ref-1)], matching the models' public `config.json`. Mistral-7B: the rolling-buffer formula reproduces the paper's own cache-memory figure – at $W$=4096 the cache caps at $131{,}072 \times 4096 = 512\text{ MiB}$ regardless of further context length – giving a 2× saving at the trained 8192-token context (derived here, not stated in the paper) and an 8× saving at 32,768 tokens, matching the paper's claim: "On a sequence length of 32k tokens, this reduces the cache memory usage by 8x, without impacting the model quality" [[62](#ref-62)]. Jamba: the claimed "8x smaller KV cache" falls out of $32 \div 4 = 8$ from its own published attention-layer ratio [[68](#ref-68)]. DeepSeek-V3, across organizations: its MLA hyperparameters ($d_c=512$, $d_h^R=64$, $L=61$) match between paper and public config, and vLLM's engineering team independently reports the FP8 KV entry as 656 bytes/token/layer ($512\text{ B latent} + 64{\times}2\text{ B RoPE key} + 16\text{ B scale} = 656$), using the same $d_c$/$d_h^R$ values [[61](#ref-61)].

### Trade-offs: quality, kernels, and paging/prefix-cache compatibility

GQA is the safe default: near-lossless when trained from scratch, cheaply retrofittable by uptraining, and supported natively by every mainstream serving engine's paged-attention and FlashAttention kernels, since a GQA cache is just a narrower version of the same per-layer tensor. MLA buys a larger reduction but is a from-scratch architectural commitment, not a drop-in conversion: its "absorb $W^{UK}$ into $W^Q$" inference-time trick changes matmul shapes enough that dedicated kernels (FlashMLA, custom decode paths in vLLM/SGLang/TensorRT-LLM) were needed before it served efficiently; DSA on top of it required a second indexer cache and new paged-block layouts, a non-trivial integration exercise even with the reference implementation in hand [[61](#ref-61)]. Sliding-window layers are well supported by windowed FlashAttention/xFormers kernels, but mixing attention types in one model requires a "hybrid KV cache manager" that allocates different physical memory per layer type rather than one uniform block size; vLLM's own account of adding Qwen3-Next support describes building exactly such a manager, alongside Triton kernels ported from the Flash Linear Attention project, to keep linear-attention and full-attention layers from fragmenting the same paged pool [[77](#ref-77)]. CLA and YOCO remain comparatively kernel- and framework-immature: their layer-sharing patterns are not yet first-class citizens in major engines' paging logic the way GQA and (increasingly) MLA are. Hybrid SSM/linear-attention models were the hardest case for prefix caching specifically: PagedAttention-style block reuse and radix-tree caches assume a cache that decomposes into independent, copyable per-token blocks, which holds for GQA, MQA, MLA, CLA and, within their window, sliding-window layers; a Mamba or gated-DeltaNet recurrent state is instead a single fused summary that cannot be split the same way, so a shared prompt prefix cannot simply have its state copied into a new request. As of September 2026 this has moved from unsolved to merely unfinished: vLLM has merged automatic prefix caching for Mamba1, Mamba2, ShortConv and Gated DeltaNet hybrid layers by checkpointing recurrent state at aligned block boundaries and resuming computation on a cache hit [[78](#ref-78)], and a first-party engineering write-up describes the Mamba-2 case as functional but "in an experimental state" as of November 2025 [[79](#ref-79)]. It ships opt-in rather than as the default, behind a `--mamba-cache-mode` flag, and – per the same tracking issue – making it the default, competitive in decode-heavy workloads, and compatible with speculative decoding remained open work as of September 2026 [[78](#ref-78)]; this is now a narrower problem of production hardening than of basic feasibility, unlike pure-attention models, where PagedAttention-style prefix caching is already the standard, on-by-default behavior of major serving engines (the subject of the "Reusing the Cache Across Requests" section below). None of this makes the techniques mutually exclusive – MLA, sliding windows and hybrid recurrence are each usually paired with GQA in their remaining full-attention layers, so in practice they stack rather than compete.

## Compressing and Evicting the Cache

Paging and 8-bit KV quantization defer, but do not solve, GPU memory pressure: at million-token contexts or large batch sizes even an 8-bit cache can exceed HBM. Two further strategies push past this ceiling: shrinking each cached element below one byte, or discarding most cached tokens and keeping only the ones a future query needs, either permanently (eviction) or transiently, re-selected at every step (sparse retrieval). Both trade quality for memory and bandwidth, and the size of that trade — not the headline ratio — is what this section examines.

### Sub-byte quantization: FP8, FP4, and learned codebooks

FP8 KV caches (vLLM, TensorRT-LLM) are a supported option on Hopper- and Blackwell-class GPUs — enabled explicitly (e.g. vLLM's `kv_cache_dtype="fp8"`; the default is otherwise the model's native dtype, and, as of late 2025, vLLM defaults to FP8 automatically only for specific architectures such as DeepSeek-V3.2) — halving KV memory versus bf16/fp16 [[51](#ref-51)]. Blackwell extends this to 4-bit floats: MXFP4 shares one power-of-two scale per 32-value block, while NVFP4 shares a higher-precision FP8 scale per 16-value block, giving lower quantization error on larger models [[80](#ref-80)]. In a December 2025 Blackwell benchmark, NVIDIA reports NVFP4 KV cache halving memory versus FP8 KV, doubling the effective context/batch budget, and staying under 1% accuracy change on LiveCodeBench, MMLU-Pro, MBPP, and RULER-64K; on Llama 3.3 70B, NVFP4 scores roughly 5 points higher than MXFP4 at the same width (vendor-reported, single model) [[4](#ref-4)]. 4-bit values must still be dequantized to FP8 before the attention matmul, so the compute saving is smaller than the memory saving.

Academic work pushes below 4 bits with data-dependent codebooks. KVQuant quantizes keys per-channel and *before* RoPE (which otherwise mixes channels of different magnitude), uses non-uniform, per-layer sensitivity-weighted levels, and stores the top ~1% of outliers per vector at higher precision, holding perplexity degradation under 0.1 on WikiText-2/C4 at 3 bits (4.8× vs fp16) across the LLaMA and Mistral families, and letting a LLaMA-7B model serve 1M tokens on one 80 GB A100 at 2 bits, or 10M tokens on 8 GPUs [[81](#ref-81)]. At 128K tokens the authors report LLaMA-7B's cache falling from 64 GB (fp16) to 16/12/8 GB at 4/3/2 bits — 8× at the most aggressive setting, since the outlier overhead is small [[81](#ref-81)]. QServe couples 4-bit KV quantization to the rest of the stack (4-bit weights, 8-bit activations, 4-bit KV), with a "SmoothAttention" transform shifting quantization difficulty to the unquantized queries; it reports end-to-end throughput 1.2×–1.4× (Llama-3-8B) and 2.4×–3.5× (Qwen1.5-72B) over TensorRT-LLM on A100/L40S, with L40S under QServe matching or exceeding A100 under TensorRT-LLM (self-reported) [[82](#ref-82)]. KIVI's tuning-free 2-bit scheme (per-channel keys, per-token values), discussed earlier, remains a useful floor case: like KVQuant it shrinks every token rather than discarding any, so it degrades gracefully as the ratio tightens [[5](#ref-5)]. LogQuant compresses selectively along an observed log-distributed attention pattern and reports 25% higher throughput and 60% larger batch size at fixed memory, with 40–200% better accuracy on math and code-completion tasks specifically than the two baselines LogQuant's own comparison names — the 2-bit quantizer KIVI and the eviction-based method H2O — at matched compression; because H2O evicts tokens outright rather than quantizing them to any bit-width, this is a comparison against other aggressive KV-compression strategies generally, not narrowly against other 2-bit quantizers, and not against uncompressed FP16 [[83](#ref-83)].

### Deciding what to keep: eviction and retention

A different family drops tokens to hold a fixed budget independent of length. StreamingLLM finds that a handful of initial tokens — "attention sinks" — absorb outsized attention regardless of content; a sliding window that drops them collapses in perplexity once full, but keeping four sink tokens plus a recent window restores stability to 4M+ tokens without fine-tuning, at up to 22.2× lower latency than recomputing a window from scratch [[84](#ref-84)]. The caveat: this restores *fluency*, not *recall* — content outside the sink-plus-window is gone, not approximated, so the method suits open-ended streaming better than tasks that must recall arbitrary earlier context.

H2O scores tokens by accumulated attention weight and splits a budget between these "heavy hitters" and recent tokens, framed as dynamic submodular maximization with an approximation bound. At a 20% budget it reports up to 5× KV memory reduction with no accuracy loss across OPT/LLaMA/GPT-NeoX (6.7B–175B) on HELM and lm-eval-harness, and up to 29× higher throughput than DeepSpeed Zero-Inference or Accelerate [[85](#ref-85)]. SnapKV instead treats the *end of the prompt* as an observation window, keeping only the prior tokens each head consistently attends to from it; fine-tuning-free, it reports 3.6× faster decoding and 8.2× lower memory at 16K tokens, with only a small NIAH drop out to 380K tokens [[86](#ref-86)]. Its authors note the limit directly: it compresses the *generation-phase* cache after the prompt is processed, so it cannot extend a model's long-context ability nor cut prefill cost [[86](#ref-86)].

PyramidKV varies SnapKV's per-head budget *by layer*, following an observed "pyramidal" pattern where attention is diffuse low and concentrated high. On LongBench it matches full-cache accuracy at a 12% budget, and at an extreme 0.7% beats StreamingLLM, H2O, and SnapKV by up to 20.5 points on TREC; on NIAH, 128 entries per layer let LLaMA-3-70B match full-cache accuracy exactly [[87](#ref-87)]. Ada-KV targets a separate inefficiency: all of the above split a budget *evenly* across heads despite heads varying sharply in attention concentration. It reallocates a shared budget across heads by minimizing a derived eviction-loss bound, plugs into SnapKV or PyramidKV at no added memory cost, and reports gains over uniform allocation across 13 RULER and 16 LongBench datasets — widest question-agnostically, where SnapKV's observation-window trick is least reliable [[88](#ref-88)].

### Retrieval-preserving sparsity: Quest and trainable sparse attention

Eviction carries a structural risk: a token dropped because it looked unimportant for one query may be exactly what a later, different query needs. Quest avoids this by discarding nothing: it keeps the full paged cache but at each step scores per-page key summaries against the *current* query to bound each page's attention weight, then attends only to the top-K pages — since the query changes every step, so does the selection. It reports up to 7.03× self-attention speedup and 2.23× end-to-end latency reduction at 32K context with negligible loss on PG19 perplexity, passkey retrieval, and LongBench [[89](#ref-89)]. For a cache of $L$ tokens in pages of size $S$ with $K$ pages selected, Quest loads roughly $1/S + KS/L$ of the cache per step — small when $K \ll L/S$, but this cuts *bandwidth and compute*, not resident memory: the full cache still has to be stored.

DeepSeek's Native Sparse Attention (NSA) makes the selection pattern *trainable*. A 27B-parameter MoE model with NSA, pretrained on 270B tokens and context-extended to 32K via YaRN, combines compressed coarse blocks, selected fine blocks, and a local window behind hardware-aligned kernels, matching or exceeding a dense baseline on general, long-context, and reasoning benchmarks, with up to 9.0×/6.0× forward/backward speedup over FlashAttention-2 at 64K — at research scale [[90](#ref-90)]. Its production successor, DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA), shipped in the 685B-parameter DeepSeek-V3.2-Exp model released 2025-09-29: a small "lightning indexer" scores each query against every prior key and selects a top-$k$ (2048 default) subset for attention, cutting cost from $O(L^2)$ to $O(Lk)$ [[59](#ref-59)], with DeepSeek reporting "virtually identical model output quality" and benchmark performance "on par with" dense DeepSeek-V3.1-Terminus (self-reported, as of September 2025) [[58](#ref-58)]. Like Quest, DSA selects over an otherwise fully retained cache, so its gain is compute and latency, not stored bytes.

**Table 3.** Reported memory savings and accuracy impact for representative methods. Figures are as reported by the cited source; model, hardware, and baseline vary per row.

| Method | Bits / budget | Reported memory saving | Reported accuracy impact | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| KIVI | 2-bit K/V (per-channel K, per-token V) | ~8× smaller KV tensor by bit-width (2.6× lower end-to-end peak memory incl. weights, per the paper's own reported figure) | Minimal degradation, tuning-free (LLaMA family) | [[5](#ref-5)] |
| KVQuant (nuq3-1%) | 3-bit + 1% outliers | 4.8× vs fp16 | <0.1 perplexity increase, WikiText-2 & C4 | [[81](#ref-81)] |
| KVQuant (nuq2, no outliers) | 2-bit | 8× vs fp16 | Substantially higher: LLaMA-7B WikiText-2 PPL 7.23 vs 5.68 baseline (+1.55); used for the 1M/10M-token long-context demonstration, not the headline accuracy claim | [[81](#ref-81)] |
| QServe (QoQ, W4A8KV4) | 4-bit KV + 4-bit weights, 8-bit activations | 4× KV vs fp16 (system-level) | Small zero-shot loss vs fp16 (self-reported, A100/L40S) | [[82](#ref-82)] |
| NVFP4 KV cache | 4-bit (E2M1 + FP8 block scale) | 50% vs FP8 KV (≈4× vs fp16) | <1% on LiveCodeBench, MMLU-Pro, MBPP, RULER-64K (vendor-reported, Blackwell) | [[4](#ref-4)] |

| PyramidKV | 12% budget (layer-adaptive) / 0.7% extreme | ~8× (12%) to ~140× (0.7%) | Matches full cache at 12%; up to 20.5-pt gain over other evictors at 0.7% | [[87](#ref-87)] |
| Ada-KV (+SnapKV/PyramidKV) | Same budget, head-adaptive allocation | No added memory vs base method | Consistent gains over uniform per-head budgets, RULER + LongBench | [[88](#ref-88)] |
| Quest | Top-K pages per step (full cache retained) | None (bandwidth/compute only) | Negligible loss on PG19 ppl., passkey, LongBench at 32K | [[89](#ref-89)] |
| DeepSeek DSA (V3.2-Exp) | Top-2048 tokens via learned indexer (full cache retained) | None (compute only; $O(L^2)\!\to\!O(Lk)$) | "Virtually identical" to dense baseline (self-reported) | [[59](#ref-59), [58](#ref-58)] |

### What the benchmarks show, and where they mislead

Most methods above are validated on LongBench (21 English/Chinese long-document tasks: QA, summarization, few-shot, code, synthetic retrieval) [[91](#ref-91)], RULER (needle-in-a-haystack variants plus multi-hop tracing and aggregation) [[92](#ref-92)], and NIAH tests descending from Greg Kamradt's original single-fact probe [[93](#ref-93)]. RULER's own headline finding warns against reading too much into NIAH alone: models scoring near-perfectly on vanilla NIAH degrade sharply on RULER's harder categories well before their claimed context length, and only about half of the 17 models tested held acceptable accuracy at 32K [[92](#ref-92)]. Since most compression papers report NIAH and/or LongBench, that warning applies to them too: passing NIAH is necessary but not sufficient, and a method's own paper is rarely its harshest test.

Two independent studies confirm this for compression specifically. A cross-method benchmark spanning quantization (FlexGen, KIVI), eviction (StreamingLLM, H2O), prompt compression (LLMLingua2), and KV-free architectures found quantization degrades gracefully and uniformly — it never discards a token outright — while other approaches are task-dependent: StreamingLLM and H2O hold near-perfect accuracy on code, hard prompt compression is weakest on the needle test since deleting the needle's surrounding text is fatal by construction, and middle-token retrieval (InfLLM) beats plain recency eviction (StreamingLLM) on NIAH; the study confines itself to "long input, short output" tasks, explicitly excluding multi-turn conversation and long generation as open problems [[94](#ref-94)].

Microsoft's SCBench fills that gap, testing methods across the full KV-cache lifecycle (generation, compression, retrieval, reloading) under multi-turn and multi-request reuse — the latter being prefix caching in production. Across sparse attention, eviction, KIVI quantization, and retrieval methods on six long-context LLMs, sub-linear-memory methods (most eviction/constant-budget approaches) perform reasonably single-turn but degrade substantially once reused across turns or requests, while $O(n)$-memory sparse attention (Quest-like, NSA/DSA-like) holds up better; query-conditioned methods such as SnapKV, which use the input's final segment to decide what to keep, specifically lose effectiveness in multi-request mode, where that segment differs across requests sharing a prefix [[95](#ref-95)].

Reasoning and long-generation workloads expose a third failure mode none of the above is built to catch, since all probe long *inputs* with short outputs. Chain-of-thought models take short prompts and produce very long, repetitive outputs — DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B has been reported generating over 8× longer traces than a matched ground-truth answer on math benchmarks [[96](#ref-96)]. Methods tuned for long-prompt compression do not automatically transfer: one evaluation found arithmetic-reasoning accuracy stable above roughly a 40% cache budget across several compression methods but falling sharply below ~20% — on the hardest configuration, from about 0.75 to under 0.5 — and, comparing model types at a 10% budget under matched compression, a DeepSeek-R1-distilled model held around 0.60 accuracy against roughly 0.5 for an instruction-tuned model of the same base that started from a higher (0.8) uncompressed accuracy [[97](#ref-97)]. Because reasoning traces are also unusually redundant, purpose-built methods can beat a full cache outright: R-KV, scoring redundancy alongside importance, matches full-cache math accuracy at a 10–34% budget and reaches 105% of it at 16% on one benchmark — the ceiling is not "how much can be discarded" but "how much of the trace is discardable regardless" [[96](#ref-96)].

### Why eviction resists prefix sharing and storage offload

Prefix caching and offload both depend on KV *blocks* being immutable and shareable: identical prefixes across requests point to the same physical blocks (copy-on-write), and offloaded blocks move as opaque, contiguous units [[8](#ref-8)]. Eviction breaks both assumptions: a policy's surviving-token set is a function of the *specific request's* later content (H2O's attention scores, SnapKV/PyramidKV's observation window, Ada-KV's per-head reallocation), so two requests sharing a prefix generally retain a *different* subset of it once their queries diverge. Block-level sharing does not carry over — the system either caches the pre-eviction prefix and re-evicts per consumer, forfeiting the saving on shared prefixes, or caches several divergent post-eviction versions, forfeiting deduplication. Offload compounds this: moving a non-contiguous, request-specific set of surviving positions needs per-token provenance tracking rather than contiguous-block transfer, defeating the large sequential transfers that make offload bandwidth-efficient. SCBench's finding that constant-budget methods underperform specifically in multi-request (shared-prefix) mode, while $O(n)$-memory sparse attention holds up better, is the empirical signature of this mismatch [[95](#ref-95)]. Quantization sidesteps the problem: it changes *how* a token is stored, not *whether* it survives, so a quantized prefix remains the same token set as its fp16 original and block identity is preserved — at the cost of a footprint that, unlike eviction, still grows linearly with sequence length.

### Multimodal KV caches: vision and video tokens change the arithmetic

Everything above is sized and validated on text tokens. Vision-language models break that assumption at the input side: a single image commonly expands into hundreds to thousands of visual tokens before it ever reaches the transformer's KV cache, so a multimodal prompt's cache can be dominated by visual tokens even when the accompanying text is short, and a multi-image or video input multiplies that cost across frames [[98](#ref-98)]. Because visual and textual tokens are cached by the same attention mechanism, every eviction and quantization technique above still applies in principle — the cache is still "a token's key and value, kept or dropped" — but several 2026 papers find that budget allocation tuned on text degrades when applied unmodified to a mixed-modality cache. HYBRIDKV classifies attention heads as "static" (stable once an image is seen, safe to prune early via text-anchored pruning) or "dynamic" (importance keeps shifting through decoding, better served by chunk-wise retrieval from an offloaded cache) and, combining both strategies with a hierarchical, head-aware budget allocator, reports up to 7.9x KV memory reduction and 1.52x faster decoding on Qwen2.5-VL-7B across 11 multimodal benchmarks with "almost no performance drop or even higher" accuracy than the full-cache baseline [[98](#ref-98)]. VisCache targets the video case specifically — filtering temporally redundant keyframes with a lightweight VLM before prefill, then applying a layer-wise, parabolic compression budget to what remains — reporting up to 2.35x speedup at only 19–28% KV retention [[99](#ref-99)].

A further multimodal-specific failure mode has no text-only analogue. "Attention Myopia" documents that, because causal attention lets earlier tokens accumulate more attention weight, images placed later in a multi-image or multi-frame sequence are systematically under-attended regardless of their actual relevance to the query — so any importance-based eviction policy inherited unmodified from text-only methods disproportionately discards later images, and in some layers discards a later image's tokens entirely [[100](#ref-100)]. The proposed fix rebalances attention scores across image position before scoring for eviction and reports roughly 1.5x faster decoding at only 20% of the original KV footprint while holding accuracy stable across more than 20 datasets [[100](#ref-100)]. None of the methods surveyed here is yet a default, on-by-default feature of a production serving engine the way FP8 KV or automatic prefix caching are — they remain research systems evaluated chiefly on Qwen2.5-VL- and LLaVA-family models — and, per the myopia finding, a text-only compression method applied unmodified to a multimodal cache risks a position-dependent accuracy failure that none of the text-only benchmarks discussed above would surface.

## Reusing the Cache Across Requests: Prefix Caching and Beyond

The preceding sections scope the KV cache to a single request. In practice, many requests to a deployment share large spans of identical tokens — a system prompt, tool schemas, a retrieved document, earlier conversation turns — and a system that recognizes the overlap can reuse the already-computed keys and values instead of recomputing them, so prefill work scales with tokens genuinely new to the request rather than its total length. Because prefill is compute-bound and dominates TTFT [[30](#ref-30), [29](#ref-29)], cross-request cache reuse acts directly on TTFT rather than TPOT, and is now the primary latency and cost lever in agentic and multi-turn deployments, where most tokens sent on a given turn were already sent on the previous one.

### Automatic prefix caching: hash-based blocks and radix trees

vLLM's Automatic Prefix Caching (APC), building on the block-based KV cache allocator (PagedAttention) introduced above, partitions the KV cache into fixed-size blocks and hashes each block from its own tokens plus the previous block's hash, so a block's identity encodes its full prefix [[101](#ref-101)]. A new prompt is hashed block by block against a hash-keyed pool; matched blocks are pinned against eviction, and LRU reclaims the rest, evicting a request's last (least reusable) block first. As of v0.11 vLLM defaults to collision-resistant SHA-256, and an optional `cache_salt` isolates reuse per tenant, closing a timing side-channel by which one tenant could otherwise infer another's cached content from latency alone [[101](#ref-101)]. vLLM is explicit about the limit: APC "does not reduce the time of generating new tokens," so it helps only prefill and gives nothing when a request shares no prefix with prior traffic or the response is long relative to the prompt [[9](#ref-9)].

SGLang's RadixAttention keeps the KV cache of all recent requests live in a radix tree instead of freeing it at completion, evicting leaves first so shared ancestors persist [[10](#ref-10)]. It defines hit rate as cached-prompt-tokens / total-prompt-tokens, and proves (Theorem 3.1) that scheduling by longest-matched-prefix-first equals a depth-first radix-tree traversal and achieves an optimal cache hit rate once the cache holds at least the longest request — the basis of its cache-aware scheduler. The paper proves this for hit rate specifically, not throughput directly; a higher hit rate plausibly helps throughput, but that link is not what Theorem 3.1 establishes, and the online case only approximates the DFS order the proof relies on. Across agent, few-shot, RAG and multi-turn chat workloads, SGLang reports up to 6.4$\times$ higher throughput than contemporary systems, with first-token latency the biggest beneficiary [[10](#ref-10)]. Mooncake's cluster scheduler (Conductor) extends this across machines, estimating TTFT per candidate prefill node from request length and that node's prefix-match length, and routing to whichever minimizes predicted TTFT rather than balancing on queue length alone [[17](#ref-17)].

### Empirical hit rates and the effect on TTFT and prefill compute

**Table 4.** Reported effects of KV cache reuse across requests ("up to" results under the cited setup, not guarantees for arbitrary workloads).

| System | Metric | Reported effect | Setup |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SGLang RadixAttention | Throughput vs. contemporary systems | up to 6.4$\times$ | Agent, few-shot, RAG, JSON-decoding, multi-turn chat benchmarks [[10](#ref-10)] |
| Mooncake (Kimi, production) | Effective request capacity under SLO vs. baseline scheduling | 59%–498% increase | Replayed real Kimi traces on a dummy LLaMA2/3-70B-architecture model; 16 nodes $\times$ 8 A800 GPUs [[17](#ref-17)] |
| Mooncake, global vs. local cache pool | Hit rate / prefill compute time | up to 2.36$\times$ higher hit rate; up to 48% less prefill time | Same trace replay; pooled vs. per-node cache, 3M-token cap each [[17](#ref-17)] |
| CacheBlend | TTFT / throughput vs. full recompute | 2.2–3.3$\times$ lower TTFT; 2.8–5$\times$ higher throughput | Three open LLMs of different sizes, four QA/summarization benchmarks, on vLLM [[102](#ref-102)] |
| EPIC (LegoLink) | TTFT / throughput vs. CacheBlend-15 (prior SOTA position-independent caching) | up to 8$\times$ lower TTFT; up to 7$\times$ higher throughput | Single A100-80GB GPU, synthetic Poisson-arrival workload on one dataset (2WikiMQA); reused content not at the prompt's start (RAG/few-shot), where prefix-only caching gets little or no hit; authors caution these numbers "should be interpreted cautiously when considering real-world scenarios" [[103](#ref-103)] |
| CacheGen | KV size vs. quantized transfer; load delay vs. recomputing from text | 3.5–4.3$\times$ smaller; 3.1–4.7$\times$ lower TTFT | KV fetched over a networked link, four datasets, three LLMs [[104](#ref-104)] |
| WEKA Augmented Memory Grid (Nebius replay) | Cache hit rate / requests served vs. HBM-only | 93% vs. 40% hit rate; 2.4$\times$ requests; 3.2$\times$ fewer prefilled tokens in total | Eight-hour replay of captured agentic coding sessions, DeepSeek-V4-Pro (NVFP4), one 8$\times$ B300 node, vendor-reported [[11](#ref-11)] |

Mooncake's public production trace — 23,608 real Kimi tool-and-agent requests sampled over about one hour, content redacted, block-hash identity preserved [[17](#ref-17)] — is the most direct evidence of reuse at scale, though two published analyses of Mooncake report different capacity metrics on it. An earlier technical-report version of the system, analyzing this trace under one global LRU pool, finds the hit rate rising from 30% at 1,000 cached blocks to 50% at 50,000 blocks and plateauing near 51% only beyond roughly 100,000 blocks: capacity past a moderate size has fast-diminishing returns here, while too little capacity leaves reusable prefixes evicted before use [[105](#ref-105)].
