The peer-reviewed FAST'25 paper instead frames capacity in raw token counts across its production-derived workloads: a 3M-token local cache (roughly 1 TB of DRAM per node) falls short of half the theoretical maximum hit rate, while pooling about 50M tokens (roughly 20 nodes' DRAM) nearly reaches it [[17](#ref-17)]. A later journal extension of the same system reports that live hit rates peaked near 50% and fell below 20% under load, until the team added hierarchical VRAM/DRAM/SSD placement and a semantics-aware eviction policy favoring context-cache entries over ephemeral results such as web-search output, raising the global hit rate a further 93% [[106](#ref-106)]. These are single-deployment figures on a stand-in model, not a cross-vendor benchmark, and read best as evidence of high reuse in agentic/chat traffic rather than a universal hit rate.

### Beyond exact prefixes: non-prefix and position-independent reuse

Hash- and radix-based caching only helps when shared text is a byte-identical *prefix*. RAG breaks this: reused chunks rarely start the prompt, and one differing token earlier invalidates every cached block after it, so prefix caching degrades toward full recompute once an input concatenates several independently-reused chunks [[102](#ref-102)]. CacheBlend reuses precomputed KV for each chunk regardless of position and selectively recomputes a small, dynamically chosen subset of tokens per layer (empirically under 15%) to restore the cross-chunk attention naive splicing ignores; recompute for layer $L$ pipelines with loading layer $L{+}1$'s cached KV, adding negligible latency and letting cache live on slower, higher-capacity media without a TTFT penalty [[102](#ref-102)]. EPIC's LegoLink uses a cheaper, static rule instead: it always recomputes only the first $k \le 32$ tokens of every non-initial chunk, enough to break the "attention sink" that otherwise corrupts accuracy under naive splicing, keeping recompute cost roughly linear in chunk count rather than quadratic in prompt length [[103](#ref-103)]. Both trade a small, bounded recompute for reuse patterns exact-prefix caching cannot exploit at all, so their headline numbers are not measured against a prefix-caching baseline: CacheBlend's are against full recompute, and EPIC's are against CacheBlend itself, the strongest prior position-independent-caching system. Both settings were chosen because prefix caching would show no benefit there in the first place, since none of the reused content sits at the prompt's start.

### Compressing the cache for network transfer: CacheGen

When a shared KV cache sits on a different machine than the consuming GPU — a disaggregated prefill/decode cluster, or a cache tier reached over ordinary datacenter networking rather than NVLink — fetching it competes with prefill itself for TTFT, since a KV cache commonly reaches tens of gigabytes while inter-server bandwidth is often single-digit Gbps [[104](#ref-104)]. CacheGen treats this as a streaming problem: it encodes KV tensors into compact bitstreams via quantization and arithmetic coding tuned to the cache's locality and per-layer sensitivity, decodes on GPU pipelined with transmission, and — like adaptive video bitrate — lowers per-chunk encoding level (or falls back to raw text for recomputation) as bandwidth drops, holding load time to an SLO [[104](#ref-104)]. Across four datasets and three models it cuts encoded KV size 3.5–4.3$\times$ versus a quantization-only baseline and total load delay 3.1–4.7$\times$ versus recomputing from text, with under 2% accuracy loss [[104](#ref-104)]. It complements the on-GPU quantization schemes discussed earlier (KIVI, LogQuant): those shrink the cache's resident footprint; CacheGen shrinks it for time in flight.

### Hosted prompt-caching APIs

**Table 5.** Hosted prompt/context caching terms, snapshot as of 2026-09-27. (Section: Measuring and Pricing the Cache gives the corresponding price-vs-hit-rate curve and GPU-hour cost accounting for the same three providers.)

| Provider | Trigger | Min. cacheable prefix (example) | Cache-write price | Cache-read price | TTL |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Anthropic (Sonnet 5) | Explicit `cache_control`, or one automatic field | 1,024 tok. (512 on Opus 5/5.5, Fable 5/5.1, Mythos 5/5.1) | 1.25$\times$ input (5-min) or 2$\times$ (1-hour) | 0.1$\times$ input (0.05$\times$ Opus 5.5; 0.025$\times$ Fable/Mythos 5.1) | 5 min default, free refresh on hit; 1 h at 2$\times$ write [[12](#ref-12)] |
| OpenAI (GPT-5.6+) | Automatic; explicit breakpoints optional | 1,024 tok. (strict) | 1.25$\times$ input | 0.1$\times$ input | $\ge$30 min via `ttl`, refreshed on reuse [[13](#ref-13)] |
| OpenAI (pre-5.6, e.g. GPT-5.5) | Automatic | 1,024–2,048 tok. (model-dep.) | none | model-specific (e.g. 0.1$\times$ on GPT-5.5) | 5–10 min inactivity up to 1 h, or up to 24 h extended [[13](#ref-13), [107](#ref-107)] |
| Gemini (implicit, 2.5+) | Automatic, best-effort | 2,048–4,096 tok. | none | best-effort; no guarantee | opportunistic [[108](#ref-108)] |
| Gemini (explicit) | Developer-created cache object | same min. as implicit | input rate + storage | ~0.1$\times$ input on the Developer API, varying by model; Google Cloud's enterprise API states a guaranteed 90% discount on Gemini 2.5+ and 75% on Gemini 2.0 | caller TTL, default 1 h, no max; storage e.g. \$4.50/M tok./hr on a Pro-tier model [[108](#ref-108), [109](#ref-109), [110](#ref-110)] |

Section: Measuring and Pricing the Cache derives this same worked example in full, alongside a broader per-provider cost-vs-hit-rate analysis; the point specific to reuse is given briefly here. A worked example on Anthropic's multipliers: an 8,000-token stable prefix (system prompt plus tool schemas) reused over 10 turns costs $8{,}000\times\$2.50/10^6\approx\$0.020$ to write once, then $8{,}000\times\$0.20/10^6\approx\$0.0016$ per turn to read on each of the next 9 — about \$0.034 total, versus $10\times8{,}000\times\$2/10^6=\$0.16$ paying full price every turn: a 78.5% reduction on the shared portion, or a ~4.65$\times$ blended cost reduction [[12](#ref-12)]. The compute-side effect is larger still: engines skip the forward pass on a hit rather than merely discounting it [[9](#ref-9), [10](#ref-10)], so prefill FLOPs on turn 10 scale with only the newest ~200–400 tokens rather than the accumulated ~9,000 — a ~23–45$\times$ compute reduction, well beyond the ~4.65$\times$ blended figure, which is diluted by the one-time write cost and turn 1's full-price miss rather than reflecting the 10$\times$ marginal discount cache reads alone receive. OpenAI reports a matching asymmetry: caching cuts TTFT up to 80% on prompts above 10,000 tokens, more than the 90% price discount alone predicts, since removing computation also removes queuing on the prefill path [[111](#ref-111)].

### Why agentic and multi-turn workloads see very high hit rates

If a session resends a stable prefix of $P$ tokens (system prompt, tools, retrieved context) plus $\Delta$ new tokens per turn, hit rate at turn $t$ is $h(t)=\frac{P+(t-1)\Delta}{P+t\Delta}$: large already at $t=1$ when $P\gg\Delta$, and approaching $(t-1)/t \to 1$ as the session lengthens regardless of $P$ — exactly vLLM's two canonical APC cases, long-document Q&A (large constant $P$) and multi-round chat (growing, append-only history) [[9](#ref-9)]. Anthropic's automatic-caching mode is built around this shape, moving the breakpoint forward each turn so only the newest turn is a write [[12](#ref-12)]. Tool-calling agents are the extreme case — schema and instructions are large and static, each round-trip appends only a short call and result, so $\Delta\ll P$ by construction — consistent with Mooncake's production traces, already dominated by chat/agent sessions, sustaining hit rates near 50% once capacity was adequate [[17](#ref-17)].

This favorable case has limits. A hit needs an exact match to the breakpoint: reordering retrieved documents, inserting a timestamp early in the prompt, or randomizing few-shot order — all common in production RAG and eval harnesses — silently defeats hash/radix matching, the exact gap CacheBlend and EPIC target rather than a corner case [[102](#ref-102), [103](#ref-103)]. Hosted caches are scoped per tenant (OpenAI: not shared between organizations), so benefit is bounded by one tenant's repeat traffic, and Gemini's implicit mode gives no cost-saving guarantee even when a prefix repeats [[108](#ref-108)]. The guaranteed 90%/75% split for Gemini's explicit cache (Table 5) is documented on Google Cloud's enterprise API surface rather than the Gemini Developer API cited elsewhere in this section; Gemini 2.0 models no longer appear on the Developer API's own current pricing or caching documentation as of September 2026, so that 75% figure is best read as an enterprise-specific or historical number rather than one confirmed on the consumer-facing API [[110](#ref-110)]. Every technique here shortens prefill only — TPOT and output cost are unchanged — so workloads dominated by long generation rather than long, repeated prompts see smaller end-to-end gains [[9](#ref-9)].

## Extending KV Cache Beyond GPU Memory (CPU Offloading & NVMe)

When the KV cache cannot fit in GPU VRAM – because of long sequences, large batches, or a small GPU – the cache (and sometimes the model weights) can be moved to host DRAM or to NVMe storage. This expands usable capacity by one to three orders of magnitude, but every tier below GPU HBM is reached over an interconnect that is both lower-bandwidth and, for storage, non-coherent, so offloading trades memory capacity for added latency and, in the worst case, a collapse in per-token throughput. The size of that penalty – and whether it is worth paying – depends on the specific interconnect, the access pattern (bulk sequential transfer vs. many small reads), and whether it would actually be cheaper to recompute the missing keys and values than to fetch them.

### Interconnect Bandwidth: PCIe, NVLink-C2C, and NVMe

The bandwidth available to move KV data off the GPU has roughly doubled with each of the last three PCIe generations, and on NVIDIA's Grace-based superchips it is now an order of magnitude higher than PCIe. **Table 6** gives the exact, per-direction figures.

**Table 6.** GPU$\leftrightarrow$host interconnect bandwidth by link, per direction and bidirectional aggregate.

| Link | Per-direction | Bidirectional | Introduced |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| PCIe Gen4 x16 | ~31.5 GB/s | ~63 GB/s | 2017 |
| PCIe Gen5 x16 | ~63 GB/s | ~126 GB/s | 2019 |
| PCIe Gen6 x16 | ~128 GB/s | ~256 GB/s | 2022 (ratified) |
| NVLink-C2C (Grace Hopper / Grace Blackwell) | 450 GB/s | 900 GB/s | 2022 (GH200); 2024 (GB200) |

*PCIe Gen4/5/6 figures are effective, encoding-inclusive x16 per-direction throughput from PCI-SIG's own bandwidth table [[112](#ref-112)] – a pre-ratification 2021 presentation marking the Gen6 row "Projected"; the PCIe 6.0 spec was itself ratified to PCI-SIG members in January 2022 [[113](#ref-113)]. NVLink-C2C figures are NVIDIA's published Grace-CPU-to-Hopper/Blackwell-GPU chip-to-chip bandwidth [[114](#ref-114), [115](#ref-115)].*

Three corrections to how this bandwidth is usually discussed matter specifically for KV-cache offloading:

**PCIe is the default GPU$\leftrightarrow$host link.** A GPU on PCIe Gen4 x16 – used, for example, in InstInfer's testbed [[30](#ref-30)] – moves KV data to and from host DRAM at about 31.5 GB/s per direction; Gen5 roughly doubles this to 63 GB/s, and Gen6 to about 128 GB/s, per PCI-SIG's bandwidth table [[112](#ref-112)], ratified to PCI-SIG members in January 2022 [[113](#ref-113)] – though commercial host platforms have lagged the specification by several years. This is one to two orders of magnitude below GPU HBM bandwidth (~3 TB/s aggregate on an H100 SXM [[44](#ref-44)]) – the offload penalty this section discusses is fundamentally this bandwidth gap, not a difference in kind.

**NVLink-C2C changes the calculus on Grace-based platforms.** NVIDIA's Grace Hopper (GH200) and Grace Blackwell (GB200/GB300) superchips connect the Grace CPU to its GPU with NVLink-C2C, a coherent interconnect delivering 900 GB/s of bidirectional bandwidth (450 GB/s per direction) – about 7× a PCIe Gen5 x16 link in each direction, and roughly 14× a Gen4 link [[114](#ref-114), [115](#ref-115)]. On these platforms, CPU DRAM sits close enough to GPU speed that "CPU offload" is a materially different proposition than on a conventional PCIe-attached host: as of the shipping GH200 product specification, up to 480 GB of Grace LPDDR5X becomes addressable by the GPU, at up to 500 GB/s of CPU-side memory bandwidth [[116](#ref-116)] – within a single order of magnitude of on-package HBM bandwidth, rather than two orders of magnitude below it. (NVIDIA's original November 2022 blog described a pre-shipping configuration at up to 512 GB and 546 GB/s [[114](#ref-114)]; 480 GB/500 GB/s reflects the shipped product, current as of September 2026.) This does not eliminate the memory hierarchy – Grace's own LPDDR5X bandwidth and GPU HBM remain separate, lower ceilings that NVLink-C2C cannot exceed – but it substantially narrows the CPU tier's penalty relative to PCIe-only systems, and represents a genuinely different regime from the PCIe-bound conditions under which FlexGen, DeepSpeed's ZeRO-Inference, and InstInfer were built and measured.

**NVMe access latency is microseconds, not milliseconds; what constrains KV offload is bandwidth, not latency.** A representative contemporary enterprise NVMe SSD, the Micron 9400, reports a typical (queue-depth-1, 4 KB random) read latency of 69 $\mu s$ and write latency of 10 $\mu s$ [[117](#ref-117)] – three to four orders of magnitude faster than the millisecond-scale figures sometimes assumed for disk. For KV-cache offload, where each fetch moves megabytes to gigabytes of contiguous key/value data rather than a single 4 KB block, per-request access latency is largely irrelevant; what determines fetch time is sustained bandwidth, which for a single drive's own external PCIe interface is on the order of 3–6 GB/s and up to 7 GB/s sequential read/write for current mainstream enterprise NVMe SSDs [[30](#ref-30), [117](#ref-117)]. Latency becomes a first-order concern only when the access pattern is many small, non-contiguous block reads issued at low queue depth – a real risk for block-based KV layouts (e.g., PagedAttention-style paging) that scatter a sequence's cache across many small pages rather than one contiguous region.

### Offloading to CPU Memory (RAM)

Two distinct DeepSpeed technologies are often conflated here, and the distinction matters for what follows. **ZeRO-Offload** [[118](#ref-118)] is a *training*-time technique: it offloads optimizer states and gradients to CPU DRAM to fit larger models on a single GPU during training, and neither targets nor was evaluated on inference serving. The inference-time analogue is **ZeRO-Inference** [[119](#ref-119)], which instead pins model weights in CPU or NVMe memory and streams them layer-by-layer into the GPU during the forward pass, freeing GPU memory for a much larger batch size. A later DeepSpeed release extended ZeRO-Inference to also offload the *KV cache* itself to CPU memory, combined with 4-bit weight quantization; DeepSpeed reports up to a 20× throughput improvement over its own prior (non-KV-offloaded) release, a release-over-release headline figure distinct from the FlexGen comparison below [[120](#ref-120)]. In its own head-to-head comparison table (single A6000 GPU, 252 GB host memory, prompt length 512, generation length 32), this release reaches generation throughput at or above FlexGen's best CPU/NVMe-offload configuration on OPT-30B, OPT-66B, and OPT-175B – the three model sizes both frameworks support in that table – and separately reports results on BLOOM-176B and LLaMA-2-70B, for which FlexGen is marked "Unsupported," so no head-to-head figure exists for those two [[120](#ref-120)]. Hugging Face Accelerate offers comparable CPU- and disk-offload primitives for model weights, via its `device_map="auto"`, `cpu_offload()`, and `disk_offload()` mechanisms [[121](#ref-121)].

Once weights or KV cache are offloaded, the mechanism is the same regardless of which framework performs it: older key/value blocks are moved to host DRAM over PCIe (or NVLink-C2C, on Grace platforms) and brought back for attention. Because this happens on every decoding step for every offloaded token, the decode phase becomes bandwidth-bound on whichever interconnect is in use rather than bound by GPU compute – a point InstInfer's authors make explicitly, describing PCIe as "a new performance bottleneck" for this reason [[30](#ref-30)]. On a plain PCIe Gen4 x16 host this can reduce per-token throughput enough that CPU offloading beyond a small batch size becomes impractical for latency-sensitive serving; it is used almost exclusively in throughput-oriented, latency-tolerant settings (batch inference, benchmarking, offline scoring).

Mitigations fall into three categories: (1) **prefetching**, overlapping computation on the current token with the transfer of the next token's KV data, which hides some but not all of the latency, particularly at long sequence lengths where transfer volume dominates compute time [[122](#ref-122)]; (2) **selective/quantized offload**, keeping a window of recent tokens on GPU and moving only older or less-attended tokens to CPU, optionally in compressed (8-bit or 4-bit) form to cut the bytes crossing PCIe [[122](#ref-122)]; and (3) **importance-guided prefetch**, exemplified by InfiniGen, which runs a cheap approximate ("rehearsal") attention pass on CPU to identify which cached keys the next decoding step will actually need, then fetches only that subset from host memory rather than the full window – reported to improve the throughput of an offloading-based system by up to 3× over prior KV-cache management while also improving output quality relative to a fixed eviction policy [[123](#ref-123), [122](#ref-122)]. Head-wise offloading is a complementary, more recent direction: HeadInfer keeps only a subset of attention heads' KV cache on GPU at any time and computes the rest from CPU-resident data, reporting a reduction in on-GPU KV footprint for a 1-million-token sequence on Llama-3-8B from 128 GB to 1 GB (total GPU memory 207 GB to 17 GB), and separately demonstrating 4-million-token generation with an 8B model on a single 24 GB consumer GPU (e.g., an RTX 4090) [[50](#ref-50)]. All of these techniques reduce *how much* must be fetched or *when* it is fetched; none of them raises the underlying interconnect bandwidth, so they compose with, rather than replace, the choice of link discussed above.

### Offloading to SSD/NVMe (Persistent Storage)

When even CPU DRAM is insufficient – or when the goal is minimizing hardware cost for throughput-oriented batch inference rather than minimizing latency – KV cache (and weights) can be spilled to NVMe. **FlexGen** is the reference system here [[124](#ref-124)]. Running OPT-175B on a single 16 GB NVIDIA T4 GPU with 208 GB of CPU DRAM and 1.5 TB of SSD, FlexGen reached, for the first time, a sustained generation throughput of 1 token/s using an effective batch size of 144 with 4-bit weight and KV-cache compression. Because the widely repeated "100×" figure is easy to misstate, the comparison is worth stating precisely: at a batch latency of about 4,000 seconds (roughly 67 minutes for the whole batch, not per token), FlexGen's 4-bit configuration reached 100× higher maximum throughput than the contemporaneous CPU/disk-offloading baselines it compared against – DeepSpeed's (pre-KV-offload) ZeRO-Inference and Hugging Face Accelerate – limited to batch size 2 by out-of-memory errors at that point on the curve; without compression, FlexGen reached 69× at about 12,000 seconds (200 minutes) [[124](#ref-124)]. The comparison is against prior *offloading* systems on the same single-GPU hardware, not against GPU-only serving, which cannot run OPT-175B at all on a 16 GB GPU without offloading, model parallelism, or heavy compression. The throughput gain also comes bundled with the latency cost: these are batch-completion times measured in thousands of seconds, and FlexGen is explicitly designed for latency-insensitive, throughput-oriented workloads, not interactive serving.

InstInfer's and DeepSpeed's own benchmarks probe the same phenomenon at smaller scale, and let us see precisely where FlexGen (and CPU-only offload) breaks down. InstInfer's authors evaluated OPT-13B, FP16, input/output length 1024, on a single 48 GB A6000 GPU (Gen4 PCIe host, ~32 GB/s to CPU): DeepSpeed's host-memory-only offload outperforms the other dense (non-InstInfer) schemes at small batch sizes (4–16), where the cache still fits in host memory, but degrades sharply – by 32.6× relative to batch size 16 – once it must additionally spill from host memory to SSD at batch size 32; FlexGen's SSD-offload mode holds up to batch size 64, and hits an outright out-of-memory error at batch size 128, because the *intermediate* KV cache generated during prefill (not just the steady-state cache) exceeds available VRAM [[30](#ref-30)]. A latency breakdown at these settings shows the bottleneck shifting with batch size: at small batches (4–8), where the KV cache still fits on GPU, most decode time is spent on weight access; once the KV cache must be offloaded, KV-cache access alone accounts for up to 98.9% of decode latency [[30](#ref-30)].

InstInfer's proposed fix is to move computation to the data rather than the reverse: a Computational Storage Drive (CSD) – an NVMe SSD with an on-board FPGA – performs the decode-phase attention computation itself, next to the flash, and returns only the much smaller attention output to the GPU over a direct peer-to-peer DMA path that bypasses the host [[30](#ref-30)]. Their testbed CSD is a Xilinx Zynq7045 FPGA-based prototype whose flash-channel bandwidth (8 channels $\times$ 1.4 GB/s) was configured to match a Samsung 980 Pro's aggregate throughput, paired with an actual Samsung 980 Pro (2 TB) for storage capacity, on a Gen4 PCIe host [[30](#ref-30)] – not a literal 980 Pro with an embedded FPGA. The CSD's realized internal bandwidth was about 11.2 GB/s – higher than a typical NVMe drive's own external PCIe interface bandwidth (3–6 GB/s, since most drives use a Gen4 x4 link) but still below the ~32 GB/s Gen4 x16 GPU$\leftrightarrow$host link in their setup.

The headline 11.1× and the more conservative 6.85× are not the same comparison, and conflating them overstates what in-storage computation alone achieves. InstInfer's own evaluation reports 6.85× over FlexGen with dense (non-compressed) in-storage attention at the *same* batch size, 64 – the largest batch FlexGen itself can reach (it runs out of GPU memory for the intermediate prefill-phase KV cache at batch size 128). The 11.1× figure is dense in-storage attention *plus* an additional sparse-attention KV-compression scheme (SparF, 1/8 compression ratio), run at batch size 256 – a batch size FlexGen cannot reach at all – compared against FlexGen's own maximum (at batch size 64) [[30](#ref-30)]. InstInfer's authors separately isolate SparF's own contribution, holding batch size fixed at 256: SparF improves throughput by 2.08× over dense in-storage attention at that same batch size [[30](#ref-30)]. Combining the two reported multipliers ($11.1 / 2.08 \approx 5.3$) implies dense in-storage attention alone reaches roughly 5.3× FlexGen's maximum at batch size 256 – a number the paper does not state directly, but which shows that part of the 6.85×$\to$11.1× gap reflects InstInfer's architecture supporting a batch size FlexGen cannot reach, on top of (not only) the compression from SparF. This is one instance of a broader pattern (also visible in InfiniGen and HeadInfer above): offload-side systems research has moved from "move the bytes faster" toward "move fewer bytes, and move the computation instead." All of these results, however, are reported for a specific model size (13B), hardware pairing (A6000 plus a single consumer NVMe drive), and workload (1024-token sequences); none of the cited papers demonstrates that the reported multipliers hold for substantially larger models, multi-drive storage arrays, or online (rather than offline/batch) serving, where tail latency rather than aggregate throughput is the binding constraint.

The move to faster NVMe also matters directly, independent of any algorithmic change. DeepSpeed's 2025 benchmarks of ZeRO-Inference on LLaMA-3-70B (single GPU, prompt length 512, generation length 32, batch size 96, using NVIDIA GPUDirect Storage to move data directly between NVMe and GPU memory) report generation throughput rising with available NVMe bandwidth: 7 tokens/s with four PCIe Gen4 NVMe drives on an Azure NC\_A100\_v4 (A100-class) VM, and 17 tokens/s with four Gen5 drives, rising to 26 tokens/s with eight Gen5 drives, both measured on an Azure ND-H200-v5 (H200-class) VM [[125](#ref-125)]. Because the GPU generation changes alongside the NVMe generation between the Gen4 baseline and the Gen5 comparison points, this is suggestive of the bandwidth-bound framing above rather than a clean, single-variable demonstration of it: a newer, faster GPU can also relieve other parts of the pipeline (e.g., the PCIe/host-to-GPU transfer itself, or on-GPU compute), so some of the 7$\to$17 tokens/s jump cannot be attributed to NVMe bandwidth alone. The 17$\to$26 tokens/s step, by contrast, holds the GPU generation fixed (both on ND-H200-v5) and varies only drive count, so it is a cleaner within-generation demonstration of the bandwidth-bound claim.

### Recompute vs. Reload: A Worked Trade-off

Offloading assumes that fetching cached keys and values is cheaper than recomputing them through prefill. That is not automatic: it depends on the ratio of KV-cache bytes to be fetched (which scales with sequence length and model width) against the FLOPs required to recompute them (which scales with sequence length and total model size), relative to the available compute throughput and the bandwidth of whichever tier holds the cache. Cake, an ICML 2025 system built specifically around this trade-off, frames the decision using representative bandwidth/capacity figures for a single-node GPU server: roughly 2 TB/s and 80 GB for GPU memory, roughly 25 GB/s and 1.8 TB for CPU memory, and 0.5–4 GB/s and 26 TB for local/remote disk [[126](#ref-126)]. Because disk capacity so far exceeds GPU and CPU memory, the same paper cites an earlier system, AttentionStore, which found that roughly 80% of KV-cache hits in a multi-turn-conversation workload land on the slowest, disk tier [[127](#ref-127), [126](#ref-126)] – exactly the regime in which the recompute-vs-load choice is least obvious.

The following illustrative calculation (assumptions stated; not a benchmark) makes the trade-off concrete for a 13B-class dense model with the published Llama-2-13B architecture – 40 layers, 5120-dimensional hidden state, no grouped-query attention [[128](#ref-128)] – reusing a $T=2048$-token cached prefix:

- **KV-cache volume.** FP16 KV bytes per token $= 2~(\text{K, V}) \times L \times d_{model} \times 2~\text{bytes} = 2 \times 40 \times 5120 \times 2 = 819{,}200$ bytes $\approx 0.82$ MB/token. For $T=2048$ tokens: $819{,}200 \times 2048 \approx 1.68$ GB (using the exact per-token byte count; rounding to "0.8 MB/token" first, as some published treatments do, understates the total by about 2%, giving 1.64 GB instead).
- **Recompute cost.** Prefill FLOPs $\approx 2 \times P \times T = 2 \times 13\times10^{9} \times 2048 \approx 5.3\times10^{13}$ FLOPs (the linear, matmul-dominated approximation; at this length the quadratic self-attention term is still sub-dominant, but it will not stay that way as $T$ grows – see below). On an RTX A6000 (154.8 TFLOPS dense FP16 – half NVIDIA's published 309.7 TFLOPS sparse figure via the Ampere 2:1 structural-sparsity convention, since the datasheet gives no dense figure [[129](#ref-129)]) at an assumed, deliberately conservative 50% of peak utilization ($\approx 77$ TFLOPS effective), recompute takes $5.3\times10^{13}/7.7\times10^{13} \approx 0.69$ s.
- **Reload cost by tier**, at the bandwidths established above:

**Table 7.** Illustrative time to reload a 1.68 GB KV-cache prefix (13B model, $T=2048$) by source tier, vs. ~0.69 s to recompute it via prefill.

| Source tier | Bandwidth | Time to load 1.68 GB |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| NVLink-C2C (Grace Hopper/Blackwell) | 450 GB/s | ~3.7 ms |
| PCIe Gen5 x16 (CPU DRAM) | 63 GB/s | ~27 ms |
| PCIe Gen4 x16 (CPU DRAM) | 31.5 GB/s | ~53 ms |
| Local NVMe, 2-drive RAID-0 [[130](#ref-130)] | ~13 GB/s | ~129 ms |
| Local NVMe, single drive [[130](#ref-130)] | ~6.5 GB/s | ~258 ms |
| Remote store, 100 Gbps RDMA | ~12.5 GB/s | ~134 ms |
| Remote store, 10 GbE | ~1.25 GB/s | ~1.34 s |

At this model size and sequence length, reloading from any *local* tier – CPU DRAM over any PCIe generation, NVLink-C2C, or a local NVMe drive – is cheaper than recomputing, by a factor of roughly 2.7× (single NVMe drive) to roughly 185× (NVLink-C2C). The trade-off flips toward recompute only once the cache must come over a slow, oversubscribed, or congested remote link; a plain 10 GbE path (1.25 GB/s) is already slower than recompute in this example. This matches the design premise of Mooncake's disaggregated KVCache pool, which relies on a well-provisioned RDMA fabric (up to 8×400 Gbps between nodes) specifically so that loading remains cheaper than recomputing even when the cache lives on a remote node's CPU/DRAM/SSD rather than locally [[17](#ref-17)].

Three caveats keep this from being a general rule. First, the crossover point moves with model size and sequence length: KV bytes per token scale roughly with $d_{model}$ (linear in model width), while recompute FLOPs per token scale with total parameter count $P$, which grows faster than $d_{model}$ across a model family (roughly $P \propto L \times d_{model}^2$ for fixed head dimension) – so larger models shift the balance further toward loading, and the quadratic growth of the self-attention term with $T$ eventually makes recompute relatively more expensive at very long context lengths as well, pushing in the same direction. Second, this calculation ignores contention: a production server holds many concurrent requests sharing the same PCIe/NVMe links and the same GPU compute, so the *effective* per-request bandwidth and FLOPs are both lower than the peak figures used here, and recomputing also consumes GPU cycles that could otherwise serve other requests' decode steps – precisely the resource-contention problem Cake's scheduler is built to manage, by running recompute and reload for different chunks of the same request in parallel rather than treating them as a binary choice [[126](#ref-126)]. Third, none of the bandwidths above account for fixed per-request overhead (kernel launch, page-table lookups for non-contiguous block-based layouts) that can matter when the cached region is small or fragmented.

### System Design Considerations

The pattern across InfiniGen, HeadInfer, InstInfer, Cake, and Mooncake is convergent: newer systems minimize *how much* KV data crosses the slow link, move computation to where the data already sits (in-storage attention, head-wise offload), or overlap reload with recompute rather than treating interconnect bandwidth as fixed and unavoidable. None of this makes the underlying bandwidth gap disappear – it remains the reason CPU/NVMe offload is a throughput-for-latency trade rather than a free capacity increase – but it does mean that a flat bytes-over-bandwidth estimate, without accounting for compression, selective fetching, or near-data compute, will systematically overstate the real cost of offloading in a well-engineered system.

Speculative decoding is sometimes described as a fix for offload latency; it is not. Speculative decoding [[131](#ref-131), [132](#ref-132)] reduces the number of full forward passes of the *large* model needed per output token, by having a small draft model propose several tokens that the large model verifies in one batched pass. Whether that helps an offloaded deployment depends entirely on whether the KV-cache fetch for the verification pass can be amortized across the $k$ draft tokens in one larger transfer rather than issued per token; if the serving stack still fetches KV data per generated token regardless of how those tokens were produced, speculative decoding changes nothing about the I/O bottleneck – it only reduces large-model compute, a separate axis from the memory-bandwidth constraint this section addresses. It is also not free: the draft model itself requires compute (and, for long contexts, its own KV cache), and an acceptance rate below 100% wastes some of it. Production stacks such as ByteDance's Doubao-1.5-pro do pair speculative decoding with decode-oriented hardware to cut TPOT [[133](#ref-133)], but that describes a general low-cost-decode strategy, not an offload-latency-masking mechanism.

## Disaggregated Serving and KV Transport

The preceding sections treated the KV cache as something to keep close to the compute that consumes it — on-GPU, then CPU, then SSD. An orthogonal strategy, now common in large production deployments, keeps the cache in GPU memory throughout but moves it *between* GPUs: the prefill phase (compute-bound, prompt-parallel) and the decode phase (memory-bandwidth-bound, autoregressive) run on separate machines, and only the resulting KV cache — not the prompt or the model — crosses the network. (Some production stacks instead ship intermediate per-layer activations across this same boundary rather than a finished KV cache — a related but distinct transfer profile this section does not size.) This section surveys the systems that implement this "disaggregated" or "P/D-split" architecture and works through the bandwidth it demands.

### Why separate the phases

DistServe and Splitwise established the case independently. Colocating prefill and decode in one continuous-batching engine forces a scheduler to interleave a compute-bound burst (prefill) with an ongoing memory-bound stream (decode); the burst stalls in-flight decodes, inflating tail inter-token latency, and chunking the prefill only trades one tail cost for another [[16](#ref-16)]. DistServe reports serving 7.4× more requests, or meeting 12.6× tighter SLOs, than a state-of-the-art colocated baseline at matched TTFT/TPOT targets and >90% SLO attainment, on A100 clusters [[16](#ref-16)]. Splitwise's characterization of production Azure traces found token generation persistently under-utilizes GPU compute even at large batch sizes, so it dedicates separate "prompt pool" and "token pool" machines, reporting up to 1.4× higher throughput at 20% lower cost, or 2.35× more throughput at matched power and cost, versus homogeneous non-split clusters [[15](#ref-15)]. Both papers make the transfer itself nearly free by overlapping it with computation: Splitwise streams each layer's KV block over InfiniBand as soon as that layer finishes rather than waiting for the whole prefill, measuring a non-overlapped transfer floor of about 5 ms on an H100 pair (400 Gb/s) and 8 ms on A100 (200 Gb/s) — under 7% of prefill time, on cloud InfiniBand links in the 25–50 GB/s-per-GPU-pair range [[15](#ref-15)].

### Production KVCache-centric systems

Moonshot AI's **Mooncake**, the serving platform behind the Kimi chatbot, pools the CPU, DRAM, SSD and RDMA-NIC resources of the *entire* GPU cluster into one disaggregated KVCache store, interconnected at up to 8×400 Gb/s per node, scheduling requests around where cache blocks already live rather than around GPU load alone [[17](#ref-17)]. Its Conductor scheduler reuses cached prefixes from remote nodes, streams newly computed KV to the decode node per-layer, and predicts overload to reject requests early rather than let them consume prefill compute they cannot finish decoding within SLO. On replayed production traces, Mooncake increases effective request capacity 59%–498% over baseline systems at matched TTFT/TBT SLOs; in Kimi's own fleet (over 100 billion tokens/day) it serves 115% more requests on A800 clusters and 107% more on H800 clusters than its predecessor (vendor-reported, production clusters) [[17](#ref-17)]. DeepSeek's own numbers show the same pattern at frontier MoE scale: DeepSeek-V3/R1 runs prefill (expert-parallel degree 32, four-node units) and decode (degree 144, eighteen-node units) as physically separate pools, reporting 73.7k input tokens/s and 14.8k output tokens/s per H800 node (8 GPUs) over a 24-hour production window (vendor-reported) [[134](#ref-134)].

### Platform- and engine-level support

**NVIDIA Dynamo** packages disaggregation as a first-class mode: a `PrefillRouter` selects a prefill worker by KV-overlap score or load, hands the request to a decode worker, and moves the KV cache with **NIXL**, NVIDIA's transport-agnostic library exposing one non-blocking, GPU-to-GPU point-to-point API over NVLink, InfiniBand/RoCE (via UCX), GPUDirect Storage or TCP [[135](#ref-135), [136](#ref-136)]. A separate KV-aware router tracks which blocks already live on which worker, so cache-hot requests can skip a remote prefill entirely. **llm-d** — a CNCF sandbox project founded by Red Hat, Google Cloud, IBM Research, CoreWeave and NVIDIA — layers the same idea onto Kubernetes: its Inference Gateway extension picks a prefill/decode pair per request and composes with prefix-cache-aware routing so only the *uncached* suffix of a prompt goes to a remote prefiller, over RDMA via NIXL or Mooncake's transfer engine (TCP is an explicit fallback marked "testing and development only") [[137](#ref-137), [138](#ref-138)]. Partner-reported benchmarks collected in the project (vendor-reported, individually sourced, not independently reproduced here) include a 70% output-throughput gain from P/D disaggregation on GPT-OSS/B200, and 10%–30% on GPT-OSS-120B/Llama-3.3-70B on AMD MI300X [[139](#ref-139)].

At the engine level, both major open-source stacks expose disaggregation as a pluggable transport. vLLM's `KVTransferConfig` supports interchangeable connectors — `NixlConnector`, `MooncakeConnector`, `LMCacheConnectorV1`, a raw-NCCL `P2pNcclConnector` — but its own documentation states plainly that disaggregated prefill "does not improve throughput" by itself: the benefit is controlling tail inter-token latency and tuning TTFT independently of TPOT [[140](#ref-140)]. SGLang's `--disaggregation-mode {prefill,decode}` delegates transfer to Mooncake's engine or NIXL/UCX over RDMA; its GPU staging buffer for heterogeneous prefill/decode tensor-parallel sizes reports 2–5× higher transfer throughput than naive per-token slicing [[141](#ref-141)]. On AMD MI300X with the Mooncake backend, one prefill/one decode instance reports 2.2×–6.9× more SLO-compliant *goodput* than a collocated server, the larger gain on the longer-prompt (3,200-in/800-out) workload versus heavy-decode (1,024-in/2,048-out) (vendor-reported, AMD ROCm blog) [[142](#ref-142)]. **LMCache** occupies an adjacent niche: a standalone KV cache layer that vLLM calls into, rather than a serving engine itself, offering tiered GPU/CPU/disk/remote storage for cross-instance KV reuse (see "The Cache as Storage: Shared and Persistent KV Tiers" for that architecture in full), and, via its NIXL integration, peer-to-peer transfer for disaggregated prefill without recomputing context [[143](#ref-143), [144](#ref-144), [145](#ref-145)].

### KV-cache-aware routing: a distinct axis from P/D disaggregation

Disaggregation decides which *machine* runs a request's prefill versus decode; a separate, increasingly load-bearing problem is deciding which *replica* — among several running the same, non-disaggregated engine — already holds the most of a new request's prefix in its KV cache, since plain round-robin or least-connections load balancing sends cache-hot requests to cold pods as often as warm ones. llm-d's router implements this as prefix-cache-aware routing through its Endpoint Picker (EPP), with two implementations: an "approximate" scorer that estimates token counts from character ratios with no model-server integration, and a "precise" scorer that subscribes to vLLM's `KVEvents` stream over ZeroMQ to maintain an exact, near-real-time index of which content-hashed blocks are resident on which pod, scoring candidates by matched-block fraction and weighting a block's contribution by which memory tier holds it [[146](#ref-146)]. On a published 16×H100 benchmark (8 replicas, TP=2, Qwen3-32B), switching a plain Kubernetes Service from round-robin to the precise scorer raised output throughput 120% (5,722 to 12,598 tok/s) and cut mean TTFT from 58.1 s to 0.247 s — a reduction of more than 99.5% — with request-completion rate essentially unchanged, since the gain comes almost entirely from eliminated queuing and redundant prefill rather than added capacity [[147](#ref-147)]. An unaffiliated AWS reference deployment on smaller hardware (8× Mistral-7B replicas on EC2 G5) reports a directionally identical, if smaller, result under sustained multi-turn Poisson load: p90 latency 44–69% lower than round-robin across a three-minute sustained-load window, with round-robin p90 degrading to 4.4 s under sustained load against 1.4 s for cache-aware routing [[148](#ref-148)]. The same mechanism appears under other names elsewhere in this ecosystem: NVIDIA Dynamo's KV-aware router (introduced earlier in this section) performs the analogous function for its own stack, and Google reports it built into GKE's Inference Gateway, where an independent benchmark commissioned by Google (Principled Technologies) measured 15.7% higher throughput, 92.8% lower mean TTFT, and 62.6% lower inter-token latency against a round-robin-only managed Kubernetes service on identical 8×A100 hardware serving Llama 3.1 8B [[149](#ref-149)]. Because this routing layer and P/D disaggregation both consume the same KV-locality signal, llm-d documents its precise scorer as composing directly with disaggregation — identifying which specific blocks a prefill worker must transfer to a decode worker, not merely which pod to send a request to in the first place [[146](#ref-146)].

### Transport options

| Transport | Typical bandwidth | Scope | Notes |
| --- | ---: | --- | --- |
| NVLink (H100 NVLink Switch) | 900 GB/s bidirectional per GPU | Intra-node / switched fabric | Fastest option, but confined to an NVLink-connected topology, not a general inter-rack link [[150](#ref-150)] |
| GPUDirect RDMA over InfiniBand NDR | 400 Gb/s per ConnectX-7 port; 800 Gb/s via twin-port aggregation | Inter-node | GPU-to-GPU, bypasses host CPU and PCIe staging copies; the substrate under Mooncake, Splitwise and NIXL's UCX backend [[151](#ref-151), [152](#ref-152)] |
| RoCE (RDMA over Converged Ethernet) | Up to 400 GbE per ConnectX-7 port | Inter-node | Same RDMA semantics on converged Ethernet fabrics, used where dedicated InfiniBand is unavailable [[153](#ref-153)] |
| TCP (no RDMA) | Fabric-dependent, well below line rate for small transfers | Inter-node fallback | NIXL and llm-d mark this explicitly as a development-only fallback, not a production transport [[138](#ref-138)] |

### A worked calculation

The bytes that must cross this fabric per request follow directly from model architecture. For a transformer with $L$ layers, $n_{kv}$ KV heads and head dimension $d_h$, storing both $K$ and $V$ at $b$ bytes/element, bytes/token $= 2 \times L \times n_{kv} \times d_h \times b$.

Llama 3 70B uses grouped-query attention with $L=80$ layers, $n_{kv}=8$ KV heads and head dimension $d_h = 8192/64 = 128$ [[1](#ref-1)]. At FP16 ($b=2$ bytes): $2 \times 80 \times 8 \times 128 \times 2 = 327{,}680$ bytes, i.e. **320 KiB per token**.

For an 8,192-token prompt (Llama 3's native training context), the KV cache to move is $320\text{ KiB} \times 8192 = 2.5\text{ GiB}$ (2.68 GB). At idealized, single-flow line rate:

| Link | 8,192-token prompt (2.5 GiB) | 256-token prompt (80 MiB) |
| --- | ---: | ---: |
| 100 Gb/s | ~215 ms | ~6.7 ms |
| 400 Gb/s | ~54 ms | ~1.7 ms |
| 800 Gb/s | ~27 ms | ~0.8 ms |

These are lower bounds: real RDMA goodput runs below line rate, and Splitwise's own measurements found a roughly constant 5–8 ms floor per transfer from connection and synchronization overhead, independent of payload size [[15](#ref-15)]. FP8 KV quantization — one byte per element instead of FP16's two — roughly halves every figure in the table [[51](#ref-51)]; KIVI's more aggressive 2-bit asymmetric quantization compresses further still, reporting up to an 8× reduction in KV cache size relative to FP16, not a simple halving [[5](#ref-5)]. Tensor parallelism changes the picture further: sharding KV heads across $p$ GPUs, each with its own NIC, both divides the per-link payload by roughly $p$ and lets shards move in parallel — the mechanism behind Mooncake's 8×400 Gb/s aggregate figure and SGLang's GPU staging buffer for heterogeneous prefill/decode TP [[17](#ref-17), [141](#ref-141)].

### When disaggregation does not pay off

The 8,192-token figures make disaggregation an easy trade: 27–215 ms of transfer is small next to the hundreds of milliseconds of queuing delay it removes by isolating a long prefill from an ongoing decode stream [[16](#ref-16)]. The 256-token case is the opposite regime, and the vendors and projects surveyed above converge on the same warning independently. vLLM states plainly that disaggregated prefill does not raise throughput on its own [[140](#ref-140)]. Ray Serve's guidance lists short outputs, network limitations and small models as the three cases favoring a colocated deployment, since KV transfer latency "is mostly determined by network bandwidth" and can dominate when transfer is unnecessary [[154](#ref-154)]. NVIDIA's Dynamo documentation is equally direct: disaggregation "is not automatically better. For small models, short prompts, low concurrency, or clusters without a fast KV-transfer fabric, an aggregated deployment is simpler and often faster" [[155](#ref-155)]. The mechanism is the one the worked calculation exposes: at 256 tokens, the theoretical transfer time (under 7 ms even at 100 Gb/s) is already comparable to the fixed RDMA connection/handshake overhead Splitwise measured, and to the cost of recomputing that small a prefix locally — so shipping the request to a remote prefiller pays real network and scheduling cost for compute the decode node's own, already-warm GPU could have done for free, particularly when a high prefix-cache hit rate means little of the prompt is actually uncached. Splitwise made the same point structurally, choosing serialized (not layer-wise-overlapped) transfer for small prompts because, at that size, the saved latency does not justify the added synchronization complexity [[15](#ref-15)].

None of this generalizes cleanly to the opposite extreme. Disaggregation's premise rests on prefill being compute-bound and decode memory-bound; at context lengths well beyond the 8K figure above, prefill's own attention cost grows with history length and starts to resemble decode's memory-bandwidth profile, eroding the split the architecture is built on. DeepSeek-V3/R1 is also a reminder that the dense-model arithmetic above does not transfer to large MoE models directly: its prefill and decode pools differ not just in count but in expert-parallelism degree (32 vs. 144) and node count (4 vs. 18), a partitioning problem the per-token KV-byte formula does not capture [[134](#ref-134)]. Model size cuts the same way from the other side: for models small enough that both phases fit comfortably in one GPU's compute and memory budget, NVIDIA's own guidance is to keep them aggregated rather than pay disaggregation's fixed operational and network cost for a phase-interference problem that scale has not yet created [[155](#ref-155)].

## The Cache as Storage: Shared and Persistent KV Tiers

Every tier examined so far — GPU HBM, host DRAM, local NVMe — is scoped to one server. Once a KV cache must outlive a single node's process, survive a restart, or be reused by a request that lands on a different GPU, it becomes a storage problem in the literal sense: bytes with a key, a lookup path, a retention policy, and a network in between. Over 2025–2026 this moved from a handful of research systems (Mooncake, AttentionStore) [[17](#ref-17)] to a full commercial ecosystem — an open-source caching layer (LMCache), a vendor-neutral tiering API (NVIDIA Dynamo's KV Block Manager), and a new storage-processor product category from NVIDIA itself — plus dated, quantitative benchmark claims from most major storage vendors. This section surveys that ecosystem as of September 2026, then works through when fetching a cached prefix from each tier is actually cheaper than recomputing it.

### Software tiering: LMCache and Dynamo's KV Block Manager

LMCache, the storage layer most widely embedded into vLLM, organizes offload as an ordered fallback: GPU memory → CPU RAM → local disk → remote store [[19](#ref-19)]. Its multiprocess mode formalizes this as two tiers: an L1 fast tier (CPU memory, or an NVMe slab reached via GPUDirect Storage/cuFile) holding every chunk currently in use, and a set of L2 "secondary" backends that a `StoreController` pushes to asynchronously and a `PrefetchController` pulls from on a miss [[156](#ref-156)]. Shipped L2 backends span local disk, Redis/Valkey, Google Cloud Bigtable, Mooncake Store, InfiniStore, NIXL-based stores, and S3-compatible object storage [[19](#ref-19), [157](#ref-157)]. Each adapter can declare its own eviction policy (typically LRU against a configured `max_capacity_gb`) and can be marked `shared: true` so several LMCache instances treat one bucket or export as a common, deduplicated pool [[156](#ref-156)].

NVIDIA Dynamo's KV Block Manager (KVBM) formalizes the same idea as a four-tier hierarchy — G1 (GPU-resident blocks), G2 (pinned host memory), G3 (local SSD, optionally via GPUDirect Storage/GDS), and G4 (remote or object storage, which KVBM treats as an opaque blob store) — with independent offload policies (`pass_all`, `presence`, or a least-frequently-used `presence_lfu`) gating each G1→G2→G3 transition [[18](#ref-18), [158](#ref-158)]. KVBM's own configuration reference documents an explicit sizing rule of thumb: the host (G2) cache should be sized above the engine's GPU KV cache and the disk (G3) cache above the host cache, or the system "churns" — offloading blocks it will immediately need back [[158](#ref-158)]. LMCache's cascading L1/L2 architecture (below) implies the same constraint — an L2 tier undersized relative to L1's working set would force the same evict-and-refetch cycle — but its own documentation does not state a sizing guideline this explicitly.

### A third software tier: SGLang's HiCache

SGLang's HiCache generalizes RadixAttention (the section on cross-request reuse, elsewhere in this whitepaper) the same way LMCache and KVBM generalize vLLM's paged allocator: it organizes GPU memory as L1, host memory as L2, and a pluggable distributed store — Mooncake, DeepSeek's 3FS, NIXL, or AIBrix KVCache — as a shared L3, modeled explicitly on a CPU cache hierarchy [[159](#ref-159)]. Unlike LMCache's or KVBM's host tier, HiCache's L1 and L2 are both instance-private by design: `--hicache-size` sizes the host memory pool per rank (so `--hicache-size 30` on 8 ranks yields 240 GB total, not 30 GB), and only the L3 tier is shared across inference instances in the cluster [[159](#ref-159)]. A "HiRadixTree" extends RadixAttention's radix tree with per-node location metadata, tracking whether a span of tokens currently lives in L1, L2, L3, or several tiers at once [[159](#ref-159)]. SGLang's own launch benchmarks reported up to 6x throughput and up to 80% lower TTFT from enabling HiCache on long-context and multi-turn workloads, and the same post cites two independent production deployments: Novita AI's coding-agent traffic on Qwen3-Coder-480B, where pairing HiCache with DeepSeek's 3FS store cut average TTFT 56%, doubled throughput, and raised cache hit rate from 40% to 80%; and Ant Group's DeepSeek-R1-671B deployment under PD-disaggregated serving, where HiCache-plus-Mooncake cut TTFT 84% on cache hits versus full recompute (vendor- and partner-reported, not independently reproduced) [[160](#ref-160)]. HiCache's own documentation frames LMCache not as one of its L3 backends but as a separate, mutually exclusive alternative: the `--enable-lmcache` flag swaps out HiCache's own radix-tree caching for LMCache's KV-cache management entirely, rather than plugging LMCache in underneath it — the two projects implement the same three-tier idea independently rather than one delegating to the other [[159](#ref-159)].

### Hardware tiering: GDS, NVMe-oF, CXL, and NVIDIA's storage push

GPUDirect Storage (GDS) is the mechanism nearly every vendor below relies on: it lets an NVMe drive or NIC DMA directly into GPU memory, skipping the CPU bounce buffer, and NVIDIA's own guidance reports roughly 25 GB/s achievable per PCIe Gen4 CPU tree (higher on Gen5/BlueField-3-class fabrics), with dynamic routing support for DDN-EXAScaler, VAST-NFS and WekaFS specifically [[161](#ref-161), [162](#ref-162)]. NVMe over Fabrics (NVMe-oF) extends the same command set across a network fabric; its authors' original design goal was no more than 10 microseconds of added latency over a local drive [[163](#ref-163)] — a target real deployments approach but do not uniformly hit, since fabric hardware, software stack, and queue depth all matter. CXL (Compute Express Link) memory pooling moved from adjacent to deployed during 2026. Microsoft's Pond estimates CXL-pooled DRAM adds only 70–90 ns over same-NUMA-node DRAM for an 8–16-socket pool (rising above 180 ns at rack scale) [[164](#ref-164)] — cheap enough to be a plausible G2 extension — and, as of September 2026, that estimate is no longer purely theoretical. Penguin Solutions' MemoryAI KV cache server, announced at GTC in March 2026, is billed by the vendor as "the industry's first production-ready KV cache server that utilizes CXL memory technology": 3 TB of DDR5 paired with up to eight 1 TB CXL add-in cards for up to 11 TB of pooled memory, marketed as compatible with NVIDIA Dynamo (vendor-reported) [[165](#ref-165)]. Samsung's own white paper reports evaluating its CMM-D CXL memory modules — aggregated behind a CXL switch into a 1 TB pool — as an LMCache backend for vLLM on RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPUs: a single-GPU configuration matched DRAM-backend performance, and an eight-GPU configuration retained roughly 92% of DRAM's throughput while sustaining a KV-cache footprint DRAM alone could not hold without recompute-driven degradation (vendor-reported) [[166](#ref-166)]. Marvell's Structera S CXL-switch family targets the same workload at rack scale — up to 48 TB of shared memory and 4 TB/s aggregate bandwidth per switch, sampling to customers in Q3 2026 — and the company reports an 8-GPU, 16 TB pooled-memory demonstration reaching 4.8x higher inference throughput and an 82.7% lower time-to-first-token than the same GPUs without the pool (vendor-reported) [[167](#ref-167)]. Astera Labs frames the same shift explicitly as a TCO argument: its Leo CXL memory controllers, deployable either inside a GPU server or in a shared, RDMA-reachable KV-cache appliance (demonstrated jointly with Penguin Solutions), are reported to lift GPU utilization 75% and inference throughput 2x by keeping more KV cache one hop away from HBM rather than on slower NVMe or across the network [[168](#ref-168)]. As with every other row in the vendor table below, none of these figures is independently reproduced outside the vendor's own lab — but the categorical claim that CXL has no announced KV-cache product no longer holds.

NVIDIA itself entered the storage-hardware market directly at CES 2026 with BlueField-4 and the Inference Context Memory Storage Platform, claiming up to 5x tokens/second and 5x power efficiency versus "traditional storage" [[20](#ref-20)]. At GTC 2026 this was detailed as CMX, adding a new "G3.5" Ethernet-attached flash tier — explicitly non-durable, since KV cache is treated as "transient, derived, and recomputable if lost" — bridging GPU/host memory and existing shared storage under the STX reference architecture [[169](#ref-169), [170](#ref-170)]. General availability across a long partner list is set for the second half of 2026; none of the headline multipliers above are independently reproduced in public benchmarks as of this writing.

### The vendor landscape

| Vendor | Product / mechanism | Vendor-reported result | Setup |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| WEKA | Augmented Memory Grid (NeuralMesh "token warehouse"), GDS+RDMA | 20x TTFT at 128K tokens; 308 GB/s single-client GDSIO read | H100 ×8, 72-NVMe 8-host cluster, OCI [[171](#ref-171), [172](#ref-172)] |
| VAST Data | AI OS on BlueField-4 / VUA; S3- and Azure-blob-compatible G3/G4 tiers via Dynamo 1.0 | 20x TTFT, ~90% less GPU compute; separately, 9x TTFT / 9.7x throughput | Dynamo 1.0 test: Llama-3 405B, 128K context, 8× Hopper GPUs, 2×100 Gb/s network; separately: AMD Instinct MI355X, NFSv3-over-RDMA, 800 Gbps, GPT-oss-120B, TP=1 [[173](#ref-173), [174](#ref-174)] |
| DDN | Infinia distributed KV-cache fabric; NIXL plugin shipped in official package | 27x (Jul 2025); 55x (Jun 2026); 18x (Jul 2026) | Setup undisclosed or varies across posts [[175](#ref-175), [176](#ref-176), [177](#ref-177), [178](#ref-178)] |
| Everpure (formerly Pure Storage [[179](#ref-179)]) | Key Value Accelerator (KVA) 1.0 on FlashBlade / FlashBlade//EXA | 20x TTFT (KVA+Dynamo); >10 TB/s single-namespace read (EXA) | [[180](#ref-180), [181](#ref-181)] |
| Hammerspace | Tier 0 (GPU-server-local NVMe joined into a global NFS/pNFS/SMB/S3 namespace) | 2.5–10x vs. IB/Ethernet transfer; 31–124x vs. separate cloud storage instances | [[182](#ref-182)] |
| Dell | PowerScale (file, GDS) / ObjectScale (object, RDMA-S3, upstreamed NIXL OBJ plugin) / Project Lightning | 19x TTFT at 131K tokens; up to 5.3x throughput | 4× H100, TP=4, vLLM+LMCache+NIXL [[183](#ref-183), [184](#ref-184), [185](#ref-185)] |
| NetApp | ONTAP S3 and pNFS-over-RDMA via LMCache's Remote FS plugin | up to 99% lower TTFT vs. no offload; 86% lower vs. CPU-only offload | Two load-balanced vLLM instances, shared bucket/volume [[186](#ref-186)] |
| Scality | ADI "Extreme" GPUDirect tier + native AI API and S3-over-RDMA via a NIXL connector | 166 ms restore vs. 102 ms DRAM vs. 2.3–3 s recompute (14K tok.) | Gemma-3-27B, six-node ADI 1.0 GA cluster [[187](#ref-187)] |

The most workload-realistic public test so far is Nebius and WEKA's September 2026 soak test: an eight-hour replay of captured agentic coding sessions (a 512-session active pool drawn from more than 5,000 sessions, some contexts approaching 900,000 tokens) against DeepSeek-V4-Pro in NVFP4 on one eight-GPU HGX B300 node, comparing HBM-only caching with WEKA Augmented Memory Grid pooling local NVMe across an eight-node cluster over GPUDirect RDMA. With the shared tier the node served 2.4× the requests (55,694 vs. 23,015), 3.7× the output tokens per GPU-hour (169,000 vs. 46,000) and a 93% cache hit rate against 40%, with p50 TTFT of 1.05 s against 5.68 s; the HBM-only run also degraded over the eight hours as its working set outgrew GPU memory [[11](#ref-11)]. It is still a vendor-run test with one model and one serving stack, but it replays real multi-turn traffic rather than synthetic single prompts, which is the condition under which a shared tier matters.

Every number above is the vendor's own, under a setup the vendor chose; DDN's own multiplier moves from 27x to 55x to 18x across three press releases within twelve months — and even the metric named shifts across those releases, from "KV cache loading performance" in the first two to the broader "faster inference" in the third — which is reason enough to treat any single "up to Nx" figure as a ceiling, not a typical, reproducible measurement.

### Object storage as a KV tier

Object storage is architecturally the most attractive KV tier — one flat, shared namespace reachable from every node — and the most exposed to overhead that has nothing to do with raw bandwidth. LMCache and Dynamo's KVBM both key objects by chunk (LMCache's default chunk size is 256 tokens; KVBM's G4 blocks are whatever size the engine pages) [[157](#ref-157), [158](#ref-158)]; for a 70B-class model this puts a single object in the tens of megabytes — large enough to amortize per-request overhead, but coarse enough that a partial-block match still forces a full re-fetch or recompute. Both LMCache's S3 backend and vLLM's own prefix cache identify a block by a hash chained from the previous block's hash and its own token content [[157](#ref-157), [101](#ref-101)] — the same content-addressing scheme used for cross-request reuse elsewhere in this whitepaper — which means a KV object's key is a deterministic function of tenant-visible content unless a tenant-specific salt is mixed in, precisely the isolation gap vLLM's `cache_salt` option was added to close [[101](#ref-101)]. Scality's own characterization of plain S3 is blunt: the data path is fast, but "the overhead in S3 is its control path — request signing, authorization, plus metadata lookups" [[187](#ref-187)], which is why every vendor above pairs the S3 *API* with an RDMA or GPUDirect *data path* rather than shipping bytes over ordinary HTTP. Where that pairing is done, the storage software is often not the bottleneck at all: Scality's own lab test sustained 87% of its test network's measured ceiling while the storage nodes stayed roughly 96% idle [[187](#ref-187)]. TTL and eviction are handled per-tier rather than globally — LMCache evicts by LRU against a configured capacity watermark [[156](#ref-156)], KVBM by configurable per-transition policies [[158](#ref-158)], and Everpure's KVA groups entries into hourly time bins with a distributed clock-sync step so a multi-host fleet agrees on one expiry boundary [[180](#ref-180)]. Multi-tenancy is the least settled piece: in a separate VAST Data thought-leadership post, the company's Director of AI Architecture states plainly that once KV cache "leaves the GPU, it contains sensitive user data and is vulnerable to manipulation or reverse engineering by attackers" [[188](#ref-188)] — a shared, content-addressed, cross-tenant store is exactly the design that needs namespace isolation, not merely the capacity to hold everyone's cache.

### A worked break-even: fetch versus recompute

This section's break-even follows the same logic Cake, an ICML 2025 system, frames explicitly for single-node deployments — representative bandwidth/capacity figures of roughly 2 TB/s and 80 GB for GPU memory, roughly 25 GB/s and 1.8 TB for CPU memory, and 0.5–4 GB/s and 26 TB for local/remote disk — and Cake in turn cites AttentionStore's production finding that roughly 80% of KV-cache hits in a multi-turn-conversation workload land on the slowest, disk tier: exactly the regime in which the recompute-vs-load choice is least obvious, and the reason a worked break-even calculation is worth doing at all rather than assuming hits always land somewhere fast [[126](#ref-126), [127](#ref-127)]. The following applies the same trade-off at cross-node scale, using this section's own vendor bandwidth figures.

Take Llama-3-70B (80 layers, $d_{\text{model}} = 8192$, 8 KV heads, head dimension 128, GQA) [[1](#ref-1)] and a 100,000-token shared prefix. FP16 KV cache per token is $2 \times 80 \times 8 \times 128 \times 2\text{ B} = 327{,}680$ B, so the prefix occupies $\approx 32.8$ GB. Its FP16 weights alone are $\approx 140$ GB, so no single 80 GB H100 can even hold this model. Parallelism regimes vary across the vendor table above: Dell's own Llama-3.3-70B benchmark uses tensor parallelism (TP=4 is typical for a 70B-class model on 80 GB GPUs) [[184](#ref-184)]; VAST's AMD test instead ran the larger-parameter-count GPT-oss-120B at TP=1 on a single MI355X GPU, whose 288 GB of HBM3E holds it without partitioning [[174](#ref-174), [189](#ref-189)]; and Scality's own benchmark used four models of 4–27B parameters on a single GPU with no NVLink interconnect at all [[187](#ref-187)] – so the "recompute" numbers compared below are not all measured under the same parallelism or model scale.

Recompute cost is commonly approximated as $2Pn$ FLOPs, a convention — used for model FLOPs utilization (MFU) accounting since PaLM/Chinchilla-era scaling work — that treats attention's own $O(n^2 d)$ term as a smaller, second-order cost safe to drop [[37](#ref-37)]. At $P = 70 \times 10^9$, $n = 10^5$, $2Pn \approx 1.4 \times 10^{16}$ FLOPs. That convention does not hold at this context length: for this architecture, the causally-masked attention term itself is $\approx 2 n^2 d_{\text{model}} L = 2 \times (10^5)^2 \times 8192 \times 80 \approx 1.3 \times 10^{16}$ FLOPs — comparable to, not smaller than, $2Pn$ — because $n = 10^5$ sits close to this model's own crossover point, $n \approx P / (d_{\text{model}} L) \approx 107{,}000$ tokens for causal attention (about half that, non-causal). On one H100 SXM (989.5 TFLOP/s dense BF16 peak) [[7](#ref-7)] at an illustrative 20–50% MFU, the $2Pn$-only estimate gives 28–71 s single-GPU, or roughly 7–18 s at TP=4 — in the same range as Dell's own measured recompute baseline of just over 17 s at a comparable 131K-token context [[183](#ref-183)]. Because the attention term is excluded, though, these figures are a lower bound on true recompute cost at this context length rather than a tight estimate — including it would roughly double the high end of the range — and the comparison to Dell's baseline, a different model at a different context length, should be read as order-of-magnitude only.

| Tier | B (achievable) | L (fixed) | Time to fetch 32.8 GB | Source |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| Host DRAM (PCIe H2D copy, Gen4 CPU-tree ceiling) | 25 GB/s (proxy: GDS's documented Gen4 CPU-tree bandwidth ceiling, not a GDS-specific measurement of this tier) | low μs | 1.3 s | [[162](#ref-162)] |
| Local NVMe (GDS/cuFile) | 10 GB/s (illustrative, between Micron's ~7 GB/s single-drive spec and DeepNVMe's ~6.5–13 GB/s single-drive/RAID-0 measurements) | ~75 μs | 3.3 s | [[117](#ref-117), [130](#ref-130)] |
| Networked flash (RDMA NVMe-oF-class shared storage) | 42–308 GB/s vendor-reported (bases differ — see note below) | sub-ms to low-ms | 1.6 s (at 20 GB/s, illustrative) | [[172](#ref-172), [187](#ref-187)] |
| Object storage, plain HTTP S3 (no RDMA) | ~2 GB/s (parallelized) | tens of ms | 16.4 s | [[190](#ref-190)] |

Two caveats on the table above. Figures follow the section's own $10^9$-byte convention throughout (GB, GB/s), not binary GiB; vendor-reported numbers are taken at face value and not independently re-derived for the two-convention difference, which could shift any one row's fetch time by roughly 7%. And the networked-flash row's range spans measurement bases that are not directly comparable: WEKA's 308 GB/s is a single-client GDSIO peak measured against an 8-way H100 host [[172](#ref-172)], while Scality's 42.47 GB/s is aggregate throughput across a six-node storage cluster, 87% of its measured 48.8 GB/s fabric ceiling [[187](#ref-187)]; DDN's own KV-cache press material discloses load times and a sub-millisecond latency claim but no bandwidth figure, so it is not cited for this row.

Every RDMA-class tier fetches faster than even the most aggressive (TP=4, 50% MFU) recompute estimate; the break-even shared-prefix length for these tiers works out to a few dozen tokens at most, since the fixed per-request latency — not raw bandwidth — is what has to be paid down, and it is paid down almost immediately. Plain HTTP object storage is a closer call: at a representative 35% MFU, its per-token transfer cost (~164 μs, at an assumed 2 GB/s parallelized connection) slightly exceeds TP=4's per-token compute cost (~101 μs), so fetch time grows faster than recompute time as the prefix lengthens and there is no length at which it wins; at a lower, still-plausible 20% MFU the ordering flips and plain S3 is faster for this 100,000-token example (though not for very short prefixes, where its latency floor dominates). This knife-edge is specific to the unaccelerated object-storage row — every RDMA-accelerated tier stays faster than recompute across the whole 20–50% MFU range — and it is exactly why Dell, DDN, and Scality all pair the S3 API with an RDMA path underneath rather than shipping large-scale KV fetch over ordinary HTTP.

### Limits

These figures describe cache hits only; a miss still costs a full recompute plus a write-back, and hit rate — not tier speed — usually dominates end-to-end economics [[17](#ref-17)]. Smaller or MHA-heavy models carry more KV bytes per parameter, narrowing or reversing the margins above. None of the public benchmarks cited here isolate GPU opportunity cost (a recomputing GPU is a GPU not serving other requests) from wall-clock latency, and none is independently reproduced outside the vendor's own lab. CXL pooling, well characterized in the systems literature, now has early production and engineering-sample KV-cache offerings (Penguin Solutions, Samsung, Marvell, Astera Labs) alongside the RDMA/GDS-based vendors above, though as with those vendors none of its performance claims is independently reproduced outside the vendor's own lab. And every multi-tenancy claim above is a vendor assertion about a mechanism — a shared, content-addressed, cross-node cache — whose isolation properties have not been independently audited.

## Evolution in Large-Scale Deployments

KV caching has moved from a single-GPU convenience to a distributed-systems problem in its own right. Early deployments (the GPT-2 era) worked with short context windows and single-digit batch sizes, so a KV cache resident entirely in one GPU's memory was sufficient. Two changes since then have made that assumption obsolete: context windows have grown by roughly three orders of magnitude — from 1,024 tokens in the GPT-2 era [[191](#ref-191)] to over 1 million tokens at today's frontier — and serving has moved from single requests on single GPUs to thousands of concurrent sessions spread across multi-GPU, multi-node clusters. This section traces both threads through September 2026 and describes how KV cache management has had to keep pace — sharding across GPUs, pooling and swapping between memory tiers, and, increasingly, surviving hardware failure. The detailed mechanics of prefill/decode disaggregation, prefix-cache reuse, and multi-tier storage are treated in dedicated sections elsewhere in this whitepaper; here the focus is the trajectory, not the internals.

### Context windows: from 32K to the million-token era

By 2023, GPT-4 shipped a 32K-token context option and Claude reached 100K [[192](#ref-192), [193](#ref-193)] — already an order of magnitude beyond the GPT-2 era. Three years on, the frontier has moved again, though unevenly across vendors and model tiers:

| Model family | 2023 baseline | September 2026 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| OpenAI GPT | GPT-4: 32K tokens [[192](#ref-192)] | GPT-5 (Aug. 2025 launch): 400K total, 272K input + 128K output [[194](#ref-194)]; GPT-5.4: 1.05M-token window, 128K max output [[195](#ref-195)] |
| Anthropic Claude | Claude: 100K tokens [[193](#ref-193)] | Claude Opus 5 and Sonnet 5 (and, earlier, Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.6–4.8): 1M tokens; Claude Sonnet 4.5 remains at 200K [[196](#ref-196)] |
| Google Gemini | *(no directly comparable 2023 release)* | Gemini 3.1 Pro: 1,048,576 input tokens, 65,536 output tokens [[197](#ref-197)] |

Two qualifications matter here — both are treated at greater length elsewhere in this whitepaper (see Section: Workload Trends Driving KV Demand (2024–2026) for the fuller per-vendor breakdown, including Llama 4, GPT-4.1 and Qwen2.5-1M, and the "advertised vs. effective context" critique). First, growth was not monotonic even within one vendor's own lineup: OpenAI's initial GPT-5 release in August 2025 held at 400K tokens, and only the later GPT-5.4 release reached the 1M-token class [[194](#ref-194), [195](#ref-195)] — and vendors continue to ship smaller, cheaper model tiers at the older 200K-token size alongside their flagship [[196](#ref-196)]. Second, "context window" as marketed is an input/output token budget, not a guarantee of usable accuracy at that length: Anthropic's own documentation states that retrieval and reasoning accuracy degrade as token count grows, a phenomenon it calls context rot, well before the nominal window fills [[196](#ref-196)]. Research systems have pushed further still: HeadInfer's head-wise CPU offloading — keeping only a subset of attention heads' KV cache on GPU at any time and computing the rest from CPU-resident data — reports its headline capacity result as 4-million-token decoding for an 8B model on a single 24 GB consumer GPU; a separate, more conservative roofline analysis in the same paper targets a 1-million-token sequence and reports cutting that sequence's on-GPU KV cache from 128 GB to 1 GB (total GPU memory from 207 GB to 17 GB) for the same model [[50](#ref-50)] (see Section: Extending KV Cache Beyond GPU Memory (CPU Offloading & NVMe) for the full mechanism). It is a research offload technique evaluated on a narrow model/hardware pairing, not a production-serving figure, and a useful reminder that "context length achievable in a paper" and "context length served at scale, for every concurrent user" are different claims. Every one of these increases multiplies KV cache size directly, since cache size per request scales linearly with sequence length; a service offering a 1M-token window must provision KV cache capacity an order of magnitude larger than one offering 100K, for every concurrent long-context session.

### Sharding the KV cache across GPUs

When a model does not fit on one GPU, tensor parallelism partitions each transformer layer's weight matrices across GPUs, and the KV cache is partitioned along with them. In the scheme introduced by Megatron-LM, the query, key and value projections are split column-wise so that the matrix multiply for each attention head executes entirely on one GPU; each GPU therefore holds and updates the K/V state only for the attention heads assigned to it, with no cross-GPU communication needed until the output projection's single all-reduce [[198](#ref-198)]. This is genuine per-GPU sharding of the cache, not replication — under this scheme, and unlike duplicated weights such as layer-norm parameters, each GPU's slice of the KV cache is unique.

That sharding has a hard limit the original scheme does not surface. Grouped-query, multi-query and multi-head-latent attention variants (see "Shrinking the Cache at the Source") use far fewer KV heads than query heads — sometimes a single shared KV head, as in multi-head latent attention. Once tensor-parallel width exceeds the number of KV heads, there is nothing left to shard: current serving engines instead replicate the full KV cache onto every GPU beyond that point, which wastes HBM and caps the batch size a cluster can serve at long context lengths [[199](#ref-199)]. NVIDIA's Helix Parallelism, published in mid-2025, targets exactly this ceiling by decoupling the parallelism strategy used for attention from the one used for the feed-forward network within the same layer: during attention, tensor-parallel width is kept at or below the KV-head count and any remaining GPUs instead shard the KV cache along the sequence dimension (KV parallelism); the same GPU pool is then re-provisioned at full tensor/expert-parallel width for the feed-forward computation [[199](#ref-199)]. On simulated million-token-plus decoding of DeepSeek-R1 (which uses a single effective KV head) on NVIDIA's GB200 NVL72 hardware at FP4 precision, the authors — NVIDIA researchers — report up to 32× more concurrent users at a fixed latency budget (and up to 1.5× lower token-to-token latency at low concurrency) compared to prior sharding strategies; for a dense 405B-parameter model with 8 KV heads (Llama-405B-class GQA), the reported gain is a narrower 4× throughput and batch-capacity improvement at 1.13× better peak interactivity [[199](#ref-199)]. These are vendor-affiliated, hardware- and precision-specific simulation results on models that do not yet natively ship million-token contexts, not a general production-deployment claim, but they mark where sharding strategy is heading as KV-head-sparse architectures meet ever-longer contexts. The same underlying idea — shard the KV cache along the sequence axis, not only the head axis — appears more generally as context parallelism. Training stacks such as Megatron-LM support it as a first-class parallelism dimension alongside tensor and pipeline parallelism, exchanging KV shards between GPUs via all-gather (forward pass) and reduce-scatter (backward pass) in a ring topology [[200](#ref-200)]; the same sequence-sharding idea has since been carried over to the decode phase of inference serving specifically — NVIDIA's TensorRT-LLM ships Helix as, in its own words, "a context parallelism (CP) technique for the decode/generation phase of LLM inference" [[201](#ref-201)].

### Disaggregation, pooling and swapping

Beyond sharding a single model's cache, large deployments increasingly split *stages* of a request onto different hardware pools — prefill on compute-optimized GPUs, decode on memory-bandwidth-optimized GPUs [[15](#ref-15), [16](#ref-16), [133](#ref-133)] — and combine that split with cache-aware request routing and cross-tier offload as one coordinated system rather than separate point techniques, as in NVIDIA's Dynamo serving stack [[202](#ref-202)]. That disaggregation, and the prefix-cache-aware routing layered on top of it, are covered in their own section of this whitepaper; the relevant point here is that KV cache placement, not model placement, has become the primary scheduling variable in these systems.

Within a single deployment, memory pooling addresses a related inefficiency: pre-allocating each session's maximum possible KV cache wastes capacity, since most sessions never reach it. vLLM's page-level pool reclaims this by allocating cache blocks on demand rather than per session [[8](#ref-8)]. The *DéjàVu* system (ICML 2024) goes further for **pipeline-parallel** deployments specifically: pipeline parallelism creates idle "bubbles" because prompt processing and token generation have very different per-step latency, and DéjàVu's *microbatch swapping* moves a microbatch's KV cache to CPU memory while that microbatch is not actively being computed on, freeing GPU memory for other microbatches in flight [[203](#ref-203)]. The authors report this swapping alone improves throughput by up to 1.8× by allowing larger batch sizes, and up to 2× overall against FasterTransformer when combined with their prompt/token disaggregation [[203](#ref-203)] — figures specific to the pipeline-parallel serving setups and cloud deployments the paper evaluates, not a general claim about swapping under every parallelism scheme.

### Fault tolerance

DéjàVu also replicates KV cache state to persistent storage or remote CPU memory as it streams, so that a GPU failure does not require regenerating a request from scratch: on failure, the system restores the most recently replicated state to a healthy GPU and resumes generation from the last completed token rather than the beginning of the sequence [[203](#ref-203)].
