Under injected failures, the authors report a 1.54× reduction in microbatch latency relative to a non-fault-tolerant baseline [[203](#ref-203)]. This is narrower than "KV caches are now routinely persisted for fault tolerance in production" — it is one research system's mechanism, evaluated in pipeline-parallel deployments, and its cost (continuously streaming replica writes) is only worth paying when failure rates and request lengths make losing in-flight generation expensive enough to justify it.

### A widening memory hierarchy

The other axis of change is which physical memory a KV cache is allowed to live in at all. Micron's own September 2026 analysis of KV cache growth frames current serving infrastructure around a five-tier hierarchy running from GPU HBM (10–100 GB per GPU at 1–20 TB/s, millisecond-to-second residency for active token generation), through DDR5 main memory (100–500 GB/s, seconds to minutes) and disaggregated expansion memory (100–200 GB/s, minutes to hours), down to NVMe SSDs (10s–100s of TB per GPU at 20–200 GB/s, hours-to-days residency for multi-turn agentic workflows) and networked object storage for cache that must persist across the turns of a long agentic session — organized explicitly by how long a piece of KV cache needs to stay reachable rather than by capacity alone. Set against that same tier table, the vendor's claim of validating NVMe-based KV cache offload "with zero throughput penalty" across hundreds of test configurations for agentic workloads is plausible chiefly because that offload targets the hours-to-days residency tier: a reload that happens once per multi-turn boundary and is overlapped with inter-turn "think" or tool-call time looks very different from one that happens on every decode step, where the 50–1000× bandwidth gap between HBM and NVMe shown in the same table would otherwise dominate. Absent a disclosed hardware configuration or baseline, this remains an unverified vendor claim (vendor-reported) [[204](#ref-204)]. On the HBM tier specifically, Micron's HBM4 — in volume production for NVIDIA's Vera Rubin platform since the first quarter of 2026 — delivers over 2.8 TB/s of bandwidth per stack, more than double the prior HBM3E generation, marketed explicitly against "context windows spanning millions of tokens" (vendor-reported) [[205](#ref-205), [206](#ref-206)]. Whether that generational bandwidth growth keeps pace with KV cache demand growth is a separate, open question this section does not attempt to settle; the narrower point is that memory vendors are now designing and marketing hardware generations explicitly around KV cache residency and reuse, which was not true in the sub-100K-token era this section opened with.

## Workload Trends Driving KV Demand (2024–2026)

The preceding sections treat KV cache size largely as a function of sequence length, batch size, and model width. Between 2024 and 2026, several shifts in how models are built and used have pushed on all three variables at once: context windows measured in millions rather than thousands of tokens; reasoning models that spend tens of thousands of tokens deciding what to say before saying it; agentic and coding workloads that re-send large, mostly static contexts on every step rather than issuing one-shot prompts; and new decoding and routing schemes — speculative decoding variants and mixture-of-experts (MoE) — that change *how* KV cache is produced and shared without necessarily shrinking it. This section quantifies each trend and, where the evidence is mixed, says so.

### Long context windows: from kilotokens to megatokens

By September 2026, a 1-million-token context window is a mainstream, not exotic, spec among frontier and open models. Google reports several Gemini models with windows of "1 million or more" tokens [[207](#ref-207)]. Meta's Llama 4 Maverick (17B active / 400B total, MoE) ships a 1M-token window and Llama 4 Scout (17B active / 109B total) an "industry-leading" 10M [[208](#ref-208)]. DeepSeek-V4-Pro and V4-Flash (April 2026) also ship open weights with a 1M-token window, and were designed around it: DeepSeek reports that V4-Pro's compressed hybrid attention needs about 10% of DeepSeek-V3.2's KV cache at 1M tokens [[3](#ref-3)]. Advertised context length is not effective context, however: Meta published no long-context evaluation of Scout beyond needle-in-a-haystack retrieval, and independent commentary at launch noted that harder long-context evaluations (RULER, NoLiMa) were absent and that early third-party results "range from medium to bad and confusing" [[209](#ref-209)]; as of September 2026, Scout scores 27.3% on the independent Fiction.LiveBench long-context comprehension benchmark at 120K tokens, ranking 32nd of 35 tested model configurations — well behind several models with far smaller advertised windows [[210](#ref-210)]. OpenAI's GPT-4.1 extended context to "up to 1 million tokens" [[211](#ref-211)]. Alibaba's Qwen2.5-1M extends the open-weight 7B and 14B Qwen2.5 models to 1M tokens via long-context pretraining and length extrapolation, and states the deployment cost directly: serving the 1M window needs at least 120 GB of VRAM (total, across GPUs) for the 7B model and 320 GB for the 14B model [[212](#ref-212)]. Anthropic documents several current Claude tiers with a 1M-token window at standard pricing, with everything in a request — system prompt, tool definitions, tool results, and extended-thinking output — counted against it [[196](#ref-196)].

| Model family | Context window | Source |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| Gemini (Google) | ≥1,000,000 tokens | [[207](#ref-207)] |
| Llama 4 Maverick (Meta, MoE, 17B active / 400B total) | 1,000,000 tokens | [[208](#ref-208)] |
| Llama 4 Scout (Meta, MoE, 17B active / 109B total) | 10,000,000 tokens | [[208](#ref-208)] |
| GPT-4.1 (OpenAI) | 1,000,000 tokens | [[211](#ref-211)] |
| Qwen2.5-1M (Alibaba, open-weight, dense 7B/14B) | 1,000,000 tokens | [[212](#ref-212)] |
| Claude (Anthropic, several current tiers) | up to 1,000,000 tokens | [[196](#ref-196)] |

For the historical baseline this growth is measured against: GPT-2 shipped a 1,024-token context [[191](#ref-191)], and by 2023 GPT-4 had reached 32K tokens and Claude 100K [[192](#ref-192), [193](#ref-193)] — already an order of magnitude beyond GPT-2. OpenAI's own progression since has been non-monotonic: GPT-4.1 reached the 1M-token class in 2025 (above), but OpenAI's initial GPT-5 release that August held at a smaller 400K tokens (272K input + 128K output), and only the later GPT-5.4 release returned to the 1M-token class (1.05M tokens, 128K max output) [[194](#ref-194), [195](#ref-195)]. Anthropic's current lineup shows the same unevenness across tiers: Claude Opus 5 and Sonnet 5 (and, earlier, Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.6–4.8) reach 1M tokens, but Claude Sonnet 4.5 remains at 200K [[196](#ref-196)]. Google's Gemini 3.1 Pro documents an exact 1,048,576-input/65,536-output-token budget [[197](#ref-197)]. A window's advertised size is not a guarantee of usable accuracy at that length, either: Anthropic's own documentation describes retrieval and reasoning accuracy degrading as token count grows — a phenomenon it calls context rot — well before the nominal window fills [[196](#ref-196)], the same caution this section's Fiction.LiveBench discussion of Llama 4 Scout illustrates concretely. Research offload systems have pushed further still: HeadInfer's head-wise CPU offloading reports a headline capacity of 4-million-token decoding for an 8B model on a single 24 GB consumer GPU (with a more conservative roofline analysis, for a 1-million-token sequence, cutting on-GPU KV cache from 128 GB to 1 GB and total GPU memory from 207 GB to 17 GB) [[50](#ref-50)] — a research offload result on one narrow model/hardware pairing, not a production-serving figure; see Section: Extending KV Cache Beyond GPU Memory (CPU Offloading & NVMe) for the full mechanism.

The KV cost is easy to size. Using Mixtral's published GQA configuration purely as an illustrative example — 32 layers, 8 KV heads, head dimension 128 [[213](#ref-213)] — the cache holds $2 \times 8 \times 128 \times 32 = 65{,}536$ elements per token, about 128 KiB at bf16, or roughly 122 GiB for one sequence's K/V tensors alone at 1,000,000 tokens and batch size 1, before weights or activations. Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M's real, published attention configuration is considerably smaller — 28 layers, 4 KV heads, head dimension 128 [[212](#ref-212)] — giving $2 \times 4 \times 128 \times 28 = 28{,}672$ elements per token (56 KiB at bf16) and roughly 53 GiB of KV cache alone at 1,000,000 tokens: about 48% of the 120 GB total, multi-GPU VRAM budget Qwen's own deployment guidance specifies for serving that model's 1M window across up to four GPUs in tensor parallel [[212](#ref-212)]. That 120 GB figure covers model weights, chunked-prefill activation buffers, and the MInference sparse-attention structures needed to make 1M-token prefill tractable, not KV cache alone [[212](#ref-212)] — a reminder that 1M-context serving depends on the offload, quantization, and paging techniques covered elsewhere in this paper, not on raw HBM capacity.

### Reasoning models: decode-heavy generation grows the cache long after prefill ends

Reasoning models generate a chain-of-thought before (or instead of) a direct answer, and that chain occupies the KV cache like any other generated token, visible or not. During RL training, DeepSeek-R1(-Zero) rollouts were capped at 32,768 tokens, raised to 65,536 tokens after step 8.2k [[214](#ref-214)], and its authors report that average response length grew on its own during training as the model "naturally learns to solve reasoning tasks with more thinking time" [[214](#ref-214)]. OpenAI documents the same mechanic for deployed models: reasoning tokens are hidden from the API response but "still occupy space in the model's context window and are billed as output tokens," ranging "from a few hundred to tens of thousands" per query, with a recommended 25,000-token reservation [[215](#ref-215)]. MLCommons' MLPerf Inference v5.1 benchmark sets a maximum output sequence length of 20,000 tokens for DeepSeek-R1 — "the highest yet in the MLPerf Inference benchmark suite" — and reports mean input/output lengths of 800 and 3,880 tokens on its curated reasoning dataset [[216](#ref-216)]. For a similarly short visible answer, then, a reasoning model's decode-phase KV cache can be many times larger than a non-reasoning model's, since the trace must stay resident to condition every later token.

This growth is not unconditionally beneficial. LongReasonArena, scaling required reasoning length toward 1M tokens, finds accuracy declining log-linearly with the number of reasoning steps required, with DeepSeek-R1 reaching only 7.5% accuracy at its hardest tier [[217](#ref-217)]. Longer chains-of-thought can buy diminishing — even negligible — returns while still paying their full KV cost, so provisioning should follow measured output-length distributions, not worst-case token budgets alone.

### Agentic and coding workloads: long, repeatedly re-sent context and tool output

Agentic and coding workloads differ from a single long prompt: a large, mostly static context (system prompt, tool schemas, accumulated history) plus fresh tool output is re-sent on *every* step of a multi-step loop. Anthropic's account of building Claude Code states that "we build our entire harness around prompt caching," running alerts — and declaring incidents — on cache hit rate, and orders every request static-first (system prompt and tools, then project memory, then session context, then conversation) to keep the shared, cacheable prefix as large as possible; changing the tool set mid-conversation "invalidates the cache for the entire conversation" [[218](#ref-218)]. Anthropic's original prompt-caching release quantifies the payoff: a cached 100,000-token prompt cuts time-to-first-token from 11.5 s to 2.4 s (−79%) and cost by up to 90%; a ten-turn conversation with a long system prompt cuts TTFT from ~10 s to ~2.5 s (−75%) [[219](#ref-219)].

Accumulated context has grown past what agent frameworks were sized for. SWE-agent originally budgeted 128K–200K-token windows as "sufficient ... to interact for several turns" [[220](#ref-220)]. A 2026 study's own corpus of long-horizon coding-agent trajectories shows an average of 87.4 steps per trajectory and 13,044 tokens of context added per step (median 10,875; max 65,536) [[221](#ref-221)], and the same authors diagnose "context explosion" as the default failure mode of the unmanaged, append-only context handling their method is built to replace. A dataset of 80,036 SWE-agent trajectories shows the same asymmetry seen with reasoning length: resolved runs average 8,352 tokens of accumulated context, unresolved ones — which keep running rather than submitting — average 15,241 [[222](#ref-222)]; SWE-agent's own analysis finds 93.0% of resolved instances submit before exhausting their budget, versus 69.0% overall [[220](#ref-220)] — agents succeed quickly and fail slowly, and the slow failures drive peak KV demand. This is partly a design artifact rather than an intrinsic cost: a compression-aware agent in the same 2026 study holds context "stable at approximately 35k tokens" across hundreds of rounds where an append-only baseline exhausts its window [[221](#ref-221)].

### Multi-turn chat: a smaller per-session footprint, multiplied by concurrency

Ordinary consumer chat traffic is comparatively modest: across one million real conversations, LMSYS-Chat-1M reports an average of 2.0 turns per session, 69.5 tokens per prompt, and 214.5 tokens per response [[223](#ref-223)] — a per-session KV footprint of tens of kilobytes, not gigabytes. Aggregate KV pressure on a serving fleet instead scales with concurrency times per-session footprint, and it is the long tail — reasoning, agentic, and long-document sessions — that sets peak memory demand, not the median chat turn; a fleet provisioned only for LMSYS-Chat-1M-like medians would be caught out by a mix that includes even a modest share of the 100K+-token sessions described above.

### Speculative decoding variants: verification breadth trades one KV cache for another

Classic speculative decoding [[131](#ref-131), [132](#ref-132)] runs a separate, smaller draft model that maintains its own KV cache alongside the target's, effectively doubling the number of caches per in-flight request. Newer variants draft from the target model itself instead. Medusa adds $K$ extra decoding heads onto the target backbone and verifies several candidates at once with tree-structured attention, reaching 2.2× (frozen backbone) to 2.3–2.8× (jointly fine-tuned) speedup with no separate draft model or cache [[224](#ref-224)]. EAGLE-3 predicts tokens directly from fused low-, mid-, and high-layer features of the target model, reporting speedups up to 6.5× (1.4× over EAGLE-2) and a 38–40% throughput gain at batch size 64 in SGLang [[225](#ref-225)]. DeepSeek-V3 trains one extra sequential module for multi-token prediction (MTP); at inference it can be discarded or repurposed as a self-speculative draft, with an 85–90% acceptance rate for the second predicted token and a 1.8× tokens-per-second improvement [[57](#ref-57)].

The common thread: Medusa, EAGLE-3, and MTP replace a second full model's KV cache with a small auxiliary cache or nothing beyond the target's own, in exchange for wider — but transient — per-step attention while candidates are verified before rejected branches are pruned. The gains are not unconditional: EAGLE-3's authors note that "speculative sampling is often thought to reduce throughput at large batch sizes" [[225](#ref-225)], since decode is already closer to compute-bound at high occupancy, leaving less slack to exploit — the batch-64 result is not a batch-independent guarantee.

### Mixture-of-experts: sparse compute, but attention — and its cache — stays dense

An MoE layer sparsifies only the feed-forward sub-block; attention, and therefore the KV cache, still runs over every token regardless of which experts are routed to. KV cache size is set by attention configuration — layers, heads, head dimension — not by active-parameter count, and the two can be decoupled in either direction. Mixtral 8x7B illustrates one direction: 47B total parameters, 13B active per token, yet an attention stack (32 layers, 8 KV heads, head dimension 128) identical to the 7B-parameter dense Mistral model it is built from [[213](#ref-213)] — its KV cache per token is that of a 7B-class dense model despite 13B-class active compute.

The trend this section's title points to runs the other way: scaling attention depth/width to a large *total*-parameter budget while keeping active compute low via sparse routing. DeepSeek-V3 (671B total, 37B active) uses 61 attention layers and 128 heads — depth and width typical of a far larger dense model. Applying DeepSeek-V2's published standard-attention formula, $2 n_h d_h l$ [[2](#ref-2)], to DeepSeek-V3's released configuration (128 heads, 192-dimension key and 128-dimension value per head, 61 layers) gives roughly $128 \times 320 \times 61 \approx 2.5$ million elements per token for an equivalent standard cache, versus DeepSeek-V3's actual Multi-head Latent Attention (MLA) cache of $(d_c + d_h^{R}) \times l = (512+64) \times 61 = 35{,}136$ elements — about 71× smaller [[57](#ref-57)]. DeepSeek-V2 reports the same mechanism cutting KV cache 93.3% and lifting maximum throughput 5.76× versus the dense DeepSeek 67B it replaced (see Section: Shrinking the Cache at the Source: Attention Architectures for the MLA mechanism and this figure's measurement caveats) [[2](#ref-2)].

| Model | Total / active params | Attention config | KV cache per token (elements) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mixtral 8x7B [[213](#ref-213)] | 47B / 13B | 32 layers, 8 KV heads (GQA), $d_h$=128 | 65,536 |
| DeepSeek-V3, hypothetical standard MHA [[2](#ref-2)] | 671B / 37B | 61 layers, 128 heads, 320-dim K+V per head | ≈2,498,560 |
| DeepSeek-V3, actual MLA [[57](#ref-57), [2](#ref-2)] | 671B / 37B | 61 layers, latent rank 512 + rotary 64 | 35,136 |

This is architecture-dependent, not a universal law of MoE: Mixtral keeps KV cache small by reusing a small dense model's attention stack, while DeepSeek shows the opposite is possible when depth and width instead track the *total*-parameter budget. The latter pattern is why KV-compressing designs like MLA, not offload or paging alone, have become part of frontier MoE architecture — though MLA is not free: efficient serving requires purpose-built low-rank attention kernels rather than standard attention-kernel libraries, as DeepSeek's own FlashMLA kernels illustrate [[226](#ref-226)], an engineering cost additional to the memory-hierarchy techniques discussed elsewhere in this paper.

## Measuring and Pricing the Cache

The preceding sections argue that KV caching turns prefill compute into a one-time cost and decode into a memory-bandwidth problem. Whether that trade is worth making in a given deployment is empirical, not architectural: it depends on how much latency and throughput the cache buys under a realistic workload, and what that saved compute is worth in dollars. This section covers both — the metrics and tools used to measure cache effects, and the public pricing and hardware-cost data needed to turn a measured effect into a dollar figure.

### Metrics that separate cache effects from everything else

Four request-level metrics dominate LLM serving evaluation [[29](#ref-29), [31](#ref-31)]. **Time to first token (TTFT)** is the latency from request submission to the first streamed token, and it bundles queueing, network, and prefill compute — the last of which shrinks whenever a prefix is served from cache instead of recomputed. **Inter-token latency (ITL)**, also called time per output token (TPOT), is the average gap between successive output tokens during decode; vLLM computes it per request as $\text{TPOT} = (\text{e2e latency} - \text{TTFT}) / (\text{output tokens} - 1)$ [[227](#ref-227)]. **End-to-end latency** is $\text{TTFT} + \text{generation time}$ [[31](#ref-31)]. **Throughput** (tokens/s, aggregate or per-GPU) says how much work a system completes but says nothing about whether any individual request met a latency bound.

That gap motivates **goodput**: the request rate a system sustains while satisfying a joint latency SLO (e.g., TTFT ≤ 2 s *and* TPOT ≤ 50 ms), as opposed to raw throughput, which counts an over-deadline request the same as an on-time one [[16](#ref-16)]. vLLM's benchmark client operationalizes this directly — `--goodput ttft:2000 tpot:50` reports the fraction of requests meeting both bounds, distinct from `request_throughput` [[227](#ref-227)]. NVIDIA's AIPerf adds SLA-attainment analysis and Pareto-frontier plots of throughput-per-GPU against tokens-per-second-per-user, so the throughput/latency trade-off, not either number alone, is the reported result [[228](#ref-228)]. A cache that raises throughput while quietly missing more SLOs — say, by admitting more concurrent requests than the freed memory can serve at the target TPOT — is not a net win; goodput and SLO attainment are what should be reported, not tokens/s alone.

### Benchmark suites

**MLPerf Inference** is the closest thing to a standardized cross-vendor comparison. Its Server scenario enforces published p99 TTFT/TPOT bounds per model, while Offline measures unconstrained bulk throughput with no latency requirement — the two are not interchangeable, and citing an Offline tokens/s figure as evidence of interactive-serving capability is a category error [[33](#ref-33)]. The bounds have tightened as usage has shifted toward lower-latency, agentic workloads: Llama 2 70B's Conversational category kept its original 2,000 ms/200 ms TTFT/TPOT limits but gained a new Interactive category at 450 ms/40 ms (25 tok/s), while the newly added Llama 3.1 405B benchmark runs at 6,000 ms/175 ms (Server) and 4,500 ms/80 ms (Interactive) [[229](#ref-229), [33](#ref-33)].

| Model | Category | TTFT limit | TPOT limit |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| Llama 2 70B | Conversational | 2,000 ms | 200 ms |
| Llama 2 70B | Interactive | 450 ms | 40 ms |
| Llama 3.1 405B | Server | 6,000 ms | 175 ms |
| Llama 3.1 405B | Interactive | 4,500 ms | 80 ms |
| GPT-OSS-120B | Server | 3,000 ms | 80 ms |
| GPT-OSS-120B | Interactive | 2,000 ms | 20 ms |

*p99 latency constraints for representative MLPerf Inference LLM tasks, per the current rules document [[33](#ref-33)]. The Llama 2 70B Interactive category and the Llama 3.1 405B benchmark were introduced in MLPerf Inference v5.0 [[229](#ref-229)]; GPT-OSS-120B was introduced in v6.0 [[230](#ref-230)], whose announcement stated an Interactive TPOT bound of ≤15 ms — since relaxed to the ≤20 ms shown here in the current rules, an example of how these bounds are revised across rounds.*

A sibling MLPerf suite measures the storage layer feeding this cache directly rather than the serving engine's own latency bounds. **MLPerf Storage v3.0**, whose results MLCommons published in September 2026, added a dedicated KV Cache test alongside its existing training and checkpointing benchmarks: it simulates GPU→CPU→NVMe cache offload under simultaneous read (decode) and write (prefill) pressure across three fixed workloads (Llama-3.1-8B and Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct, varying user count and CPU-memory buffer size) and reports tokens/second alongside per-tier read/write bandwidth and p95 latency [[21](#ref-21), [231](#ref-231)]. First-round closed-division submissions span close to two orders of magnitude by configuration: Samsung's PM1763 PCIe Gen6 NVMe SSD sustained 813 tok/s (13.54 GiB/s read, 2.87 GiB/s write, concurrently) on the 8B workload, HPE's Cray Supercomputing Storage Systems K3000 reported over 8,700 tok/s and over 151 GiB/s aggregate across 16 clients, and Inspur's AS13000G7 reported 14,579 tok/s single-node throughput on the same 8B configuration [[232](#ref-232)]. As with the vendor KV-tier table elsewhere in this whitepaper, these figures depend heavily on client count, model, and configured cache-hit rate rather than reflecting one universal number — the benchmark's own documentation notes hit rate alone can be tuned from roughly 85–97% (ShareGPT-derived traffic) down to 5–15% (multi-turn and prefix caching both disabled) to stress different parts of the storage path [[231](#ref-231)].

MLPerf's serving-side rules are also being extended to stress KV-cache reuse and routing directly. A newly proposed **Agentic Inference** benchmark (previewed by MLCommons in July 2026) replays 613 real multi-turn agentic trajectories — agentic-coding traffic that grows context aggressively and stresses raw KV-cache capacity, and shared-prefix agentic-workflow traffic that stresses prefix-cache efficiency and routing locality instead — and builds this whitepaper's own benchmark-contamination warning (below, under Pitfalls) directly into its methodology: a stable per-trajectory session header lets a KV-aware router preserve cache locality across turns, while deterministic cache-salting permits valid within-trajectory reuse but blocks cross-trajectory contamination, rather than leaving cache-hit pollution as a pitfall for benchmark operators to avoid on their own [[233](#ref-233)].

**SemiAnalysis InferenceMAX** (now InferenceX) is an open-source, continuously refreshed benchmark across vLLM, SGLang, and TensorRT-LLM on current accelerators; its central metric is cost per million tokens as a function of interactivity (tokens/s/user), normalized by each system's total-cost-of-ownership per GPU-hour rather than list price, since a chip with lower throughput but lower TCO can still win on \$/Mtok [[234](#ref-234)]. **vLLM's `vllm bench serve`** and **SGLang's `bench_serving`** are the de facto client-side tools for both projects, reporting TTFT/TPOT/ITL/e2e-latency percentiles and, for vLLM, goodput against configurable SLOs; both document that a server's default-on prefix cache can silently contaminate repeated runs (see below) [[227](#ref-227), [235](#ref-235)]. **LMCache** and **NVIDIA Dynamo** ship KV-cache-specific benchmarks: LMCache's multi-turn benchmark isolates prefill acceleration under realistic shared-prefix conversation traces [[236](#ref-236)], while Dynamo's disaggregated-serving flow validates predicted TTFT/TPOT against measured values and its deployment walkthrough warns that KV transfer without RDMA degrades TTFT by roughly 40× (355 ms to 10+ seconds) in that illustrative configuration [[237](#ref-237)]; a separate, more detailed NVIDIA communication guide breaks the same failure mode down by transport and reports a wider, configuration-dependent range on different reference hardware — from +37 ms overhead with AWS EFA plus GPUDirect to +90–100 s under a TCP fallback (roughly 200–500×) — underscoring how strongly the multiplier depends on the specific interconnect rather than being a fixed constant [[238](#ref-238)]. **NVIDIA GenAI-Perf/AIPerf** is the general-purpose client for OpenAI-compatible endpoints, with concurrency sweeps, cache-hit-ratio sweeps, and goodput/SLA analysis built in [[228](#ref-228)].

### Pitfalls

Benchmark documentation increasingly warns about one specific, quantified failure mode: a deterministic synthetic-prompt generator (fixed seed) rerun against a long-lived server with prefix caching on by default silently replays cache-hit prompts. A reproduced case on vLLM's benchmark client — a DGX Spark GB10 running Qwen3-30B-A3B under vLLM 0.19.2rc1 — showed 49× lower mean TTFT, 34% lower TPOT, and 2.32× higher throughput at a 16K-token input length between a cold run and a cache-contaminated run of the identical configuration; re-measured cold across an ascending-concurrency sweep, the contamination was non-uniform rather than a fixed offset, inflating apparent TPOT by +59.3% at concurrency 4 and +76.0% at concurrency 8 before falling to +25.0% at concurrency 16, once that cell's ~1M input tokens had exceeded the KV cache and evicted most of the earlier shared prefixes [[22](#ref-22)]. A controlled H100 study found prefix reuse worth a real 5–6.5× TTFT reduction at an 8,192-token shared prefix, but also found that which serving runtime "wins" on tail latency flips by 2.4–9.2× depending purely on arrival pattern (burst vs. fixed-rate) at fixed concurrency — a single synthetic workload does not generalize across arrival patterns, model sizes, or hardware [[23](#ref-23)]. More generally, closed-loop (fixed-concurrency) tests report throughput at one operating point and hide where latency blows up, whereas open-loop (fixed-arrival-rate) tests reveal the saturation curve; a discarded warm-up period and enough samples for the reported percentile (roughly 1,000+ for p99) are both necessary for the numbers to mean anything [[239](#ref-239)]. Finally, a benchmark run entirely at one cache state — always warm or always cold — will not resemble production, where hit rate is a distribution: a blend of 70% warm (400 ms) / 30% cold (1.8 s) requests gives an unremarkable mean but a p95 dominated by the cold tail, exactly the SLO-relevant number — illustrated there for hosted-API prompt caching, but the same hit-rate-as-a-distribution argument applies equally to self-hosted prefix-cache benchmarking [[240](#ref-240)].

### Pricing the cache: cost per million tokens vs. hit rate

Public API pricing gives a lower bound on what a cache hit is worth, since it is what a customer would otherwise pay to recompute the same tokens. For a base (uncached) input price $P$ and a cached-input price $rP$ (so $r$ is the fraction of the base price a cache hit costs), the blended price per million input tokens at hit rate $h$ is:

$$P(h) = P\left[(1-h) + hr\right] = P\left[1 - h(1-r)\right]$$

As of September 2026, three major providers price cache reads at $r \approx 0.1$ (a 90% discount) on comparable mid-tier models: OpenAI's GPT-6 Sol (\$2.00 base / \$0.20 cached, short-context tier) [[107](#ref-107)], Anthropic's Claude Sonnet 5 (\$2.00 base / \$0.20 cache-read) [[12](#ref-12)], and Google's Gemini 3.8 Flash (\$0.75 base / \$0.075 cached, standard paid tier, pricing through December 31, 2026) [[109](#ref-109), [241](#ref-241)].

| Hit rate $h$ | OpenAI GPT-6 Sol | Anthropic Claude Sonnet 5 | Google Gemini 3.8 Flash |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 0% | \$2.00 | \$2.00 | \$0.75 |
| 50% | \$1.10 | \$1.10 | \$0.4125 |
| 90% | \$0.38 | \$0.38 | \$0.1425 |

*Blended cost per 1M input tokens, $P(h)=P[1-h(1-r)]$, $r=0.1$ for all three [[107](#ref-107), [12](#ref-12), [109](#ref-109)]. Google's per-token-storage caching surcharge is omitted from this table, as are all three vendors' cache-write charges (discussed below).*

Provider terms also vary in trigger mechanism, minimum cacheable prefix, and retention window, not only in $r$. As of 2026-09-27: Anthropic requires an explicit `cache_control` (or one automatic field) over a minimum 1,024-token prefix (512 tokens on Opus 5/5.5 and the Fable/Mythos 5.x tiers), with a 5-minute default TTL (free refresh on hit) or an opt-in 1-hour TTL at 2$\times$ the base write price; OpenAI's GPT-5.6-and-later tiers cache automatically above a strict 1,024-token minimum, with a TTL of 30 minutes or more via an explicit `ttl` parameter, refreshed on reuse (earlier tiers such as GPT-5.5 cached automatically with no write charge and a shorter 5–10-minute inactivity window, up to 24 hours extended); and Gemini's implicit caching (2.5+) is automatic and best-effort with no savings guarantee over a 2,048–4,096-token minimum, while its explicit, developer-created cache objects carry a caller-set TTL and a separate storage charge on top of the input rate [[12](#ref-12), [13](#ref-13), [108](#ref-108)]. Google Cloud's enterprise Vertex AI surface states a guaranteed discount on Gemini's explicit cache — 90% on Gemini 2.5+, 75% on Gemini 2.0 — but Gemini 2.0 no longer appears on the Developer API's own current pricing or caching documentation as of September 2026, so the 75% figure is best read as enterprise-specific or historical rather than confirmed on the consumer-facing API [[110](#ref-110)]. A worked example on Anthropic's multipliers makes the compute/price gap concrete: an 8,000-token stable prefix (system prompt plus tool schemas) reused over 10 turns costs $8{,}000\times\$2.50/10^6\approx\$0.020$ to write once, then $8{,}000\times\$0.20/10^6\approx\$0.0016$ per turn to read on each of the next 9 — about \$0.034 total, versus $10\times8{,}000\times\$2/10^6=\$0.16$ paying full price every turn: a 78.5% reduction on the shared portion, or a ~4.65$\times$ blended cost reduction [[12](#ref-12)]. The compute-side effect is larger than this blended price ratio suggests, since engines skip the forward pass on a hit rather than merely discounting it: prefill FLOPs on turn 10 scale with only the newest ~200–400 tokens rather than the accumulated ~9,000, a ~23–45$\times$ compute reduction [[9](#ref-9), [10](#ref-10)]. OpenAI states a matching asymmetry: caching can cut TTFT up to 80% on prompts above 10,000 tokens, more than the 90% price discount alone predicts, since removing computation also removes queuing on the prefill path [[111](#ref-111)].

At $r=0.1$, savings simplify to $0.9h$: an 81% cost reduction at a 90% hit rate. This is not a fixed physical constant, though — it is a per-model, per-vendor, and per-generation business decision. OpenAI's own historical pricing shows $r$ ranging from 0.5 (GPT-4o, October 2024) to 0.25 (GPT-4.1) to 0.1 (GPT-5-nano, GPT-5.2) as inference-serving efficiency improved [[242](#ref-242)]; at $r=0.5$, the same 90% hit rate yields only a 45% saving, half the headline figure above. The formula also omits the cache-write charge (1.25–2× the base price, depending on cache lifetime), which both OpenAI and Anthropic now bill on a first write; a prefix must be read back at least once (5-minute cache) or roughly twice (1-hour cache) before writing it pays for itself at all [[13](#ref-13), [12](#ref-12)]. Put differently, hit rate alone is an incomplete predictor of savings — a high hit rate on prefixes that are each read only once after being written can cost *more* than no caching, which is precisely the KV-cache-as-prefill-avoidance mechanism this whitepaper describes, made visible in a bill [[243](#ref-243)].

### GPU-hour cost of avoided prefill compute

Self-hosted deployments can price a cache hit directly in avoided GPU-hours rather than through a vendor's per-token rate. LMCache's published multi-turn benchmark (Qwen3-8B, single NVIDIA RTX 4090 24GB, vLLM 0.10.1.1, 20K-token shared-prefix conversations) measured mean TTFT drop from 5.07 s without cache reuse to 2.09 s with it — 2.98 s of prefill recomputation avoided per request [[236](#ref-236)]. Scaled to 1 million such requests, that is roughly 828 GPU-hours of avoided prefill-blocked time, priced at two current on-demand cloud rates for datacenter H100s:

| | Value |
|---|---:|
| TTFT saved per request | 2.98 s |
| Avoided GPU-time @ 1M requests | ≈828 GPU-hours |
| @ CoreWeave on-demand H100 ($6.16/GPU-hr, 8×80GB node) | ≈$5,100 |
| @ Lambda Labs on-demand H100 ($2.99/GPU-hr, 8×80GB node) | ≈$2,475 |

*[[236](#ref-236), [244](#ref-244), [245](#ref-245)]. On-demand GPU pricing is a snapshot and varies by region, spot vs. reserved terms, and provider.*

Two limits apply. First, the benchmark ran on an 8B model on a single consumer GPU; prefill cost per token, and therefore the absolute seconds saved, scales with model size and differs on datacenter accelerators, so this is an order-of-magnitude illustration, not a validated prediction for a 70B- or 405B-class deployment. That consumer GPU also has roughly a third of an H100 SXM's HBM bandwidth (1,008 GB/s vs. ~3 TB/s aggregate [[246](#ref-246), [44](#ref-44)]) and no comparable FP8 tensor-core throughput, so a second of freed RTX 4090 prefill time is not the same quantity of compute as a second of freed H100 time; pricing the former at the latter's hourly rate is a further approximation layered on top of the model-size mismatch, not a restatement of it. Second, "avoided GPU-hours" is an upper bound on freed serving *capacity*, not a guaranteed dollar saving — it is realized only if that capacity is actually resold or reused for other requests; on an under-utilized server it simply shows up as idle time. As with token pricing, the dollar figure moves by more than 2× just from which cloud market it is priced against, underscoring that any single-number cache ROI claim needs its hardware and pricing baseline stated alongside it.

### Energy per token: a threshold effect, not a smooth curve

Every dollar figure above has an energy analogue, and it does not always move in the same direction. A HotCarbon 2026 workshop study replaying production agentic traces (from Applied Compute) found that KV-cache offloading's *energy* benefit is gated by a sharp threshold in KV-cache utilization rather than a smooth function of it: below the 95th-percentile KV-utilization point of a run, enabling offload changes energy per token by less than measurement noise (idle-power variance of ±2–3 W), but above roughly 90% p95 utilization it saves 14.4–31.9% of marginal GPU energy per token [[14](#ref-14)]. The mechanism is a "recomputation tax": once the reserved KV pool fills, the scheduler must preempt and later fully recompute evicted requests at peak (up to 700 W) prefill power, and offloading avoids that recompute rather than merely deferring it — the paper measures the underlying asymmetry directly, at roughly 0.2 nJ per KV byte moved over PCIe to host DRAM versus roughly 100 nJ to recompute that same byte on GPU, a ≥400× gap, because each cached byte embeds on the order of 10^5 FLOPs of transformer work [[14](#ref-14)]. Host-side (CPU/DRAM) energy from the offload itself was statistically indistinguishable from zero across every condition tested, so the GPU-side saving is realized rather than merely relocated to the host [[14](#ref-14)]. The same study documents a distinct failure mode at large offload buffers under low request pressure — a "throughput-collapse" state in which GPU power falls to under 200 W and completed output drops roughly 4–5× against a normally-provisioned run — a reminder that offload sizing has an energy-correctness dimension as well as a capacity one [[14](#ref-14)].

A second, independent and purely analytical study derives a complementary relationship it calls the "1/W law": tokens-per-watt roughly halves every time a fleet's serving context window doubles, because a longer context shrinks the number of concurrent sequences a fixed KV-cache budget can hold while GPU power draw stays roughly flat [[247](#ref-247)]. On the paper's calibrated H100 model, a fleet serving 64K-token sessions holds only 16 concurrent sequences per GPU (1.5 tok/W) against 256 sequences at 4K tokens (17.6 tok/W) — a roughly 12× spread from context length alone — and the authors argue that *routing topology* (which context-length pool a request lands in) is therefore a larger energy lever than buying a newer GPU generation: a context-length-aware two-pool routing scheme is modeled to deliver about 2.5× better tokens-per-watt than a homogeneous fleet, versus roughly 1.7× from upgrading H100 to B200 at fixed topology, with the two gains multiplying to roughly 4.25× when combined [[247](#ref-247)]. The authors are explicit that B200/H200 figures are analytical projections calibrated only to published H100 power measurements, not new hardware experiments, so the absolute multipliers should be read as directional rather than as a validated production result; the relationship this whitepaper's own KV-cache-sizing formulas make structural — that context length and concurrency trade off against a fixed memory budget — is what the paper argues also governs energy, not only capacity and cost [[247](#ref-247)].

## Security, Privacy and Integrity of Shared KV Caches

Every optimization discussed so far in this whitepaper – prefix sharing, disaggregated prefill/decode, offload to CPU or NVMe, and persistent multi-tier stores such as Mooncake – makes the KV cache *shared* and *long-lived* rather than private and ephemeral to a single request. That shift has a security cost: a KV cache's presence, timing behavior, and in some cases raw contents are all correlated with the prompt that produced it. Once a cache is shared across tenants or persisted outside GPU memory, three risks follow – timing side channels that leak prompt content through cache-hit signals, tampering once caches live in shared or remote storage, and the fact that KV tensors are a derived, sometimes invertible, representation of the prompt that must itself be protected at rest and in transit.

### Timing side channels: cache hits leak prompt content

Prefix caching (discussed earlier in this whitepaper, in "Reusing the Cache Across Requests: Prefix Caching and Beyond") accelerates a request whose prompt shares a prefix with something already cached, and the speedup is visible externally as a lower Time to First Token. If the cache is shared across users, that timing difference becomes an oracle: an attacker who can measure TTFT can test whether a guessed prefix matches another tenant's cached prompt. Song et al. formalized this as a family of timing side channels in production LLM serving systems, targeting both the KV cache and semantic response caches (e.g. GPTCache) [[25](#ref-25)]. Using a threshold-based classifier on timing measurements plus an incremental, token-by-token search that exploits the prefix-match requirement, they recovered system and peer-user prompts with an average accuracy of 89.0% at a cost of about 112.72 queries per recovered token in black-box tests against real serving stacks; a related "peeping neighbor" attack against semantic caches reached 81.4% accuracy in a single trial, improving to 95.4% after 5 trials under GPTCache's default settings [[25](#ref-25)]. As a worked illustration: recovering a 50-token system prompt at that measured rate costs on the order of $50 \times 112.7 \approx 5{,}600$ queries – expensive against a rate-limited API, cheap against an unthrottled internal service. Independently, Zheng et al.'s InputSnatch pairs a learned input constructor with statistical time-fitting to attack both prefix caching (vLLM) and semantic caching (GPTCache), without privileged access beyond a standard client [[248](#ref-248)]. Wu et al. showed the channel need not be raw latency at all: their PROMPTPEEK attack exploits the *change in serving order* under longest-prefix-match scheduling, inferring cache hits from where a response lands relative to decoy requests, reconstructing prompts with 99%, 98%, and 95% success under decreasing levels of attacker background knowledge on Llama-2-13B/A100 [[249](#ref-249)]. These are not lab-only curiosities: an independent statistical audit of 17 production LLM APIs (Sept.–Oct. 2024) found prompt caching enabled in 8 and cross-user ("global") sharing in 7, including a since-patched OpenAI embedding endpoint; the same signal also leaked that OpenAI's `text-embedding-3-small` is a decoder-only Transformer, otherwise undocumented [[250](#ref-250)]. vLLM's own chunk-based PagedAttention prefix cache carried a CVE-assigned instance (CVE-2025-46570): with a 1-token shared prefix, hit/miss classification reached a ROC AUC of 0.571; at 8 tokens, AUC rose to 0.99 on LLaMA2-70B-GPTQ [[24](#ref-24)].

| Shared prefix length | ROC AUC (hit vs. miss) |
| --- | ---: |
| 1 token | 0.571 |
| 8 tokens | 0.99 |

**Table 8.** Distinguishability of vLLM prefix-cache hits from misses by shared-prefix length, as measured by the reporters of CVE-2025-46570 on a single LLaMA2-70B-GPTQ deployment [[24](#ref-24)]. Results are hardware- and model-specific and should not be read as a universal AUC curve.

### Provider and framework mitigations: isolation, salting and selective sharing

The general fix is to stop sharing cache state across mutually distrustful principals, or to make sharing decisions unobservable to clients. vLLM's response to CVE-2025-46570 was an optional per-request `cache_salt`, injected into the hash of a request's first KV block so only requests carrying the same salt can reuse each other's blocks, shipped in v0.9.0 [[24](#ref-24), [101](#ref-101)]. The RFC that proposed this mechanism labeled it the "single-barrier" design – one salt per request – and, in the same proposal, described a "multi-barrier" alternative that instead salts only a tenant-specific suffix while leaving a genuinely shared prefix unsalted [[251](#ref-251)]. The single-barrier design fully isolates tenants but is blunt: with 256 tenants sharing one system prompt, per-tenant salting forces 256x duplicate prefill of that prompt's blocks, since salted tenants can no longer reuse each other's blocks at all [[252](#ref-252)]. The multi-barrier extension was meant to recover that lost reuse; the original RFC closed its multi-barrier half as future work ("getting back to multi barrier design...only if needed"), a full hierarchical implementation was proposed roughly a year later and closed unreviewed after five months of inactivity, and as of September 2026 a narrower, core-only reimplementation of the multi-barrier hashing contract remains open and unmerged, with the request-level API and per-message salt mapping not yet built on top of it [[251](#ref-251), [253](#ref-253)]. vLLM's cache key also folds `cache_salt` in with block token content, LoRA adapter ID, and multimodal input hashes, making it a content-addressed structure; the project explicitly documents that swapping its default cryptographic hash (SHA-256) for a faster non-cryptographic one (xxHash) "increases the risk of hash collisions, which can cause undefined behavior or even leak private information in multi-tenant environments" [[101](#ref-101)] – a direct, vendor-documented trade-off between hashing speed and cross-tenant isolation. Hosted APIs mitigate the same risk structurally rather than via client-supplied salts: OpenAI scopes caches to the organization, never shared across organizations, and exposes a `prompt_cache_key` explicitly described as helping "prevent cache-hit probing across users" [[13](#ref-13)]; Anthropic isolates per *workspace* (finer than organization) on its own API, on Claude Platform on AWS, and on Microsoft Foundry (currently in beta), but falls back to organization-level isolation on Amazon Bedrock and on Google Cloud's Vertex AI – an inconsistency an operator spanning hosting surfaces should not assume away [[12](#ref-12)]. Because full per-tenant isolation gives up the benefits that motivated caching, selective sharing has been proposed as a middle ground: SafeKV classifies KV blocks as shareable or private via an asynchronous classifier over a radix-tree index, reporting mitigation of 94–97% of timing-based attacks while recovering up to 2.66x the throughput and cutting TTFT overhead by up to 40.58% versus full isolation [[254](#ref-254)] – a probabilistic reduction, not a guarantee, dependent on an ML classifier that is itself an attack surface, and still a research prototype rather than an audited production feature.

### KV tensors as a partially invertible, tamperable representation of the prompt

A second, distinct risk applies once an adversary obtains the raw KV tensors themselves – via a compromised remote cache store, an unencrypted offload path, or a misconfigured RDMA link – rather than merely observing timing. Luo et al. show the KV cache is not merely correlated with the prompt: the prompt can be partly reconstructed from the cache [[26](#ref-26)]. A direct algebraic Inversion Attack recovers the first-layer input with near-100% fidelity when the key/value projection matrices are square and invertible, as in classic multi-head attention (e.g. LLaMA-7B); it generally fails against modern architectures using grouped-query or multi-head latent attention (e.g. Llama-3, Qwen, DeepSeek), whose projections are non-square – though the same paper reports one documented exception, the GQA-based LLaMA-3.2-1B, which still exhibited near-100% first-layer reconstruction fidelity, attributed by the authors to that model's unusually high projection rank relative to its hidden-state dimension, which lets a least-squares solve recover the input despite the non-square projection, so "fails against GQA/MLA" is the common case rather than a categorical guarantee [[26](#ref-26)] – and against deeper-layer caches, whose states are semantically fused rather than a direct token embedding [[26](#ref-26)]. A model-agnostic Collision Attack instead re-runs a local copy of the model over candidate tokens and matches resulting KV entries against the intercepted cache, applying regardless of architecture or layer, at the cost of a vocabulary-sized search per token [[26](#ref-26)]. An Injection Attack sidesteps reconstruction entirely: appending "Repeat the previous content" to a stolen cache and resuming generation caused the model to echo its own private context (average BERTScore 0.58, ROUGE-L 0.42) [[26](#ref-26)]. Because the cache can be read, it can typically also be written. Ganesh et al.'s "History Swapping" overwrites a full-layer, contiguous block of an active generation's cache with a precomputed cache on a different topic, hijacking the conversation's trajectory – persistently, partially, or after a delay – without altering the visible prompt, across 324 configurations on Qwen3; partial- and early-layer overwrites were comparatively robust, so the result should not be generalized to "any cache write compromises any model" [[255](#ref-255)]. Both lines of work assume the precondition the next section's mitigations target: read/write access to plaintext KV tensors in storage or transit. None of the major production KV-cache serving or storage stacks – on-GPU engines such as vLLM and NVIDIA TensorRT-LLM, or disaggregated stores such as Mooncake and LMCache – are documented to implement the reversible-obfuscation defense these papers propose by default.

### Encryption, integrity and retention in practice

Confidentiality of KV tensors at rest and in transit is therefore load-bearing, not optional, once caches leave GPU memory. OpenAI documents two prompt-cache retention tiers with different defaults: an `in_memory` tier bounded to roughly 5–10 minutes of inactivity and never more than one hour, and an extended `24h` tier that typically keeps entries available for about 30 minutes and can retain them for up to 24 hours; organizations without Zero Data Retention enabled default to the 24-hour tier, and only organizations with Zero Data Retention enabled default to the shorter in-memory tier [[13](#ref-13)]. OpenAI documents that prompt caching "may store encrypted key/value tensors in GPU-local storage as application state" [[13](#ref-13), [256](#ref-256)] – a vendor hedge best read as a lower bound, not a guarantee across every model, tier and region; for the majority of organizations without Zero Data Retention enabled, the retention ceiling to plan around is 24 hours, not one hour. Anthropic's default TTL is 5 minutes, refreshed on each hit, with an opt-in 1-hour TTL priced at 2x the base input-token rate versus 1.25x for the 5-minute default – about 1.6x the 5-minute cache-write price, not literally double it; Bedrock documents the same TTL options with per-model minimum token counts per checkpoint [[12](#ref-12)]. The self-hosted reference stacks this whitepaper surveys for offload and persistence expose far less structured protection. Mooncake Store's public deployment and client-configuration references document only transport, host and buffer-size parameters for cross-machine KV transfer (RDMA/TCP protocol, RDMA device selection, segment and buffer sizes), with no authentication or encryption field anywhere in that surface for the Master/Client data path itself [[257](#ref-257), [258](#ref-258)]; the one credential-bearing option documented in the public surface, `MC_REDIS_PASSWORD`, authenticates only to the optional Redis metadata backend, not the RDMA/TCP data plane that actually carries KV bytes [[259](#ref-259)]. Separately, Mooncake Store's own design documentation describes a (now legacy, superseded by a newer SSD-offload subsystem) tiered mode that asynchronously backs up in-memory KV objects to a distributed file system keyed by the caller-supplied object key – in practice a content-derived hash when Mooncake is driven by a prefix-caching client such as LMCache – with no mention of at-rest encryption anywhere in that design [[260](#ref-260)]. That is an absence in the public reference, not proof no such option exists anywhere in the codebase, but it means an operator layering Mooncake, LMCache, or a similar disaggregated store into a multi-tenant deployment must add transport security (mutual TLS at a gateway, a private VPC, IPsec on the RDMA fabric) and at-rest encryption themselves; none of it is on by default. Retention deserves the same scrutiny as encryption: a KV cache with a bounded but nonzero TTL, built from – and, per the inversion results above, sometimes recoverable to – the original prompt, is a store of derived personal or proprietary data. It is easy to omit from data-retention and right-to-erasure processes that target request logs and application databases but never look inside the inference engine's own memory hierarchy; an erasure request honored in the application layer while a matching KV block still lives in a shared cache for the next several minutes (or, on an offloaded/persistent path, considerably longer, and up to 24 hours on OpenAI's non-ZDR default tier) is not actually honored.

### Checklist for operators

- Treat unsalted, globally shared prefix/KV caching across mutually distrustful tenants as unsafe by default; enable per-tenant isolation (`cache_salt`, an org/workspace-scoped hosted API) before enabling cross-request reuse.
- If isolating every tenant forces unacceptable recompute of a genuinely shared prefix, scope isolation to the tenant-specific suffix rather than disabling caching outright, and verify isolation with a cache-hit-rate or timing conformance test – hash-equality unit tests alone don't prove no cross-tenant reuse at serving time.
- Use a cryptographic hash (SHA-256 or equivalent) for cache keys in multi-tenant deployments; treat a switch to a faster non-cryptographic hash as a security decision, not just a performance one.
- Encrypt KV tensors at rest and in transit at every hop they leave GPU memory – PCIe offload, NVMe spill, RDMA transfers, remote KV stores – since the reference implementations surveyed here do not enable this by default.
- Rate-limit and monitor for cache-hit probing patterns (bursts of near-duplicate requests, systematic TTFT fingerprinting), as with credential-stuffing or enumeration attacks.
- Where full isolation is too costly, consider selective/classified sharing as a middle ground, but budget it as a probabilistic mitigation (reported in the 90s of percent, not a guarantee) whose classifier is itself part of the attack surface.
- Document retention windows and cache scope (user/workspace/organization) for every caching layer, and confirm data-deletion and right-to-erasure processes actually reach the KV cache, not only logs and databases.

## The Cache as an Editable State: Attention Interventions (NaLa)

Every optimization considered so far treats the KV cache as a performance structure to be paged, quantized, offloaded or replicated, while leaving its *contents* untouched: the cache is a faithful record of what the model actually computed during prefill, and inference reads it unmodified. A separate line of interpretability and steering work treats the cache differently: as the literal, per-token record of what each layer currently has available to attend to, and therefore as a surface that can be read and edited at inference time to test or correct a model's behavior without touching its weights or its prompt. This section examines that idea through a concrete public case study, NaLa, an MIT-licensed research tool from Hassana Labs, together with the accompanying mathematics of exact attention responses [[261](#ref-261), [27](#ref-27), [262](#ref-262)]. As of September 2026 this is recent, actively developed research code rather than a serving-system feature, and the evidence behind it is narrower than the mechanism itself; both the capability and its limits are described below.

### What a cache intervention is

A *cache intervention*, in this sense, edits the keys and/or values that one query's attention computation reads at one layer, for that read only, rather than modifying the stored K/V tensors that persist in the cache. NaLa's published interface exposes two such edits at the granularity of one layer and one or more token positions: *deletion*, which excludes selected cache entries from the attention read while keeping the causal complement's softmax normalizer intact, and *reweighting*, which multiplies a span's unnormalized attention logits by a factor before renormalizing over the full causal bank — a factor below one suppresses a span, above one boosts it [[261](#ref-261)]. Execution keeps every branch on an independent copy of the shared prefix cache, so a candidate edit cannot leak into another candidate or into the underlying stored state [[261](#ref-261)]. The accompanying paper's calculus additionally covers finite positional (rotary) displacements and joint key–value content edits, though the shipped command-line tool's documented interface centers on deletion and reweighting [[27](#ref-27)].

This is a narrower claim than either prompt engineering or weight editing. Prompt engineering changes what gets computed before it is cached; a cache intervention changes what is read from an already-computed cache, leaving the underlying prompt and parameters untouched, which is why the same edit can be tested, compared against a control, and discarded without retraining or re-prompting [[261](#ref-261)]. It is also narrower than residual-stream activation steering, which adds a fixed direction to one hand-selected layer's activations at every generated token to shift a global output property such as sentiment or topic [[263](#ref-263)]; a cache intervention instead edits specific cached entries at one layer for one query, targeting *which tokens are attended to* rather than adding a global bias to the representation.

### Exact response versus first-order attribution

Scoring a candidate edit requires predicting how the model's eventual output would change — ordinarily this means executing the edit and re-running the forward pass, which is prohibitive when there are tens of thousands of candidates. The standard cheap alternative, attribution patching, approximates a component's causal effect with a linear approximation: two forward passes (clean and corrupted) and a backward pass, rather than a full re-execution per candidate [[264](#ref-264)]. AtP\* refines this approximation to correct specific failure modes of the naive linear version while keeping its cost profile [[265](#ref-265)].

NaLa's underlying identity instead gives the *exact* change in a single query's attention output for a finite edit, using the exponential divided difference $\varphi_1(B) = \int_0^1 e^{tB}\,dt$, which for a scalar argument reduces to $\rho(s) = (e^s-1)/s$ (with $\rho(0)=1$) — the same divided difference that connects a finite rotary displacement, $z(p+\delta) - z(p) = \delta A \varphi_1(\delta A) z(p)$, to the corresponding softmax score change [[27](#ref-27), [262](#ref-262)]. Softmax is not linearized and RoPE is not removed; the identity is exact for the edited attention write itself. Predicting the *downstream* effect on an answer or a loss still requires contracting that exact local response with a frozen baseline gradient obtained from one backward pass, and this is where approximation re-enters: the local response is exact, but its propagation through the remaining network layers is not [[27](#ref-27)].

On the authors' own held-out evaluation, retaining this exact local response rather than a first-order approximation makes a measurable quantitative difference:

| Study | Executed edits | Metric | Reported result |
| --- | ---: | --- | ---: |
| Finite positional (rotary) edits | 92,160 (768 held-out prompt sets) | sign accuracy | 95.36–96.52% |
| Finite positional (rotary) edits | 92,160 (768 held-out prompt sets) | MAE vs. a linearized (Jacobian) baseline | 73.6–82.5% lower |
| Joint key–value edits | 5,120 (2 Qwen sizes × 2 tasks × multiple layers) | MAE vs. separately attributed key and value effects | more than 9× lower, every setting |

These figures are the authors' own reported evaluation on their own held-out sets [[27](#ref-27)]. The independent replication described below reproduced the recorded repair case, not these held-out studies.

### Cost: one backward pass, many candidates

The practical payoff of an exact, closed-form response is that scoring does not require re-running the model per candidate. NaLa's capture step performs a no-grad forward pass over the prefix, one gradient-enabled forward pass for the query being edited, and a single backward pass that yields gradients for every layer under consideration; after that, scoring any number of candidate edits is closed-form arithmetic over the captured per-token statistics, making zero further calls to the model [[261](#ref-261)]. Naively scoring $C$ candidates by execution costs $C$ forward passes; this approach instead costs a fixed, small number of forward/backward passes for the capture plus $O(C)$ arithmetic, with actual model execution reserved for the handful of top-ranked candidates chosen for verification. No public wall-clock or memory benchmark of the capture step itself against plain, no-grad inference was found; the efficiency argument here is structural — a fixed capture cost independent of $C$ versus a per-candidate cost that scales with $C$ — rather than an empirically measured latency or memory figure.

The published illustration of this is a small coding-repair case: a model calling a removed API function, checked against six fixed unit tests. Scoring covered 109,080 candidate edits — every token position, at every layer, under fifteen edit templates — from that single capture, and only the top-ranked candidate was actually executed:

| Branch | Result on the fixed test suite |
| --- | ---: |
| Ordinary generation | 0/6 |
| Top-ranked attention edit (of 109,080 scored candidates) | 6/6 |

The authors are explicit that this is one adaptively selected synthetic task, not a benchmark [[261](#ref-261)].

### Scope and what has not been shown

The published scope is narrow along several axes at once. Native support is limited to FP32 weights under eager (unfused) full causal attention with fixed, unscaled RoPE, on the Qwen2 and Qwen2.5 architectures; recorded results are CPU-only, and a 7B-scale run on a GPU is documented as prepared but not validated [[261](#ref-261)]. Each candidate edits one layer; deletion masks an entry rather than shortening the prompt or shifting token positions, so it changes what is attended to, not what exists in the sequence [[261](#ref-261)]. And the central quantitative claims above are the authors' own measurements against their own held-out sets, not an independent audit — a materially weaker evidentiary standing than the peer-reviewed baselines this whitepaper otherwise draws on.

### Independent replication: what survives

An independent replication fork of NaLa, whose documentation was rewritten in place wherever a claim did not survive, supplies the evidence that the public repositories lack [[28](#ref-28)]. Its findings sharpen both the capability and its limits:

- **The mechanism and the recorded case reproduce.** A full 24-layer re-capture of the repair case on different hardware and a different PyTorch build matched the recorded baseline margin (−6.683786 vs. −6.683828), the top-ranked predicted gain (+11.360970 vs. +11.360963), the executed gain (+9.398861 vs. +9.3989) and the winning site (layer 21, row 79, odds ×64). The attention identity's residual stays at about $10^{-14}$ at every size from 0.5B to 7B, once a tautological check in an earlier version of the harness was corrected.
- **The headline count overstates the search.** The 109,080 candidates are 7,272 distinct token/layer sites under fifteen edit templates, seven of which belong to one monotone reweighting family; the tool's default template set gives 72,720. The distinct-site count is the more honest measure of the search breadth.
- **Exactness mattered for magnitude, not for the winner.** On the recorded case, a first-order (attribution-patching-style) score picks the same top candidate as the exact response. Over the exact top 1,000, however, the first-order score is a median 0.216× the exact value (a roughly five-fold underestimate), and in 18 of the 24 individual layers the two methods disagree on which candidate ranks first.
- **The case was selected from 107 attempts.** Of 107 case-construction attempts, 85 were already solved by a control before any attention edit was scored, 11 had invalid controls, and 10 reached an attention edit, of which one passed. The published case is that one.
- **The repair does not survive scale.** Run unchanged across ten checkpoints in three families, the repair reproduced only at Qwen2.5-0.5B and Qwen3-0.6B. It failed at 1.5B, 3B and 7B (Qwen2.5), 1.7B, 4B and 8B (Qwen3), and at SmolLM2-360M and 1.7B. The achievable gain shrinks with scale (from +9.4 to about +3.5 nats on Qwen2.5) while the deficit it has to close does not. A pre-registered prediction that at least one checkpoint below 1B would repair was falsified: SmolLM2-360M, smaller than both repairing models, did not.
- **A favourable prediction is necessary, not sufficient.** In a pre-registered multi-site experiment with 48 executed edits, no edit repaired the program without its margin crossing zero, but eleven edits crossed zero (after-margins +0.913 to +4.891) and still failed. Nothing measured so far predicts which crossings produce a passing program.
- **A diagnostic claim was withdrawn.** A second, two-document "stale context" case, earlier offered as evidence that the method distinguishes *attending to the wrong tokens* from *attending correctly and deciding wrongly*, could not be reproduced from any fixture, script or recorded measurement and was removed; that diagnostic claim is unevidenced.

Taken together, the replication supports the mathematics and the tooling, and supports the repair as a real, reproducible event on two specific small checkpoints; it does not support treating attention editing as a general repair capability. The fork also extends the public tool (Qwen3 and fixed-RoPE Llama support, donor-copy key/value edits, a source-code repair controller, and an exact KV-row precision-restoration routine evaluated only on simulated quantization); these extensions are not part of the public release [[28](#ref-28)].

### Systems implications

Treating the cache as editable rather than merely relocatable puts this idea in tension with exactly the optimizations covered earlier in this paper. Production serving systems already share KV cache blocks across requests with a common prefix, mapping several requests to the same physical block until one diverges, at which point that block is copied rather than shared further — the block-sharing and copy-on-write mechanism vLLM introduced for a single request's parallel-sampled or beam-searched output sequences [[8](#ref-8)] is generalized across separate requests by automatic prefix caching [[266](#ref-266)]. A per-read cache intervention is a deliberate, request-scoped divergence of precisely this kind: to keep other requests reading the unedited shared block, the affected entries must be copied into a private branch before the edited read, which is consistent with why NaLa's own execution model gives every branch an independent copy of the prefix cache rather than editing in place [[261](#ref-261)]. In a paged, prefix-sharing serving engine, an intervention therefore forces the same copy-on-write fork that prefix caching exists to avoid, so the two features pull in opposite directions unless an intervention is scoped tightly enough that only the diverging request pays the copy cost.

A second implication concerns provenance. Because an edit changes what is read without changing the stored tensors, a persisted cache — of the kind considered earlier for fault tolerance and checkpointing [[203](#ref-203)] — would be bit-identical in shape whether or not an intervention occurred; only a separate record of which layer, which entries, and which edit were applied could preserve the fact that a resumed generation is not what an unedited forward pass would have produced. No such persistence or provenance mechanism is part of the published tool, which operates on ephemeral, in-process cache copies rather than durable state [[261](#ref-261)].

Third, the intervention as published depends on the cache being present as directly addressable, full-precision per-token tensors: the documented scope is FP32 under eager attention [[261](#ref-261)]. That is a different execution path from the block-table indirection of paged attention [[8](#ref-8)] or from a quantized cache holding 2-bit [[5](#ref-5), [83](#ref-83)] or FP8 [[51](#ref-51)] entries rather than full-precision keys and values. Porting a per-read intervention of this kind into a paged, fused, or quantized production layout would require, at minimum, an unfused reference attention path over the specific entries being edited and dequantization of the affected cache blocks before the edit — a materially different, and slower, code path than the ones production serving engines are built around. Nothing in the public record indicates this integration exists yet; the mechanism has been demonstrated as a diagnostic and repair tool on small, single-request, CPU workloads, not as a production serving capability.

## Conclusion

The KV cache began as an implementation detail of autoregressive decoding and has become the organizing constraint of LLM serving. The sections above follow it through a memory hierarchy that now reaches from on-chip SRAM to shared object storage. At every level the same trade recurs: capacity against bandwidth and latency, and reuse against isolation. Four observations generalize across that hierarchy.

First, **the cheapest byte is the one never stored**. Grouped-query and latent attention, sliding windows, sequence compression and FP8 storage reduce the cache at its source. Each carries quality, kernel and compatibility costs that production engines are still absorbing; heterogeneous caches, for example, strain paged block managers designed for uniform blocks.

Second, **the most valuable byte is the one reused**. For interactive and agentic traffic, prefix hit rate matters more than any single tier's speed. Operators should measure it first, on replayed production traffic rather than synthetic prompts, before sizing hardware.

Third, **a lower tier helps only when it is faster than recomputation**. That holds for host memory and RDMA-attached flash across realistic settings, and only marginally for plain HTTP object storage. The storage question has therefore shifted from "how much capacity?" to "how fast is the first byte, and how many small objects per second can be looked up?"

Fourth, **a shared cache is shared data**. Once caches outlive a request or cross a tenant boundary, they need the controls applied to any other derived user data: isolation, encryption, integrity, provenance and deletion.

Several questions remain open as of September 2026:

- **Portability:** no standard KV cache format lets one engine, model revision or precision read another's cache.
- **Eviction versus sharing:** eviction and sparse retention remain hard to combine with prefix sharing and offload.
- **Independent benchmarks:** almost every storage-tier figure is vendor-reported and none has been independently reproduced, so shared benchmarks such as MLPerf Storage's KV test matter.
- **Security of shared caches:** multi-tenant isolation of shared KV stores has not been independently audited.

Progress on these, more than any further kernel speed-up, will decide how far million-token, many-session inference can scale.

## Appendix A: KV Cache Sizing Calculator

`tools/kv_calc.py` is a dependency-free Python 3 script that computes transformer KV cache size -- bytes per token and totals for a given context length, batch size, and storage dtype -- for a built-in table of eleven open-weight models spanning three cache-shaping mechanisms (including Mistral 7B v0.1, kept as the canonical example of a uniform sliding window, alongside more recent releases): plain grouped-query attention (bytes/token/layer = $2 \cdot n_{kv} \cdot d_{head} \cdot s$), DeepSeek-style Multi-head Latent Attention (bytes/token/layer = $(d_c + d_h^R) \cdot s$, independent of head count, per the formula in DeepSeek-V2's Table 1 [[2](#ref-2)]), and hybrid models that interleave full-attention layers with sliding-window layers whose cache stops growing once the sequence exceeds the window (Mistral 7B's uniform window, and Gemma 2/3's and gpt-oss's local/global interleaving). Run `python3 tools/kv_calc.py --list` to see all eleven models, their architecture parameters, and each model's own documented or configured maximum context length (each sourced from a public Hugging Face `config.json` -- Llama 3 8B and 3.1 70B [[267](#ref-267), [268](#ref-268)], Mistral 7B v0.1 [[269](#ref-269), [62](#ref-62)], Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen3-8B [[270](#ref-270), [271](#ref-271)], DeepSeek-V2/V3 and Kimi K2 Instruct [[272](#ref-272), [273](#ref-273), [274](#ref-274)], and Gemma 2 9B and Gemma 3 27B [[275](#ref-275), [276](#ref-276)], with gpt-oss-20b's explicit alternating layer pattern [[277](#ref-277)]); `python3 tools/kv_calc.py --model llama3-8b --tokens 128k --batch 4 --dtype fp8` to size one model at a specific context/batch/dtype; or omit `--model` and add `--markdown` to print a comparison table across the whole built-in set, as below. `--tokens` accepts binary-prefix shorthand (`4096`, `128k`, `1m`).

Four of the eleven built-in models do not, in fact, support the 131,072-token (128K) context used for the two comparison tables below, per their own config.json or paper: Llama 3 8B and Gemma 2 9B (`max_position_embeddings=8192`, no RoPE extension configured), Qwen3-8B (`max_position_embeddings=40960`, `rope_scaling=null`), and Mistral 7B v0.1 (its own paper's Table 1 states `context_len=8192` [[62](#ref-62)]; config.json's `max_position_embeddings=32768` is a separate, looser positional-embedding ceiling, not a documented trained or evaluated length). The tool now records this as a `max_context` field per model, flags any `kv_cache_bytes()` result where the requested token count exceeds it, and marks the corresponding rows below with a dagger (†) -- these four rows are the sizing formula applied 3x-16x past what the model is documented to support, not a realizable deployment configuration on their own. The bytes/token arithmetic itself is unaffected; this is a caveat about deployability at 128K, not a correctness issue in the formula.

### 128K tokens, batch 1, BF16

KV cache sizing at 131,072 tokens, batch 1

| Model | Org | Architecture | Layers | dtype | Bytes/token (avg) | Total per sequence | Total (batch) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Llama 3 8B † | Meta | GQA | 32 | bf16 | 128.00 KiB | 16.00 GiB | 16.00 GiB |
| Llama 3.1 70B | Meta | GQA | 80 | bf16 | 320.00 KiB | 40.00 GiB | 40.00 GiB |
| Qwen2.5 7B | Alibaba | GQA | 28 | bf16 | 56.00 KiB | 7.00 GiB | 7.00 GiB |
| Qwen3 8B † | Alibaba | GQA | 36 | bf16 | 144.00 KiB | 18.00 GiB | 18.00 GiB |
| DeepSeek-V2 | DeepSeek-AI | MLA | 60 | bf16 | 67.50 KiB | 8.44 GiB | 8.44 GiB |
| DeepSeek-V3 | DeepSeek-AI | MLA | 61 | bf16 | 68.62 KiB | 8.58 GiB | 8.58 GiB |
| Kimi K2 Instruct | Moonshot AI | MLA | 61 | bf16 | 68.62 KiB | 8.58 GiB | 8.58 GiB |
| Mistral 7B v0.1 † | Mistral AI | HYBRID | 32 | bf16 | 4.00 KiB | 512.00 MiB | 512.00 MiB |
| Gemma 2 9B † | Google | HYBRID | 42 | bf16 | 173.25 KiB | 21.66 GiB | 21.66 GiB |
| Gemma 3 27B | Google | HYBRID | 62 | bf16 | 83.25 KiB | 10.41 GiB | 10.41 GiB |
| gpt-oss-20b | OpenAI | HYBRID | 24 | bf16 | 24.02 KiB | 3.00 GiB | 3.00 GiB |
